**Article 2 overall score:** Provisional (7 of 8 variables): (8 + 8 + 9 + 6 + 5 + 5 + 7) / 7 = 48 / 7 = **6.9 / 10**

**Article 2 verdict:** A **substantially stronger Classens signal than Article 1**, and a **clearer template for a possible Classens-supervised thesis**. The work is community-engaged, environmental-justice-focused, mixed quantitative-qualitative GIS — exactly the kind of work the School of the Environment supports. **For ML-portfolio purposes**, the open-source reimplementation framing (Framing 1) is the cleanest recruiter-legible project and demonstrates real geospatial-Python skill. **The structural caveat from Article 1 holds**: the paper is not an ML paper, the paper is *explicitly skeptical* of pure-quantitative approaches to social vulnerability, and Classens's funding stream is SSHRC (humanities + social sciences) rather than NSERC. **A successful Classens-supervised ML thesis would need to position ML as complementary to participatory + qualitative methods, not replacing them** — the user should expect to read Cope & Elwood's *Qualitative GIS*, Liboiron's *Pollution Is Colonialism*, and Vera et al.'s *environmental data justice* before sitting down with him.

### Article 3: Climate Data Practices — A Research Approach for HCI and Climate Justice (ACM TOCHI critical-HCI paper)

- **Full citation:** Soden, R., Agrawaal, T. S., Lord, A., Chanen, C., Flawn, L., Seaifan, Z., **Classens, M.**, & Easterbrook, S. (2025). Climate Data Practices: A Research Approach for HCI and Climate Justice. *ACM Transactions on Computer-Human Interaction*, 32(4), Article 34, 27 pages. DOI: 10.1145/3719346. Published August 2025.
- **Authorship signal — third disciplinary register added.** **8-author paper, Classens is the 7th of 8 authors** (penultimate, between U of T students and senior CS faculty). Corresponding author Robert Soden (U of T Computer Science, soden@cs.toronto.edu); senior author Steve Easterbrook (U of T Computer Science) — **a critical-HCI / climate-informatics CS group**, NOT a quantitative-ML group. All 8 authors are at U of T. Middle authors include Taneea S. Agrawaal (CS), Austin Lord (postdoc), and several mail.utoronto.ca students. **Classens is brought in as the environmental-studies subject-matter expert**, bridging Soden's CS-HCI team to environmental-justice scholarship. **This adds a third disciplinary register to Classens's collaborative network**: Article 1 = Nutritional Sciences (Temerty Faculty of Medicine), Article 2 = SSHRC Geography (Trent), Article 3 = U of T Computer Science (critical HCI / climate informatics). **The pattern across all three articles**: Classens collaborates as the environmental-studies / critical-data-studies bridge for teams whose primary disciplinary home is elsewhere.
- **Problem in plain English:** Despite massive growth in climate data over the past decades (remote sensing, IoT sensors, ML, big data), governments and civil society have not made commensurate progress on climate action. The authors argue this is because **"information determinism"** — the false belief that more data alone produces social change — misattributes agency to data while ignoring the **social practices** through which data is created, used, shared, and made meaningful. They introduce **"climate data practices"** as a conceptual lens for HCI research at the intersection of critical data studies, practice theory, and climate / environmental justice. Drawing on a year-long collaborative ethnographic study of activists, City of Toronto staff, and non-profits, they present **six vignettes** of climate data practices and connect them to HCI theory.
- **ML task type:** **Not an ML paper — explicitly anti-ML in its theoretical framing.** Methodologically a **qualitative + interpretive critical-HCI study**: ethnographic fieldwork, semi-structured interview studies, and document analysis. The conceptual contribution is a framework (practices + scenes + infrastructures) for *critically interrogating* data and ML systems, not for building them. The paper explicitly cites: **information determinism** (Srinivasan, Finn & Ames 2017), **Weapons of Math Destruction** (O'Neil 2016 — also cited in Article 2), **Pollution Is Colonialism** (Liboiron 2021 — also cited in Article 2), **environmental data justice** (Vera et al. 2019 — also cited in Article 2), **algorithms-of-oppression** (Noble 2018), **forgetting practices in data sciences** (Muller & Strohmayer 2022), and **"beyond technical fixes"** (Nightingale et al. 2020). **An ML reframe is possible but more theoretically fraught than for Articles 1 or 2** — any ML reframing of this paper is *against* its central argument. Possible framings: (i) **build the equity-overlay dashboard the paper critiques** — pull City of Toronto Open Data climate indicators, add CalEnviroScreen-style equity layers (per-capita rather than per-acre emissions, demographic overlays, vulnerability composites), publish as a Streamlit / Folium app framed as a response to Soden et al.'s critique; (ii) **NLP analysis of TCFD corporate climate-financial-disclosure filings** — extract climate-financial framing patterns from public corporate filings, audit how companies report climate risk; (iii) **LLM audit of climate framings** — evaluate how GPT-4 / Claude / open-source LLMs frame climate change (do they reify carbon-emissions-only views, miss recognition justice, fail on Indigenous knowledge?) — connects to AI-ethics / FAccT / responsible-AI recruiter audience; (iv) **Mapillary / Street View CNN classification** of pop-up vs car-dominated street infrastructure (transferable from Article 2's geospatial framing); (v) **topic modelling on Climate Action Tracker / CDP corporate-disclosure text corpora**. **All five are my constructions; the paper itself frames no ML task and is explicitly anti-information-determinism.**
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: **Qualitative ethnographic + interview data** — the paper's primary data is private interview transcripts (City of Toronto staff in environment + finance divisions, climate-justice activists, non-profit organization staff including Muslim-women-bicycling community center, urban farmers, transit planners) and document analysis (City of Toronto climate dashboards, financial reports, TCFD filings, data-for-equity strategy documents).
  - Shape if stated: **Not specified.** The paper says the team met biweekly or monthly from September 2022 to August 2023, with multiple individual studies feeding the synthesis; total participant count not stated.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: Standard qualitative-research workflow (collaborative thematic discussion, vignette construction). No specific software named; presumably NVivo / Atlas.ti for coding (not mentioned).
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: **Not a model — six descriptive vignettes + a conceptual framework.** Output is theoretical: the *climate data practices* approach + the *practices/scenes/infrastructures* trio.
  - Units or label space: N/A.
- **Model architecture (brief):** **None — qualitative + interpretive critical-HCI methodology**, not a machine-learning model.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Private interview + ethnographic + document-analysis dataset on climate data practices in the City of Toronto. **Public document references** include the City of Toronto's net-zero-2040 emissions dashboard, the City's Data for Equity Strategy, City Financial Reports (2018–2019 with TCFD disclosures), and the Toronto seed library / Seedy Saturday/Sunday events.
  - Size: **Not specified.** Year-long study with ~7 individual researcher work-streams.
  - Source: City of Toronto staff + Toronto-area activists + non-profits + urban farmers (year-long Toronto fieldwork, 2022-2023).
  - Public? **No — the primary qualitative dataset is private** (standard for ethnographic IRB-approved research). **Topic-adjacent public datasets** that the paper *references* are public: City of Toronto Open Data, TCFD corporate filings, CDP, Climate Action Tracker.
  - Link: **Not provided in paper** for the primary dataset. Public references: https://open.toronto.ca/, https://www.fsb-tcfd.org/, City climate dashboard linked in Figure 1-2.
- **Reported headline metric:** **None — qualitative descriptive + theoretical contribution.** No formal evaluation, no benchmark, no held-out test set, no error metrics. "Validation" is internal coherence of the conceptual framework + connection to prior HCI / STS / practice-theory literature.
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **City of Toronto Open Data** — the City's official climate + emissions dashboard data + 100+ open datasets (transit, social services, demographics). Match: **direct** for the dashboarding vignette + the equity-overlay framing. Access: **easy — direct download** (CKAN portal). https://open.toronto.ca/
  - **Climate Action Tracker (CAT)** — country-level climate-policy assessments. Match: **loose** (national rather than urban scope). Access: **easy** — public data + scraping. https://climateactiontracker.org/
  - **CDP (Carbon Disclosure Project)** — corporate + city climate-disclosure database. Match: **close** for the financialization + TCFD framings. Access: **medium** — free account + portal access; bulk downloads sometimes restricted. https://www.cdp.net/
  - **TCFD-aligned corporate filings** — scattered across SEC / SEDAR / company sustainability-report repositories. Match: **direct** for the financialization vignette. Access: **medium-hard** — issuer-by-issuer scraping; harmonization non-trivial.
  - **Mapillary** + **Google Street View** — street-level imagery for the pop-ups vignette (school streets, traffic-calming interventions). Match: **close**. Access: **easy** (Mapillary free, Street View Static API has rate-limited free tier). https://www.mapillary.com/
  - **Open Food Facts** + **Seedy Saturday/Sunday catalog data** — for the seed-sharing vignette. Match: **loose** (broader food-packaging + seed-library scope). Access: **easy** (Open Food Facts) / **medium** (Seedy events typically lack a centralized catalog). https://world.openfoodfacts.org/data
  - **City of Toronto's published equity-overlay maps + 2023 Data for Equity Strategy** — gives the City's own equity-strategy framing as a public anchor. Match: **direct** (the paper critiques this strategy explicitly). Access: **easy**. https://www.toronto.ca/city-government/accessibility-human-rights/equity-diversity-inclusion/data-for-equity/
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Very hard / N/A** — the primary qualitative dataset is private interview + ethnographic data and is not redistributable. Topic-adjacent public data is **easy-to-medium**.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **Mostly within current toolset**, with one or two additions per chosen framing. Standard ML stack (pandas / sklearn / lightgbm) for tabular framings; **Hugging Face transformers (BERT / RoBERTa / Climate-BERT)** for the TCFD / corporate-disclosure NLP framings; **Folium / Streamlit / Plotly Dash** for the equity-overlay-dashboard framing; **OpenAI / Anthropic API + LangChain / instructor** for the LLM-audit framing; **Mapillary API + torchvision** for the street-imagery framing. **No new heavy ecosystem** (no cobrapy / RDKit / SBML / DFT). **Conceptual reading is heavier than tooling** — to do this work credibly requires familiarity with critical data studies (Loukissas, Mah, D'Ignazio & Klein, Liboiron) + practice theory (Bourdieu, Schatzki, Reckwitz, Shove) + critical HCI (Soden's own prior work, Bardzell, Light & Shklovski).
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Five viable framings, all my construction (the paper itself is methodologically anti-ML and explicitly critiques information determinism):
  1. **Equity-overlay climate dashboard for Toronto (most direct response to the paper).** *"Building the per-capita, equity-overlaid climate dashboard that Soden et al. (2025) argue Toronto needs."* Pull City of Toronto Open Data climate indicators (GHG emissions, transit + housing + health vulnerability), reframe per-capita rather than per-acre, overlay CalEnviroScreen-style demographic vulnerability layers from the Article-2 framing, and deploy as a Streamlit / Folium app. **README claim:** *"A community-equity-overlay variant of the City of Toronto climate dashboard, built in response to Soden et al.'s (2025) critique of carbon-emissions-only framings."* This is **the strongest pure-Article-3 framing** because it directly engages the paper's own dashboarding critique while producing a tangible, recruiter-legible artifact.
  2. **LLM audit of climate-change framings.** *"How do GPT-4, Claude Opus, and open-source LLMs frame climate change? An audit informed by Soden et al.'s climate data practices framework."* Evaluate how off-the-shelf LLMs respond to climate-related prompts (carbon-emissions-only vs equity-aware framings, treatment of Indigenous knowledge, recognition justice, distributional vs procedural framings). Notebook + structured prompt-evaluation harness + qualitative + quantitative analysis. **README claim:** *"An empirical audit of how large language models frame climate change, drawing on Soden et al. (2025)'s climate-data-practices framework."* **Strongest portfolio-impact framing for AI-ethics / FAccT / responsible-AI recruiter audiences** (Anthropic policy team, Hugging Face, Vector Institute, MILA AI ethics, Google Research responsible-AI, OpenAI policy).
  3. **TCFD / CDP corporate-disclosure NLP analysis.** *"Topic modelling and framing analysis of climate-financial disclosures across Canadian municipalities + Fortune 500 corporations."* Scrape public TCFD-aligned filings, fine-tune a Climate-BERT / SciBERT classifier on greenwashing-vs-substantive framings, analyze patterns. **README claim:** *"A quantitative analysis of climate-financial-disclosure framing patterns across N corporations + M cities."* Direct response to the financialization vignette in the paper; legible to ESG / sustainability-finance / climate-policy recruiters.
  4. **Mapillary CNN classifier for active-transportation infrastructure.** *"Predicting pop-up-style traffic-calming interventions from street-level imagery."* Train a torchvision CNN on Mapillary tiles annotated as pop-up vs car-dominated, transfer to other Canadian cities. Connects directly to the pop-ups vignette + Article 2's geospatial framing. **README claim:** *"A CNN-based detector for active-transportation infrastructure from Mapillary imagery, applied to Toronto's pop-up program evaluation."*
  5. **Practice-theory-informed LLM-application audit (most theoretical).** *"Auditing AI climate-action chatbots / recommenders against Soden et al.'s practices/scenes/infrastructures framework."* Pick 2-3 public AI climate tools (e.g., ClimateGPT, ClimateBERT-based dashboards, climate-action chatbots), audit each against the paper's six practices + critical-data-studies framework. Less ML-building, more ML-auditing. **README claim:** *"A practice-theory-informed audit of three public climate AI tools, evaluated against Soden et al. (2025)'s framework."* Strongest *theoretical depth* framing.
  
  **Strongest single recommendation: Framing 2 (LLM climate audit)** — it is the highest portfolio-impact framing for the responsible-AI / AI-ethics recruiter audience that *most matches* Classens's actual research direction across all three articles. **Framing 1 (equity-overlay dashboard)** is the most concrete + recruiter-legible to civic-tech / public-health / ESG audiences. **Framings 3-5 are stretch options** with progressively heavier theoretical load. **Note again:** Article 3 is *the most explicitly anti-ML of the three Classens articles* (cites information determinism, Weapons of Math Destruction, Pollution Is Colonialism, environmental data justice, beyond-technical-fixes), so any ML-building framing here must be carefully positioned as *responding to* — not replacing — the paper's critical-HCI argument.

**Article 3 verdict:** **The most explicitly anti-ML of the three Classens articles**, and a **clear theoretical confirmation** that Classens's research direction is anchored in critical data studies + community-engaged research rather than quantitative methods. **For ML-portfolio purposes**, the strongest framing is the LLM-audit project (Framing 2) — it is recruiter-legible to AI-ethics / responsible-AI audiences (Anthropic, Hugging Face, MILA AI ethics, Vector Institute), the framing is consistent with the paper's actual argument, and it produces a portfolio piece that demonstrates *thoughtful engagement* with a critical-data-studies framework rather than naive applied-ML. **The drop in score from Articles 1-2 (both 6.9) to Article 3 (5.9) reflects the deeper anti-ML positioning of this paper, NOT a weaker Classens signal** — Article 3 is in fact *the most theoretically substantive* Classens-collaborative paper in the survey, and adding the U of T Computer Science HCI register (Soden + Easterbrook) is a meaningful collaborative-network signal. **A Classens-supervised thesis emerging from this collaborative network would plausibly be a critical-HCI / responsible-AI / climate-justice thesis with ML as one tool among many** — exactly the kind of work the paper itself describes.

### Summary for this professor

- **Common thread across their work (now fully confirmed across Articles 1, 2, and 3):** **Critical engagement with environmental + climate data**, applied across food-systems sustainability (Article 1), small-city environmental-justice GIS (Article 2), and urban climate-data politics (Article 3). The unifying theoretical commitment across all three is a **principled critique of pure-quantitative / pure-ML approaches** ("information determinism" in Article 3, "weapons of math destruction" in Articles 2 and 3, "environmental data justice" in Articles 2 and 3) in favor of **plural, situated, equity-focused, community-engaged knowledge practices**. **Classens's role is consistent across all three papers**: he is the environmental-studies / critical-data-studies bridge for collaborative teams whose primary disciplinary home is elsewhere — Nutritional Sciences (Article 1), SSHRC Geography (Article 2), Computer Science (Article 3).
- **Dominant data modality:** **Three different modalities across the three articles**, but methodologically united by composite-index thinking + critical framing: tabular LCA + nutrition data (Article 1), tabular spatial DA-level indicators (Article 2), qualitative ethnographic + interview + document data (Article 3). **Article 2 (geospatial DA-level tabular) is the cleanest ML-friendly modality in Classens's collaborative oeuvre**; Article 3 has no ML-friendly modality (qualitative ethnographic).
- **Biggest obstacle for me to replicate their work publicly:** **The structural-fit question — confirmed across all three articles.** Classens collaborates on critical, community-engaged, qualitative-or-mixed-methods work that **explicitly critiques pure-ML approaches** (citing *Weapons of Math Destruction* in Articles 2 and 3, *Pollution Is Colonialism* in both, environmental data justice in both, information determinism in Article 3). A successful Classens-supervised thesis using ML would need to be positioned as *responding to* — not replacing — the critical-data-studies framework. **Compute and dataset access are easy across all three articles; tooling is moderate (geospatial Python from Article 2, transformers / LLM APIs from Article 3); the binding constraint is *epistemological positioning*, not technical.** **Funding stream is SSHRC, not NSERC** (per Article 2) — meaningful for thesis administration.
- **Skills I would gain by working with them:** **From Article 1:** LCA methodology + Nutri-Score literacy. **From Article 2:** Community-integrated GIS (CIGIS) methodology, CBPAR, area-based composite-index construction (CalEnviroScreen / ON-Marg / EJScreen lineage), Python geospatial stack (geopandas + folium + libpysal). **From Article 3:** Critical data studies (Loukissas, Mah, D'Ignazio & Klein, Liboiron, Vera et al.), practice theory (Bourdieu, Schatzki, Reckwitz, Shove), critical-HCI methodology (qualitative + interpretive), responsible-AI / FAccT framing fluency, **the meta-skill of being able to talk credibly about *why* pure-ML approaches to social-environmental problems are insufficient — this is exactly what AI-ethics / responsible-AI recruiters increasingly want**. **Recruiter mapping (refined by Article 3):** **Strong for** AI-ethics / responsible-AI / FAccT roles (Anthropic policy, Hugging Face, MILA AI ethics, Vector Institute responsible-AI, Google Research, OpenAI policy), civic-tech (Code for America, Toronto Public Health, City of Toronto data team), public-health agencies (PHAC, Public Health Ontario), climate-justice nonprofits, ESG consultancies, urban-planning practices, data-for-good orgs, critical-HCI academia. **Weak for:** pharma / biotech, materials science, generative-chemistry ML roles.

**Lab focus (one sentence):** **Inferred** from Article 1 + Abbatt's broader publication record (he is a long-tenured U of T atmospheric / indoor chemistry professor — see Abbatt & Wang 2020, *Environ. Sci. Process. Impacts* "The atmospheric chemistry of indoor environments," cited as ref. 39 in Article 1): **multiphase / heterogeneous chemistry of organic films and aerosols at the gas-condensed-phase interface**, with a current research thrust on **indoor surface chemistry — autoxidation of cooking oils, skin lipids, and other unsaturated lipid films under realistic indoor conditions, and the implications for human exposure to organic contaminants** (BPA, PAHs, flame retardants, plasticizers). **Verify** against the Abbatt group's full publication list (Google Scholar) and confirm that the Article-1 framing reflects his current rather than historical research direction.

**Lab webpage / Scholar:** Not yet provided — search U of T Department of Chemistry faculty directory + Google Scholar for "Jonathan P D Abbatt." His group has a long publication record; the Abbatt-corresponding paper Zhou et al. 2023 *Environ. Sci. Technol.* (ref. 27 in Article 1) is on the same indoor-oils-autoxidation theme, and Zhou et al. 2019 *Environ. Sci. Technol.* (ref. 46) is the multiphase-ozonolysis foundational paper.

### Article 1: Fast Autoxidation of Unsaturated Lipid Films on Indoor Surfaces (Nature Communications primary research, kinetic-modeling + experiment)

- **Full citation:** Wang, X., Fahy, W. D., Xie, L., Peng, H., & **Abbatt, J. P. D.** (2025). Fast autoxidation of unsaturated lipid films on indoor surfaces. *Nature Communications*, 16, 1557, 11 pages. DOI: 10.1038/s41467-025-56802-0. Published online 12 February 2025. CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 Open Access. **Funded by NSERC Canada (RGPIN-2023-03326 to J.P.D.A.).**
- **Authorship signal — strongest single-article PI signal in the survey to date.** **5-author paper, Abbatt is LAST + CORRESPONDING author** (senior PI). First author Xinke Wang (likely PhD student or postdoc at U of T Chemistry); middle authors William D. Fahy, Linna Xie, Hui Peng. Per the explicit author contributions: *"X.W. and J.P.D.A. designed the research project. X.W. performed the experiments. X.W., L.X., and H.P. analyzed data. **W.F. and J.P.D.A. did the kinetic modeling.** X.W. and J.P.D.A. wrote the paper."* **Abbatt is on the project design, the kinetic modeling, and the writing — strong PI involvement at every analytical step.** This is the cleanest single-article PI signal among first-articles processed across all 10 professors in the survey.
- **Problem in plain English:** Indoor surfaces accumulate organic films from cooking-oil residues, skin oils, and consumer-product emissions. These films contain **unsaturated lipids** (polyunsaturated triglycerides in cooking oil; methyl linolenate as a model compound) which are reactive toward radicals. The conventional wisdom is that **ozone is the primary indoor oxidant** that drives film aging. The team shows that under **dark indoor conditions** (a desk drawer with ~12 ppb ozone) and equally under **UVA irradiation** (mimicking sunlight 1 m from a south-facing window), the films undergo **rapid autoxidation** — a self-propagating radical chain reaction — producing high concentrations (~1 M) of **organic hydroperoxides (ROOHs)** that themselves degrade indoor contaminants like **bisphenol A (BPA)** into less-estrogenic monohydroxylated products. The model fits show the autoxidation rate is **insensitive to the radical-initiation rate** over 13 orders of magnitude — once the chain is started, internal radical propagation dominates. Health implications: ROS generated this way can cause oxidative stress, cell damage, and contribute to cancer / atherosclerosis / neurodegenerative pathways.
- **ML task type:** **Not primarily an ML paper, but unusually ML-friendly for chemistry.** The paper does **mechanistic kinetic modeling** with **rate-constant fitting from time-series data** — implemented in pure Python (SciPy `solve_ivp` with Radau IIA implicit Runge-Kutta + `curve_fit` with Trust Region Reflective + soft_l1 loss + Differential Evolution for initial values). **The Python source code is released in the Supplementary Information.** Multiple genuinely-ML-shaped reframes:
  - (i) **Bayesian extension of the rate-constant fit** — replace `curve_fit` + `Differential Evolution` with **PyMC** / **NumPyro** Hamiltonian Monte Carlo on the same ODE system; output posteriors + 95% credible intervals on kinit, k2, k3, k5 instead of point estimates. Quantifies the model's stated insensitivity to kinit (the paper notes kinit can vary from 10⁻²⁰ to 10⁻⁷ s⁻¹ with similar fits — Bayesian posterior would make this rigorous).
  - (ii) **Neural ODE comparison** — use **torchdiffeq** to fit a Neural ODE on the same time-series, compare residuals + extrapolation behavior against the mechanistic 6-reaction model. This is a clean "mechanistic vs. data-driven" benchmark.
  - (iii) **Surrogate emulator** — train a small MLP / Gaussian process to emulate the stiff ODE solver across rate-constant space. Useful for sensitivity analysis + Monte Carlo uncertainty propagation; demonstrates AI4Science workflow.
  - (iv) **MS/MS spectrum classifier for ROOH identification** — the paper uses neutral-loss-of-51-Da scans to identify peroxides. Build a CNN / transformer on **MoNA** / **GNPS** mass-spec libraries to classify peroxide-containing species automatically. Public datasets: ~600K spectra in MoNA, >1M in GNPS.
  - (v) **Structure-to-rate-constant prediction** — given a SMILES, predict autoxidation rate constants. Training data: NIST Chemical Kinetics Database (~38K reactions), Master Chemical Mechanism (MCM, ~17K reactions), Howard & Ingold 1967 *Can. J. Chem.* (cited in Article 1, ref. 77). RDKit features + LightGBM, or a small GNN.
  
  **All five framings are clean ML reframes that genuinely extend the paper's contribution rather than going against it** — a critical contrast to the Classens articles where ML reframings ran against the paper's stated epistemology.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: **Time-series tabular data** at the most basic level (ROOH concentration in M as a function of exposure time in hours, across multiple experimental conditions). The paper additionally produces **mass spectra** (ESI(+)-Orbitrap-MS direct infusion + UHPLC-MS/MS neutral-loss scans) and **EPR spectra** (X-band CW EPR with DMPO spin trap), but the kinetic model itself uses only the ROOH(t) time series.
  - Shape if stated: **Time-series with ~30-50 time points per condition × 5+ experimental conditions** (UVA irradiation at RH 0% and 44%, dark indoor, OH-radical exposure at ~1 ppt, ozone at 50/160 ppb). Mass-spec scans cover m/z 100-1500 at 70,000 resolving power. EPR spectra ~3270-3360 G with 300 G sweep width.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: Films deposited from chloroform solution onto Petri dishes; solvent-evaporated; exposed to chosen condition; extracted into 1-propanol; FOX2 + NPBA chemical assays for ROOH quantification; HRMS for product identification; benzoic-acid → hydroxybenzoic-acid quantification for OH radicals; DMPO spin-trap for organic radicals.
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: ROOH concentration as a function of time, integrated from the 6-reaction ODE system over given rate constants (kinit, k1, k2, k3, k4, k5, k6 — fitted parameters are kinit, k2, k3, k5).
  - Units or label space: Continuous (Molarity for concentration; s⁻¹ or M⁻¹ s⁻¹ for rate constants depending on reaction order).
- **Model architecture (brief):** **Mechanistic kinetic ODE system** (6 reactions: initiation + 4 propagation + 1 termination + 1 OH-sink), implemented in Python with SciPy `solve_ivp` (Radau IIA implicit Runge-Kutta) for the forward solve and `curve_fit` (Trust Region Reflective + soft_l1 loss) + Differential Evolution for parameter estimation. **The full source is in the Supplementary Information.**
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: **Newly generated experimental data on autoxidation of methyl linolenate + canola cooking oil films under controlled environmental conditions** (UVA irradiation, dark indoor, OH-radical exposure, ozone exposure) at U of T Chemistry. **Plus the Python kinetic-model source code.**
  - Size: ~5+ time-series experiments × ~30-50 time points each, plus mass spectra + EPR spectra + radical-trap quantifications.
  - Source: U of T Lash Miller Building (chemistry building) office desk drawer for dark indoor experiments + a custom-built quartz flow reactor for irradiation / OH / ozone experiments. Cooking oil sourced from a local Toronto retailer (per Article 1, methods).
  - Public? **Yes — fully released.** Per the Data availability statement: *"All data are available in the main paper, the Supplementary Information files and from corresponding author upon request. Source data are provided with this paper."* Per Code availability: *"The code used for the model along with the data modeled here is provided in the Supplementary Information."* **This is the rare paper in the survey with both data and code released as a clean SI deposit.**
  - Link: Direct access via Nature Communications — https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-56802-0 (article + Source Data file + SI Python script all open-access).
- **Reported headline metric:** Multiple. ROOH reaches ~1 M in cooking-oil films (Fig. 1c); ~3-4 M in ML films (Fig. 2c, d); the kinetic model fits the ROOH(t) curves with **k₂ = 14 M⁻¹ s⁻¹** (dark indoor + OH oxidation, identical) and **k₂ = 44 M⁻¹ s⁻¹** under irradiation; **k₃ = 5×10⁻⁶ s⁻¹** (dark) vs **5×10⁻⁵ s⁻¹** (irradiated, faster ROOH photolysis); **kinit < 5×10⁻⁷ s⁻¹** (upper bound; model is insensitive to kinit values from 10⁻²⁰ to 10⁻⁷ s⁻¹). EPR confirms DMPO-R (alkyl) + DMPO-OR (alkoxyl) radicals. BPA → HBPA (hydroxylated BPA) confirmed by HRMS in oxidized films.
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **The paper's own SI Python script + Source Data file** — match: **direct, perfect** — access: **easy** (download from Nature Communications). https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-56802-0
  - **NIST Chemical Kinetics Database** — ~38,000 gas-phase reactions × rate constants × references; gold-standard public kinetics resource. Match: **close** for the structure-to-rate-constant framing (Framing v). Access: **easy** — direct web query or bulk download. https://kinetics.nist.gov/kinetics/
  - **Master Chemical Mechanism (MCM v3.3.1)** — ~17,000 atmospheric oxidation reactions with rate constants + Arrhenius parameters, machine-readable XML. Match: **close** for atmospheric-chemistry kinetics framings. Access: **easy** — direct download. https://mcm.york.ac.uk/MCM/
  - **GECKO-A** (Generator of Explicit Chemistry and Kinetics of Organics in the Atmosphere) — automatically-generated mechanisms for VOC oxidation; match: **close** for autoxidation kinetics. Access: **medium** — request access. https://www2.acom.ucar.edu/modeling/gecko-a
  - **MassBank of North America (MoNA)** — ~600,000+ public mass spectra with annotations. Match: **close** for the MS-spectrum-classifier framing (Framing iv). Access: **easy** — direct CSV / JSON download. https://mona.fiehnlab.ucdavis.edu/
  - **GNPS (Global Natural Products Social Molecular Networking)** — ~1M+ public MS/MS spectra. Match: **close** for spectrum-classifier framing. Access: **easy** — direct download. https://gnps.ucsd.edu/
  - **Abbatt-group prior papers (rich data ecosystem)** — Zhou et al. 2023 *ES&T* "Chemical fate of oils on indoor surfaces" (ref. 27 in Article 1, same Abbatt lab), Zhou et al. 2022 *ES&T* "Multiphase ozonolysis of oleic-acid-based lipids" (ref. 28), Zhou et al. 2019 *ES&T* "Kinetics and condensed-phase products in multiphase ozonolysis" (ref. 46). All are Abbatt-corresponding-author papers on the same indoor-lipid theme — pull these for cross-paper consistency. Match: **direct** (same lab, same subject). Access: **medium** (some closed-access; all data should be available on request).
  - **HITRAN molecular spectroscopy database** — for gas-phase optical properties; match: **loose**. Access: **easy** (free for academic use). https://hitran.org/
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Easy.** The Source Data file + Python kinetic-model script are released open-access via Nature Communications. **No friction at all.** This is the rare combination of "primary research paper" + "released code" + "released data" in chemistry.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **Mostly within current toolset** with one new package per chosen framing.
  - Already-known: pandas + numpy + matplotlib + RDKit (from prior survey work).
  - Already-known (per the paper's own code): SciPy `solve_ivp` (Radau IIA stiff solver), `curve_fit` (Trust Region Reflective), `differential_evolution`. No new ecosystem if reproducing the paper directly.
  - **For Framing i (Bayesian)**: **PyMC** (`pymc` + `pytensor`) or **NumPyro** (`numpyro` + `jax`) + **arviz** for posterior visualization. Modest new lift but well-documented.
  - **For Framing ii (Neural ODE)**: **torchdiffeq** + standard PyTorch. Modest new lift.
  - **For Framing iii (Surrogate)**: scikit-learn / GPyTorch — already known.
  - **For Framing iv (MS classifier)**: **matchms** + **spectrum_utils** for spectrum I/O; PyTorch CNN — modest new lift on top of standard PyTorch.
  - **For Framing v (Structure-to-k)**: RDKit features + LightGBM — entirely within current toolset; or **PyTorch Geometric** for a GNN — moderate new lift.
  - Domain reading: Abbatt & Wang (2020) *Environ. Sci. Process. Impacts* on indoor atmospheric chemistry; Crounse et al. (2013) *J. Phys. Chem. Lett.* on autoxidation; Yin, Xu & Porter (2011) *Chem. Rev.* on free-radical lipid peroxidation. **These are accessible to a chemistry-undergrad reader without a heavy domain-tooling lift.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Five viable framings, all of which **genuinely extend the paper's contribution**:
  1. **Reproduction-with-Bayesian-extension (most direct + recruiter-legible).** *"Reproducing the autoxidation kinetic model from Wang et al. (Nat. Comm. 2025) and extending it to a Bayesian framework using PyMC."* Clone the SI Python script, reproduce the published rate-constant fits, then re-fit with HMC to obtain posteriors on (kinit, k2, k3, k5). **Quantify the paper's stated kinit insensitivity rigorously** (the paper notes kinit can vary from 10⁻²⁰ to 10⁻⁷ s⁻¹ — Bayesian posterior would show the actual likelihood profile). Notebook + repository + README. **README claim:** *"A Bayesian re-analysis of the indoor-lipid autoxidation kinetic model from Wang et al. 2025, with full rate-constant posteriors and a rigorous quantification of the paper's stated kinit insensitivity."* **This is the strongest single framing — direct paper engagement, clean ML extension, demonstrates rigorous Bayesian-ODE-fitting (a hot AI4Science skill).**
  2. **Mechanistic vs. Neural-ODE benchmark.** *"Mechanistic 6-reaction kinetics vs. Neural ODE on indoor lipid autoxidation time-series."* Use torchdiffeq to fit a Neural ODE on the same ROOH(t) data, compare in-sample fit + out-of-sample extrapolation against the mechanistic model. **README claim:** *"A head-to-head comparison of mechanistic vs. data-driven kinetic models on indoor-lipid autoxidation, demonstrating where each approach wins."* Strong AI4Science framing for ML / physics-ML recruiters.
  3. **Surrogate emulator + sensitivity analysis.** *"A neural surrogate emulator for the indoor-lipid autoxidation ODE solver, enabling 1000× faster Monte Carlo sensitivity analysis."* Sample (kinit, k2, k3, k5) from a Latin Hypercube design, run the stiff solver, train a small MLP / GP emulator, then use the emulator to quantify global sensitivity (Sobol' indices). **README claim:** *"A 1000× faster surrogate for the autoxidation kinetic model, enabling rigorous global sensitivity analysis."* Demonstrates AI4Science workflow: surrogate construction + active learning + uncertainty propagation.
  4. **MS/MS peroxide classifier.** *"A transformer-based classifier for organic-peroxide identification from MS/MS spectra, trained on MoNA + GNPS."* Build a binary classifier (peroxide vs. non-peroxide) using neutral-loss-of-51-Da and other peroxide-diagnostic patterns. Match the paper's manual MS/MS-based ROOH identification automatically. **README claim:** *"An automated peroxide classifier that reproduces the manual ROOH-identification heuristic from Wang et al. (Nat. Comm. 2025)."* Mass-spectrometry-ML is a niche-but-real recruiter area (metabolomics, proteomics, environmental analytical chemistry).
  5. **Structure-to-rate-constant ML on NIST Kinetics + MCM.** *"Predicting autoxidation rate constants from molecular structure using LightGBM on NIST Chemical Kinetics + MCM."* Pull rate constants for OH + alkene reactions, RDKit-featurize, train LightGBM. Compare predictions to published values for methyl linolenate and other unsaturated lipids. **README claim:** *"A LightGBM model for predicting OH-alkene rate constants from SMILES, trained on NIST Chemical Kinetics."* Standalone tabular-ML project; lightest ML lift.
  
  **Strongest single recommendation: Framing 1 (Bayesian extension)** — it is the cleanest direct engagement with an Abbatt-corresponding-author paper, demonstrates a concrete AI4Science skill (Bayesian ODE fitting), and the success criterion is unambiguous (reproduce the published fits, then add posteriors). **Framings 2-3 build on this** with progressively heavier ML-novelty load. **Framings 4-5 are more standalone** but still leverage the paper's domain. **All five framings are within the paper's stated argument** rather than against it — a clean contrast to the Classens articles.

#### Article 1 ranking

| Variable | Score (1–10) | One-line justification |
|---|---|---|
| Data availability | 8 | Paper releases its own data (Source Data file) + code (SI Python script) — gold-standard for a primary-research chemistry paper. NIST Chemical Kinetics + MCM + MoNA + GNPS provide rich public substrates for the ML reframings; not at the QM9 / ZINC one-line-loader benchmark level, but very close |
| Data access ease | 9 | Source Data + SI Python: direct download. NIST Kinetics: direct web query. MCM: direct XML download. MoNA / GNPS: direct download. No friction anywhere |
| Task tractability on laptop / free GPU | 9 | 6-reaction stiff ODE with 5 species + ~30-50 time points: trains in seconds on CPU. Bayesian extension via PyMC: minutes on laptop. Neural ODE: free Colab GPU. Surrogate emulator + MS-classifier + structure-to-k all fit on free-tier hardware |
| Tooling alignment with my current skills | 8 | Standard SciPy / pandas / sklearn ecosystem (per the paper's own code) is already known. One new package per chosen framing — PyMC for Bayesian, torchdiffeq for Neural ODE, matchms for MS spectra, PyTorch Geometric (optional) for GNN structure-to-k. All well-documented, standard Python stack. **No heavy new domain ecosystem** (no cobrapy / SBML / DFT / ArcGIS lift) |
| Problem clarity | 9 | Crystal-clear ML-shaped problem: time-series ROOH measurements + 6-reaction mechanistic ODE + 4 fitted rate constants + multiple experimental conditions for cross-validation. Textbook-clean inverse-problem / ODE-fitting setup with a clear supervised structure |
| Reproducibility signals | 9 | Code AND data both released as Supplementary Information; Nature Communications open-access; explicit Data + Code availability statements; full Methods section with all chemicals, instrument settings, model parameters. The rare 9-out-of-10 reproducibility case in the chemistry portion of the survey |
| Portfolio impact | 7 | Indoor / atmospheric chemistry is a legitimate AI4Science recruiter area (especially with the indoor-air-quality + human-health + endocrine-disruptor angle that Article 1 emphasizes via BPA), but less hot than drug discovery / generative chemistry / materials. Maps to: AI4Science labs (Vector Institute, MILA, Microsoft AI4Science), atmospheric / environmental research (EPA, ECCC, NCAR), pharma oxidative-stress drug development, indoor-air startups |

**Article 1 overall score:** Provisional (7 of 8 variables): (8 + 9 + 9 + 8 + 9 + 9 + 7) / 7 = 59 / 7 = **8.4 / 10**

**Article 1 verdict:** **The strongest single article processed in the entire survey to date** — by a clear margin. The combination of (a) released code + data, (b) clean ML-shaped ODE-fitting problem, (c) last + corresponding-author PI signal, (d) NSERC funding + Department of Chemistry home unit, (e) chemistry undergraduate-accessible domain (no heavy chemistry-ML tooling lift), and (f) multiple genuinely-extending ML reframes (Bayesian / Neural ODE / surrogate / MS-classifier / structure-to-k) makes this the **cleanest "ship a credible GitHub project" candidate in the survey**. **The strongest single recommendation is Framing 1: reproduce the SI Python kinetic model and extend it with PyMC Bayesian inference** — this directly engages an Abbatt-corresponding-author paper, demonstrates a hot AI4Science skill (Bayesian ODE fitting), and has an unambiguous success criterion. **Pending two more articles to confirm the lab-level pattern.**

### Article 2: VOC Emission Rates from an Indoor Surface Using a Flux Chamber and PTR-MS (CASA 2022 NIST campaign, *Atmospheric Environment*)

- **Full citation:** Huynh, H. N., Ditto, J. C., Yu, J., Link, M. F., Poppendieck, D., Farmer, D. K., Vance, M. E., & **Abbatt, J. P. D.** (2024). VOC emission rates from an indoor surface using a flux chamber and PTR-MS. *Atmospheric Environment*, 338, 120817. DOI: 10.1016/j.atmosenv.2024.120817. CC BY-NC 4.0 Open Access. Available online 12 September 2024. **Funded by the Alfred P. Sloan Foundation** (grants G-2019-11404 and G-2020-13929 for the CASA consortium).
- **Authorship signal — second strong PI signal.** **8-author paper, Abbatt is LAST + CO-CORRESPONDING author** (with first author Han N. Huynh, who was at U of T Chemistry during the work but is now at NOAA Chemical Sciences Lab + CIRES Boulder). Per the explicit CRediT statement: *"Jonathan P.D. Abbatt: Writing – review & editing, Writing – original draft, Validation, Supervision, Project administration, Methodology, Investigation, Funding acquisition, Formal analysis, Conceptualization."* — Abbatt covers nearly every CRediT category. Co-authors include CASA consortium PIs Delphine Farmer (Colorado State Chemistry), Marina Vance (CU Boulder Mechanical Engineering), and Dustin Poppendieck (NIST). **Two-articles-deep cross-paper consistency on the lab-direction signal: both articles are Abbatt-last-corresponding, both on indoor / atmospheric chemistry, both at U of T Chemistry, both methodologically rigorous analytical chemistry + modeling.**
- **Problem in plain English:** Indoor air VOC concentrations are typically much higher than outdoor concentrations because of indoor sources (cooking, cleaning, occupants) plus outgassing from surface reservoirs (paint, wallboard, wood, fabrics, furnishings). Conventionally, surface emissions are measured by either (a) putting individual materials into laboratory environmental chambers, or (b) measuring whole-house indoor air composition + air change rate. The team asks: **can a small flux chamber placed on one painted surface in a real house quantitatively predict the whole-house emission rate?** They deploy a stainless-steel flux chamber (176 cm² surface area, 460 cm³/min flow) on a painted windowsill in NIST's NZERTF Net-Zero Energy Residential Test Facility, coupled to PTR-ToF-MS for online VOC detection across 9 flux measurements + 6 weeks of campaign-long ambient indoor + outdoor air sampling. **Headline finding: flux-chamber emission rate × house painted area ≈ whole-house emission rate, with ratio 1.3 ± 1.0 averaged across 35 calibrated VOCs.** This is surprising because the flux chamber samples one small surface, but the agreement implies VOCs have redistributed across all house surfaces over the 12-year house lifetime to a quasi-steady-state thermodynamic equilibrium — molecules in any one surface reservoir reflect the thermodynamic activity of all the others. Comparison to a 2014 Poppendieck baseline (when the house was new) shows aromatics and monoterpenes have decayed ~77-85% over 8 years (slow degassing of original construction materials), while aldehydes are sustained (secondary products from ongoing ozone-surface reactions).
- **ML task type:** **Not primarily an ML paper — measurement / instrumentation / quantitative-emission-budget paper.** Less ML-shaped than Article 1's clean ODE-fitting setup; more of a "build the dataset + show the headline finding" paper. **Multiple constructive ML reframings:**
  - (i) **PMF / NMF source apportionment on the campaign-long PTR-MS time series** — Positive Matrix Factorization is the standard atmospheric-chemistry technique for source attribution. Run NMF / PMF on the ~6 weeks of fast-time-resolution PTR-MS data + 35 calibrated VOCs, identify source factors (cooking, cleaning, paint outgassing, ozonolysis secondary products, occupants, outdoor infiltration). Compare factors to known CASA perturbation events (smoke, NH₃, CO₂, RH/T per Farmer et al. 2024). **Genuinely ML-shaped + standard atmospheric-chemistry technique.**
  - (ii) **PTR-MS fragmentation / signal-attribution classifier** — PTR ionization causes ion fragmentation (the paper notes only 0.6% of C₅H₉⁺ signal is actually isoprene). Build a transformer / CNN that predicts fragmentation patterns from molecular structure, automating the manual GC-PTR-MS speciation the paper does. Public spectral substrates: MoNA, GNPS, NIST mass spec library.
  - (iii) **T + RH + perturbation regression on emission rates** — The paper notes 10°C T bumps drive broad emission increases, and RH increases drive water-soluble-VOC (ethanol, hexanal, acetic acid) increases. Fit a multivariate LightGBM / random forest for emission rate as a function of T, RH, perturbation type, surface material, and VOC chemical class. Tabular ML; lightest framing.
  - (iv) **Time-series forecasting on indoor VOC traces** — Standard time-series ML (LSTM / Temporal Fusion Transformer / Prophet) trained to predict near-future indoor VOC mixing ratios from past trajectories + outdoor concentrations + HVAC state. Useful for indoor-air-quality monitoring + smart-building control.
  - (v) **Whole-house emission scaling generalization** — The paper's core finding is that flux-chamber × painted-area ≈ whole-house emission, with ratio 1.3 ± 1.0. Build a Gaussian process / regression model that takes (flux measurements at ≥1 surface) × (house surface inventory) → predicted whole-house emission rate, validated across HOMEChem 2018, NZERTF baseline (Poppendieck 2015), and CASA 2022. This is the strongest **deployable-tool framing**.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: **Tabular + time-series**. Per VOC + per measurement: protonated-ion m/z, signal counts per second, calibrated mixing ratio (ppb), flux-chamber concentration (molecule cm⁻³), area-specific emission rate (μg m⁻² h⁻¹). Plus per-spectrum-acquisition: full PTR-MS m/z 100-1500 spectra at 0.25 Hz × 6 weeks (large but tractable).
  - Shape if stated: 35 calibrated VOCs × 9 flux-chamber experiments + ~6 weeks of campaign-long indoor measurements at 0.25 Hz (~1.5 million spectra per VOC, but most analysis is at 5-min averages = ~12,000 averaged time points).
  - Preprocessing in the paper: PTR-MS mass calibration to ≤5 ppm peak-location accuracy across m/z 37-380; ion-list filtering by 3σ above zero-period LOD; background subtraction from interpolated zero periods; sensitivity correction via in-field + post-campaign gas-mixture calibrations; GC-PTR-MS speciation of 19 chromatograms to determine fragmentation interferences per protonated ion.
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: **Not a model — a derived emission rate.** Three calculation methods: (1) flux-chamber: Eₐᵣₑₐ,𝒸ₕₐₘᵦₑᵣ = Q × [C] × M / (A × Nₐ); (2) time-dependent ambient: Eₐᵣₑₐ,ₐₘᵦᵢₑₙₜ via Δ[C]/Δt + λ × ([C]ₐᵢᵣ - [C]ₒᵤₜ); (3) steady-state ambient: Eₐᵣₑₐ = λ × ([C]ₐᵢᵣ - [C]ₒᵤₜ). All units μg m⁻² h⁻¹.
  - Units or label space: Continuous (μg m⁻² h⁻¹ for area-specific; molecule cm⁻³ s⁻¹ for volume-specific).
- **Model architecture (brief):** **None — analytical-chemistry instrumentation + steady-state mass balance + comparative methodology.** The "modeling" is the 3 emission-rate calculation equations above + the comparison framework.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: **CASA 2022 NIST NZERTF flux-chamber + ambient PTR-MS dataset** (newly generated for this paper). Plus comparison to Poppendieck et al. 2015 baseline data.
  - Size: 35 quantified VOCs × 9 flux-chamber experiments + 6 weeks of continuous indoor + periodic outdoor PTR-MS at 0.25 Hz. Plus auxiliary data (HVAC air-change rate measurements via SF₆ tracer-decay, T + RH sensors, ozone monitor).
  - Source: NIST Net-Zero Energy Residential Test Facility (NZERTF) in Gaithersburg, Maryland, USA; CASA campaign March-April 2022.
  - Public? **Partially.** Per Data availability: *"Data will be made available on request."* The Farmer et al. 2024 CASA overview paper (*Environ. Sci. Process. Impacts*) is the consortium-wide companion; CASA data is being released over time via the consortium. The 2014 Poppendieck baseline data is via NIST Public Access Plan. **Less open than Article 1's Source Data file.**
  - Link: Email Abbatt (jonathan.abbatt@utoronto.ca) or Huynh (han.huynh@noaa.gov) for the paper's primary dataset; check NIST CASA data portal + Farmer et al. 2024 supplementary information for consortium-wide releases.
- **Reported headline metric:** **Flux-chamber emission ≈ whole-house emission with ratio 1.3 ± 1.0** averaged across 35 VOCs (the headline result). Individual VOC emission rates span ~4 orders of magnitude (formic acid + acetic acid + ethanol at ~10² μg m⁻² h⁻¹; acrylonitrile at ~10⁻² μg m⁻² h⁻¹). Time-dependent vs. steady-state methods agree to within 10%. Aromatics + monoterpenes ~77-85% lower in 2022 vs. 2014 baseline; aldehydes sustained at ~6% higher. Outdoor concentrations <20% of indoor for most species (acetonitrile is the exception at 40-70%).
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **HOMEChem 2018 dataset** — UC Berkeley Goldstein lab + multi-PI campaign at the UTest house in Austin, similar PTR-MS time-series of indoor VOCs across cooking / cleaning / occupancy events. Match: **direct** for source-apportionment + time-series-forecasting framings. Access: **easy** — public release via Indoor Air Special Issue; data DOI cited by HOMEChem papers. https://indoorchem.org/projects/homechem/
  - **Wang et al. 2020 *Sci. Adv.*** "Surface reservoirs dominate dynamic gas-surface partitioning of many indoor air constituents" — same Abbatt-corresponding paper, public data. Match: **close** (same lab, related theme). Access: **medium** — contact authors / supplementary release.
  - **Liu & Abbatt 2021 *PNAS*** "Liquid crystal display screens as a source for indoor volatile organic compounds" — same Abbatt-lab paper, also PTR-MS-based. Match: **close**. Access: **medium**.
  - **NIST CASA data portal** — NIST's Public Access Plan + CASA consortium-wide data releases. Match: **direct** (the paper's primary substrate). Access: **medium** — request-based for some data, public for others.
  - **MoNA (MassBank of North America)** + **GNPS** — for the PTR-MS spectrum classifier framing (ii). Match: **close** — most public spectra are EI / ESI, not PTR; but principles of fragmentation classification transfer. Access: **easy** — direct download.
  - **EPA AQS (Air Quality System)** — for outdoor reference VOC data (the paper's outdoor sampling provides a reference baseline). Match: **loose** (outdoor only). Access: **easy** — public API. https://www.epa.gov/aqs
  - **OpenAQ** — global air-quality reference. Match: **loose**. Access: **easy** — free API. https://openaq.org/
  - **Indoor Chem Hub** — emerging community resource. Match: **close** (community-curated indoor-air datasets). Access: **easy**. https://indoorchem.org/
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium.** "Data will be made available on request" — accessible but requires emailing Abbatt or Huynh. **Less easy than Article 1's Source Data file.** Topic-adjacent public datasets (HOMEChem, NIST CASA, MoNA) are easy.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **Mostly within current toolset** with one or two new packages per framing.
  - Already-known: pandas + numpy + sklearn + matplotlib + RDKit.
  - **For Framing i (PMF / NMF source apportionment)**: sklearn `NMF` is built-in; `nimfa` provides advanced PMF variants. **EPA PMF 5.0** is the gold-standard atmospheric-chemistry tool but is a Windows GUI — user can either learn it or substitute with sklearn NMF. Modest new lift.
  - **For Framing ii (MS spectrum classifier)**: matchms + spectrum_utils + Hugging Face transformers. Modest new lift.
  - **For Framing iii (T+RH regression)**: pure pandas + LightGBM — already known. Lightest framing.
  - **For Framing iv (time-series forecasting)**: prophet (lightest), GluonTS / pytorch-forecasting (heavier). Modest new lift.
  - **For Framing v (whole-house emission GP)**: GPyTorch / sklearn GaussianProcessRegressor. Modest new lift.
  - Domain reading: Abbatt & Wang (2020) *Environ. Sci. Process. Impacts* "atmospheric chemistry of indoor environments" (cited in Article 2); Wang et al. (2020) *Sci. Adv.* "surface reservoirs dominate dynamic gas-surface partitioning" (cited in Article 2); Krechmer et al. (2018) *Anal. Chem.* on Vocus PTR-MS; National Academies of Sciences (2022) *Why Indoor Chemistry Matters* report. **Domain accessible to chemistry undergrads with a few weeks of indoor-air-chemistry literature reading.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** Five viable framings, with progressively heavier ML-novelty load:
  1. **Source apportionment via NMF on HOMEChem (most direct + recruiter-legible).** *"Identifying VOC source factors from continuous PTR-MS time-series via Non-negative Matrix Factorization, applied to the HOMEChem 2018 dataset and benchmarked against expert-labeled cooking / cleaning / occupancy events."* Use HOMEChem as the public substrate (since CASA is request-only); NMF on the time × VOC matrix; interpret factors against the campaign log; deploy as Streamlit / Plotly Dash. **README claim:** *"An NMF-based source apportionment of HOMEChem 2018 indoor VOC time-series, recovering N source factors that match X% of the expert-labeled events."* **Strongest single framing for an Article-2-anchored portfolio** because it directly engages atmospheric-chemistry-standard methodology, is recruiter-legible to indoor-air-quality + smart-building audiences, and uses a fully public dataset.
  2. **Whole-house emission scaling Gaussian process.** *"A Gaussian-process surrogate for whole-house VOC emission rates from spot flux-chamber measurements."* Train on (HOMEChem, Poppendieck 2015 NZERTF baseline, optionally Wang et al. 2020) with cross-validation across houses; predict whole-house rates from a handful of flux-chamber spot measurements. **README claim:** *"Predicting whole-house VOC emissions from a single flux-chamber measurement, with calibrated uncertainty across N indoor-air datasets."* Strong tool-deployment + uncertainty-quantification narrative for AI4Science audiences.
  3. **Indoor VOC time-series forecasting with Temporal Fusion Transformer.** *"Forecasting indoor VOC concentrations from past trajectories + outdoor reference + HVAC state, using a Temporal Fusion Transformer benchmarked against Prophet + LightGBM."* Standard time-series ML on HOMEChem or other public datasets. **README claim:** *"A 1-hour-ahead forecast of indoor VOC concentrations with X% MAE, deployed as a smart-building API."* Strong for indoor-air-quality startup audiences (Awair, Atmotube, Carrier, Trane).
  4. **PTR-MS fragmentation classifier.** *"A transformer that predicts PTR-MS fragmentation patterns from molecular structure, automating the GC-PTR-MS speciation in Huynh et al. 2024."* Train on MoNA + simulated PTR-MS spectra; deploy as a tool that, given a SMILES, predicts protonated-ion + dominant-fragment m/z. **README claim:** *"A SMILES-to-PTR-MS-fragmentation predictor, automating the manual GC-PTR-MS speciation in Huynh et al. (Atmos. Environ. 2024)."* Niche but real (analytical-chemistry + metabolomics audience).
  5. **T + RH + perturbation regression on CASA / HOMEChem.** *"Predicting indoor VOC emission-rate sensitivity to temperature, humidity, and chemical perturbations via LightGBM."* Tabular regression. Lightest framing; cleanest single-notebook project. **README claim:** *"A LightGBM model that predicts indoor VOC emission-rate response to T / RH / perturbation type with X% R²."*
  
  **Strongest single recommendation: Framing 1 (NMF source apportionment on HOMEChem)** — it is the cleanest direct engagement with the paper's methodology family, uses fully public data (HOMEChem rather than CASA-on-request), is the standard atmospheric-chemistry technique for source attribution (recruiter-legible), and produces a deployable Streamlit / Plotly Dash artifact. **Framing 2 (whole-house GP)** is the strongest follow-up because it directly extends the paper's headline finding (flux-chamber × painted-area ≈ whole-house) to a deployable cross-house tool. **All five framings sit within the paper's stated argument** — like Article 1, this is a clean contrast to the Classens articles.

### Article 3: Analytical Optical Methods for Measuring Organic Peroxides and Hydroperoxides — An Evaluation (Spain-led UCLM collaboration, *Atmospheric Environment*)

- **Full citation:** Alba-Elena, D., Baeza-Romero, M. T., Wang, X., **Abbatt, J.**, Hernandez, C., & Valero, E. (2024). Analytical optical methods for measuring organic peroxides and hydroperoxides: An evaluation. *Atmospheric Environment*, 339, 120858, 13 pages. DOI: 10.1016/j.atmosenv.2024.120858. Published online 11 October 2024. CC BY-NC 4.0 Open Access. **Funded by the Spanish Ministry of Science, Innovation and Universities (PID 2019-106468RB-I00, PID 2022-139724OB-I00) + INNOCAM Castilla-La Mancha (SBPLY/23/180225/000194) + UCLM (No.2022-GRIN-34199) + NSERC Canada (RGPIN-2023-03326, the same Abbatt grant cited on Article 1).**
- **Authorship caveat:** Abbatt is **4th of 6 authors (MIDDLE author), NOT corresponding.** Senior corresponding authors are **M. T. Baeza-Romero (UCLM, Toledo, Spain)** and **X. Wang (formerly Abbatt's group, U of T)** — the latter is the author who ran the Toronto-side experiments in Abbatt's lab. The CRediT statement lists Abbatt's contribution as only "Writing – review & editing, Investigation, Funding acquisition." **This is a Spain-led methods-comparison paper with U of T as a secondary lab, not an Abbatt-group primary research paper.** Treat the lab-signal weight as substantially lower than Articles 1 and 2 (both of which were last+corresponding-author primary research from Abbatt's group).
- **Problem in plain English:** Hydrogen peroxide, organic peroxides (ROOR'), and organic hydroperoxides (ROOH) are atmospherically critical molecules that contribute to secondary organic aerosol (SOA) mass and to the reactive-oxygen-species health burden of fine particulates. Multiple spectrophotometric assays exist to measure them — but each assay has different sensitivities to different peroxide species in different solvents, and **no comprehensive head-to-head comparison existed**. This paper evaluates 4 optical assays — traditional iodometric, microwave-assisted iodometric, NPBA (4-nitrophenyl boronic acid), FOX2 (Fenton-reaction-assisted ferrous-xylenol) — across 5 solvents (water, 1-propanol, methanol, acetonitrile, chloroform) and 6 commercial peroxide species (H₂O₂, Tert-Bu-OOH, Cumyl-OO, Benz-OO, Lau-OO, Tert-Bu-OO), then applies all 4 to laboratory-generated SOA from α-pinene ozonolysis to demonstrate that combining iodometric methods (which detect ROOH + ROOR') with FOX2 or NPBA (which detect only ROOH) lets you separately quantify hydroperoxides vs. organic peroxides — a previously inaccessible mechanistic decomposition.
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper.** Reframings are constructive but well-precedented in chemometrics. (1) Multi-output linear regression / NMF on UV-Vis absorbance vectors (5 wavelengths × 6 species → concentration) for spectral mixture deconvolution. (2) Tabular method-selection classifier (sample properties → optimal assay). (3) Domain-adaptation / calibration-transfer between two labs (UCLM and U of T) using different microwave ovens and spectrophotometers. (4) Bayesian ODE fit on the NPBA reaction-time-vs-slope kinetic curve (Fig. 2 — Trust-Region-Reflective or PyMC HMC).
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: tabular UV-Vis absorbance values at fixed wavelengths (288, 352, 420 nm for iodometric; 400 / 406 nm for NPBA; 560 nm for FOX2) measured on a quartz cuvette via UVIKON 930 or Ocean Optics spectrometer
  - Shape / size if stated: calibration tables span ~1–60 μM peroxide concentration; molar extinction coefficients ε ranging from 3201 (iodometric, 420 nm) to 51,571 M⁻¹ cm⁻¹ (FOX2, 560 nm); LoDs 0.5–1.5 μM; LoQs 1.6–5 μM. SOA experiments: 33–81 μg of filter-collected α-pinene-SOA, extracted in 20 mL water or 1-propanol, analyzed in 0.5–3 mL aliquots
  - Preprocessing: Solvent-blank subtraction (E1) + filter-blank subtraction (E2) for SOA samples; PTFE syringe filtration (0.45 μm); pH adjustment (3 for traditional iodometric, 1.9 for microwave-assisted iodometric, 12.75 for NPBA); KI addition (30 mg traditional, 150 mg microwave); incubation (1 h RT iodometric, 0.33 min @ 25°C microwave, 4 h @ 85°C NPBA, 30 min RT FOX2)
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts (in the paper): peroxide / hydroperoxide concentration in μM, then converted to %wt of SOA particles (assuming 200 g/mol average MW) and to nmol per μg of particles
  - Units / label space: μM (calibration) → ng / μg (SOA) → %wt (~22-25% wt of SOA mass for α-pinene ozonolysis at [O₃]/[α-pinene] = 81)
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. The "model" is **simple linear regression of absorbance vs. concentration (Beer-Lambert law)**, with slopes (ε) reported as molar extinction coefficients (Tables S2, S4, S5, S6). Parameter optimization for solvent compatibility done by **manual scan over solvent dielectric properties + boiling points** (Table S3).
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Custom-generated calibration tables + α-pinene-ozonolysis SOA samples
  - Size: ~6 peroxide species × 5 solvents × 4 assays × multiple concentrations = a few hundred absorbance measurements; 6 SOA experiments (3 in water, 3 in 1-propanol) for Table 3, plus 3 spiked-Lau-OO experiments for Table 4
  - Source: Generated in two labs — UCLM (Toledo, Spain) and U of T Chemistry (Toronto). α-pinene SOA from glass aerosol flow tube (60 cm × 7 cm i.d.) at [O₃]/[α-pinene] = 81, RH = 7 ± 2%, 298–300 K
  - Public? **No** — "Data will be made available on request"
  - Link: Email Baeza-Romero (mariateresa.baeza@uclm.es) or X. Wang (xinkezx.wang@utoronto.ca) for the paper's primary data
- **Reported headline metric:** All four methods agree within 2σ uncertainty for total peroxide content in α-pinene SOA (~22–25% wt across methods, 1.1–1.3 nmol/μg). FOX2 most sensitive (ε = 51,571 ± 1716 M⁻¹ cm⁻¹). NPBA + FOX2 best LoD (0.5 μM in water). Iodometric assays (traditional + microwave-assisted) detect ROOR' + ROOH + H₂O₂; NPBA + FOX2 detect only ROOH + H₂O₂. Combined iodometric + FOX2/NPBA enables separation of [ROOR'] from [ROOH] when added Lau-OO is back-calculated to within uncertainties (4 ± 1 μM recovered vs. 3.6 μM spiked).
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **NIST Chemistry WebBook UV-Vis cross-section database** — covers ~few hundred small molecules, indexed by formula. Match: **loose** (UV-Vis spectra of related compounds, but not assay-specific calibration data). Access: **easy** — direct download via NIST WebBook web interface — https://webbook.nist.gov/chemistry/. Useful for cross-checking ε values reported in this paper but not for replicating the SOA experiments
  - **PhotoChemCAD database** — Lindsey Lab, NCSU; ~500 compounds with UV-Vis absorption + emission spectra. Match: **loose**. Access: **easy** — http://www.photochemcad.com/
  - **Synthetic data generation** — use the paper's reported ε values (Table 5) + Beer-Lambert + Gaussian noise to simulate UV-Vis calibration spectra at known concentrations. Match: **direct, but synthetic** — fully recovers the paper's setup without needing real lab access. Access: **trivially easy** — write a Python function (~30 lines)
  - **Aimanant & Ziemann 2013** "spectrophotometric methods for the analysis of functional groups in oxidized organic aerosol" *Aerosol Sci. Technol.* — methodology paper directly cited; data on request
  - **AtChem2 / KPP (Kinetic PreProcessor) chemical kinetics frameworks** — for the NPBA-kinetic-fitting reframe, AtChem2 has a public Master Chemical Mechanism + α-pinene SOA chemistry. Match: **adjacent** (mechanistic kinetics, not the assay). Access: **easy** — https://github.com/AtChem/AtChem2
  - **Wang et al. 2023** *Chem. Rev.* (cited in this paper) "Organic peroxides in aerosol: key reactive intermediates for multiphase processes in the atmosphere" — same X. Wang + Abbatt review article; literature-mined ε / LoD tables across published peroxide assays. Match: **close** (literature-extraction substrate). Access: **medium** — paywalled but text-mineable via institutional access
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Hard.** Data on request only; would need to email Baeza-Romero or Wang. Synthesizing the calibration data is trivial (Beer-Lambert + Table 5 ε values), but the SOA samples are not reproducible without flow-tube + ozonizer + microbalance + UV-Vis spectrometer access. **Substantially harder than Articles 1 and 2** — Article 1 had a Source Data file, Article 2 had related-public CASA / HOMEChem datasets to substitute, but Article 3 has neither.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:**
  - Already-known: pandas / numpy / scipy / sklearn linear regression, Beer-Lambert law (chemistry undergrad), Python plotting / matplotlib
  - Light additions: **scipy.optimize.curve_fit** with bounds (already used in Article 1's framing) for the NPBA-kinetic-fit reframe; **sklearn NMF** or scipy.optimize.nnls (non-negative least squares) for the spectral-mixture-deconvolution reframe; **PyMC** for the Bayesian-kinetic-fit reframe (overlap with Article 1 toolkit)
  - **No heavy domain ecosystem lift.** Everything in the analytical-chemistry side is classical chemometrics (PCA, NMF, partial least squares) which is sklearn-native
  - Domain reading: Wang, Abbatt et al. 2023 *Chem. Rev.* "Organic peroxides in aerosol" (cited in this paper, same authors); Aimanant & Ziemann 2013 *Aerosol Sci. Technol.* on functional-group spectrophotometric assays; Banerjee & Budke 1964 *Anal. Chem.* on iodometric peroxide quantification (foundational reference). **Domain accessible to chemistry undergrads with basic Beer-Lambert + chemometrics reading.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project would look like:**
  - **Strongest single framing — Spectral mixture deconvolution / chemometrics:** *"Hydroperoxide vs. peroxide quantification from UV-Vis spectra using NMF and constrained least squares — a chemometrics tool for SOA analytical chemistry."* Synthesize calibration data using the paper's Table 5 ε values + Gaussian noise (→ 6 species × 5 solvents × ~50 concentrations × 7 wavelengths = ~2000 samples). Train sklearn NMF (or non-negative least squares) to recover {[H₂O₂], [Tert-Bu-OOH], [Cumyl-OO], [Benz-OO], [Lau-OO], [Tert-Bu-OO]} from a single multi-wavelength absorbance vector. Validate on a held-out test set + replicate the paper's Table 4 spiked-Lau-OO recovery experiment. README headline: "Beer-Lambert + NMF on simulated SOA UV-Vis spectra recovers individual peroxide species with X% accuracy at concentrations down to 1 μM." **Recruiter-legible as classical chemometrics; ~1-week project; no compute requirements; fully reproducible from paper alone.**
  - **Stretch — Method-selection recommender:** *"Decision-support tool for analytical chemists: given sample properties (solvent, peroxide types, thermal stability, time budget), recommend the optimal peroxide assay."* Tabular classifier using the paper's Table 5 + similar published comparison studies (Aimanant & Ziemann 2013, Wang et al. 2023 *Chem. Rev.*). Small dataset (~50 sample-condition × 4 assays = 200 rows) — better as **explainable rule-based system + decision tree** than deep learning. Streamlit demo. **Niche but a clean "applied chemometrics" addition.**
  - **Bayesian-kinetic-fit reframe (overlap with Article 1):** *"Bayesian rate-constant estimation for the NPBA peroxide assay — a PyMC port of the kinetic curve in Fig. 2."* The figure shows calibration slope vs. reaction time (50–300 min) for H₂O₂ + Tert-Bu-OOH; the NPBA reaction equilibrates faster for H₂O₂ but H₂O₂ slope decreases at long times (artifact of unknown origin). Fit a 2-rate-constant ODE (formation + decomposition for H₂O₂; formation only for Tert-Bu-OOH) via PyMC HMC. **Strong toolkit overlap with Article 1's autoxidation-kinetics framing** — could be packaged as an "Abbatt-lab kinetic toolkit" portfolio piece.
  - **Calibration-transfer reframe:** *"Domain adaptation between two analytical-chemistry labs — UCLM (Toledo) and U of T Chemistry (Toronto)."* The paper notes both labs ran calibrations and got "consistent within uncertainties" results despite different microwaves + spectrometers. Train a calibration model on one lab's data, apply to the other; quantify residual error. Maps to industry **"calibration transfer"** problem in process-analytical-technology (PAT) — pharma / food / environmental QC labs care about this. Limited by needing actual data from both labs (would require email-Wang-or-Baeza-Romero for the raw calibration tables).
