### Article 1: Wastewater Pretreatment, Attachment Material, and Inoculation Strategy in Algal Biofilm Cultivation (qPCR Quantification of *Chlorella vulgaris* and *Scenedesmus obliquus*)

- **Full citation:** Cheung, S. L., Short, S. M., & **Allen, D. G.** (2022). The influence of wastewater pretreatment, attachment material, and inoculation strategy on the growth of target algal species in cultivated biofilms. *Journal of Applied Phycology*, 34, 113–125. DOI: 10.1007/s10811-021-02637-3. Published online 30 November 2021. © Springer Nature B.V. 2021 (exclusive license, not CC BY). **Funded by NSERC Canada — Strategic Grants 463037-14 + Discovery Grants 2016-05524.** First-author Cheung supported by Ontario Graduate Scholarships, NSERC CGS, Queen Elizabeth II Graduate Scholarship in Science and Technology, and the Department of Chemical Engineering and Applied Chemistry.
- **Authorship signal:** Allen is **last + corresponding author**. Cheung (first author) is **his PhD student** in U of T ChemE. Short is from U of T Mississauga Biology (Department of Biology) — collaborator providing molecular-biology / qPCR expertise. **Strongest PI signal class — primary research from Allen's own lab.** Cheung has a previous foundational paper in this series (Cheung et al. 2020 *Bioresour. Technol.* — the qPCR primer development paper), suggesting a sustained PhD-thesis project.
- **Problem in plain English:** Microalgal biofilms are an attractive alternative to suspended algal cultures for biofuel / bioproduct production because they self-concentrate (~30% of conventional process cost is in harvesting / dewatering, and biofilms can be scraped off attachment surfaces) and they can be coupled with wastewater treatment for cheap nutrients. **But** non-sterile algal biofilm reactors get colonized by an unknown community of bacteria, cyanobacteria, protozoa, and competing eukaryotic algae — meaning the species you intended to grow may not be the species actually producing biomass. The paper applies TaqMan qPCR to quantify two specific target species (*Chlorella vulgaris* and *Scenedesmus obliquus*) in cultivated biofilms while systematically varying three growth factors: **(1)** wastewater pretreatment (introduces microbial community + EPS) vs. no pretreatment; **(2)** attachment material (porous cotton vs. non-porous polycarbonate); **(3)** inoculation strategy (algae added to suspension only, to coupon only, or both). The goal is to characterize how each factor influences both **biofilm biomass** and **species composition**.
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper.** Reframings are constructive but limited by tiny dataset:
  - (1) **Tabular regression** — predict {biofilm dry weight, log₁₀(*C. vulgaris* cells/m²), log₁₀(*S. obliquus* cells/m²), log₁₀(16S rRNA gene copies/m²)} from {coupon material ∈ {polycarbonate, cotton, cotton+CV inoc, cotton+SO inoc}, pretreatment ∈ {yes, no}, suspension inoculum ∈ {yes, no}, experimental trial}. Effective sample size ~24-32 rows after duplicate trials and triplicate coupons.
  - (2) **Meta-analysis reframe** — combine this paper's data with Irving & Allen 2011, Genin et al. 2014, Gross et al. 2013/2016, Ozkan & Berberoglu 2013a/b, etc., to build a literature-mined biofilm-growth dataset. Could plausibly assemble 100-500 rows of {algae species × material × pretreatment × dry weight}.
  - (3) **qPCR-Cq → cell abundance pipeline** — calibration curves at R² = 0.99, efficiency 94.5% are standard ML targets in qPCR diagnostics (industry-relevant: Bio-Rad, Thermo Fisher, Qiagen).
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: tabular categorical + numerical experimental factors → tabular continuous outputs
  - Shape / size if stated: 2 experiments × 2 duplicate trials × 2 reactor conditions (pretreated / non-pretreated) × 3-4 coupon types × 3 replicate coupons = **~24-32 biofilm samples** per output. Plus ~24 suspension samples (Day 0 + Day 10) for the suspension-composition analysis. **Tiny dataset by ML standards.**
  - Preprocessing in the paper: DNA extraction (Cheung et al. 2020 protocol); TaqMan qPCR with technical duplicates + 3 no-template controls + 4 inter-plate calibrators; log₁₀ transformation of cell-abundance and gene-abundance values; ANOVA + Tukey HSD with α = 0.05 + Bartlett's homogeneity-of-variance test + Shapiro-Wilks normality test
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: total biofilm dry weight (g per coupon, ~10⁻⁵ g precision), absolute *C. vulgaris* cell abundance (cells/m²), absolute *S. obliquus* cell abundance (cells/m²), 16S rRNA gene abundance (gene copies/m²)
  - Units / label space: continuous regression targets in log₁₀-space (cells/m² spans 10¹⁰ – 10¹³)
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. Statistical analysis only — ANOVA + Tukey HSD on factorial design. Calibration curves for qPCR are simple linear regression of Cq vs. log₁₀(gene copies).
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Custom-generated experimental dataset
  - Size: ~24-32 biofilm samples + suspension samples + qPCR calibration curve data
  - Source: U of T ChemE lab (parallel-plate air-lift reactors) + U of T Mississauga Biology lab (qPCR analysis); wastewater from Ashbridge's Bay Wastewater Treatment Plant (Toronto, March 4 2019 sampling); algal cultures from Canadian Phycological Culture Centre (CPCC, Waterloo) — *S. obliquus* CPCC 5, *C. vulgaris* CPCC 90
  - Public? **Partial** — "All data generated or analyzed during this study are included in this published article [and its supplementary information files]." **The Online Resource (Springer supplementary file) contains the raw Cq values + detection limits.**
  - Link: Springer journal article + supplementary info — https://doi.org/10.1007/s10811-021-02637-3 (institutional access likely required; not CC BY)
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **5.6× biofilm dry weight increase** with cotton coupons + wastewater pretreatment vs. polycarbonate non-pretreated (the strongest factorial effect)
  - 16S rRNA gene calibration curve: efficiency 94.5%, R² = 0.99
  - Detection limits: 24.6 cells/mL (*C. vulgaris*), 95.3 cells/mL (*S. obliquus*), 1.18 × 10⁵ genes/mL (16S rRNA)
  - **Counter-intuitive finding:** *S. obliquus* dominated planktonic suspensions; *C. vulgaris* dominated biofilms — and **inoculating cells directly into the cotton coupons had no observable effect on species composition**. This challenges the seed-into-substrate strategy commonly employed.
  - **Pretreatment increased *C. vulgaris* abundance in biofilms by 3.0–17.3× across all coupons**, while *S. obliquus* abundance was largely unchanged — wastewater drives species-specific recruitment, not just total biomass
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **NCBI Sequence Read Archive (SRA) — algal-biofilm metagenomics datasets** — keyword searches for "Chlorella vulgaris biofilm" / "algal biofilm 16S" return ~50-100 SRA studies. Match: **loose** (community composition rather than dry-weight regression). Access: **easy — direct download via SRA toolkit / fasterq-dump** — https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/sra
  - **Earth Microbiome Project (EMP)** — generic microbial-community 16S rRNA data from thousands of environments. Match: **very loose** (no algal-biofilm specificity). Access: **easy** — https://earthmicrobiome.org/
  - **MicrobiomeHD** — 28-study collection of curated 16S rRNA datasets. Match: **very loose**. Access: **easy** — https://github.com/cduvallet/microbiomeHD
  - **Cheung et al. 2020 *Bioresour. Technol.*** "Specific quantification of *Scenedesmus obliquus* and *Chlorella vulgaris* in mixed-species algal biofilms" — same lab, same students, foundational qPCR primer development paper. Match: **direct** (sister paper). Access: **medium** (paywalled but supplementary data likely free)
  - **Irving & Allen 2011 *Appl. Microbiol. Biotechnol.*** — same Allen-lab paper on biofilm species/material considerations; foundational reference for this work. Match: **close** (precursor study). Access: **medium** (paywalled)
  - **Open-Access Microalgal Cultivation Repositories (AlgaePARC, Wageningen)** — limited public availability. Match: **loose**. Access: **hard** (much of the data is consortium-internal)
  - **Literature-mined meta-analysis dataset** — assemble dry-weight + species-composition data from Irving & Allen 2011, Genin et al. 2014, Gross et al. 2013/2016, Christenson & Sims 2012, Kiperstok et al. 2017, Ye et al. 2018, etc. Could plausibly build 100-500 rows. Match: **direct, after construction**. Access: **medium** (manual extraction from paper tables)
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium.** Paper data is in Springer supplementary files (institutional access via U of T should work). NCBI SRA / EMP / MicrobiomeHD are direct downloads but only loosely match. The strongest reframe (literature-mined meta-analysis) requires manual data extraction from ~10-20 published papers.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:**
  - Already-known: pandas / numpy / sklearn linear regression / random forest / gradient boosting / matplotlib; standard scipy.stats for ANOVA and Tukey HSD
  - Light additions: **Biopython** for primer-sequence alignment (BLAST against NCBI) if you want to verify or extend the qPCR primer specificity analysis (the paper notes the 16S rRNA primers are 100% homologous with *C. vulgaris* and *S. obliquus* chloroplast 16S); **statsmodels** for explicit ANOVA + Tukey HSD post-hoc tests if you want to replicate the paper's statistical reporting
  - Optional heavy additions: **QIIME2 / DADA2** for full 16S rRNA sequencing analysis if you pursue the SRA-metagenomics reframe; **SHAP** / explainable-ML for the tabular regression reframe to identify which factor (material vs. pretreatment vs. inoculation) matters most
  - Domain reading: Cheung et al. 2020 *Bioresour. Technol.* (qPCR primer-development sister paper); Irving & Allen 2011 *Appl. Microbiol. Biotechnol.* (Allen-lab foundational paper on species/material in biofilms); Wang et al. 2018 *Renew. Sustain. Energy Rev.* (microalgal-attachment-systems review). **Domain accessible to chemistry undergrads with a few weeks of microbial-ecology + bioprocess-engineering reading.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project would look like:**
  - **Strongest single framing — Literature-mined meta-analysis of algal-biofilm growth factors:** *"Predicting algal-biofilm dry weight from material + pretreatment + species across 15 published studies — a tabular meta-regression with SHAP explanations."* Manually extract 100-500 rows from this paper + Irving & Allen 2011 + Genin et al. 2014 + Gross et al. 2013/2016 + Christenson & Sims 2012 + Kiperstok et al. 2017 + Ye et al. 2018 + ~10 other algal-biofilm cultivation papers. Train sklearn LightGBM / RandomForest, evaluate via 5-fold CV, surface SHAP feature importances ("does porosity matter more than wastewater? Does species choice matter more than material?"). README headline: *"Across 15 published algal-biofilm cultivation studies, [X] explains [Y]% of biomass variance — wastewater pretreatment ranks above attachment material."* **Recruiter-legible as 'applied ML for bioprocess engineering' / 'meta-analysis ML.'** ~2-3 week project; CPU-only; substantial citation-mining work but no compute requirements.
  - **Stretch — Tabular regression directly on the paper's dataset (24-32 rows):** *"Bayesian regression with strong priors on Allen et al.'s factorial algal-biofilm dataset."* Tiny n; PyMC Bayesian regression to handle small-sample uncertainty properly. Replicates the paper's findings (5.6× pretreatment+cotton effect; species-specific pretreatment-recruitment effect) within a probabilistic framework. Useful as a chemometrics demonstration but limited recruiter-legibility.
  - **qPCR-Cq → cell abundance ML pipeline:** *"Robust ML correction of qPCR calibration curves under contamination."* Train a regression model that predicts true cell abundance from raw Cq values + sample-specific covariates (DNA extraction yield, no-template-control noise, inter-plate variation). Industry-relevant for diagnostics labs (Bio-Rad, Thermo Fisher, Qiagen). **Limited by needing real qPCR data with ground-truth cell counts** — could synthesize from this paper's calibration curves + published diagnostic-ML studies.
  - **Sequence-based primer specificity reframe:** *"Predicting qPCR primer cross-reactivity from sequence homology — a Biopython + ML pipeline."* The paper notes 100% homology between 16S rRNA primers and *C. vulgaris* / *S. obliquus* chloroplast 16S — an unintended cross-reactivity. Train a small classifier on NCBI sequences to predict whether a primer pair will amplify a given organism's genome. Maps to **bioinformatics + applied ML** — recruiter-legible to diagnostic / pharma / agbio audiences.

**Article 1 verdict:** A **clean primary-research paper from Allen's own lab** (last + corresponding author, PhD student first author, NSERC-funded ChemE) that establishes Allen as a strong PI signal. The paper itself is bioprocess engineering with a small experimental dataset and statistical (not ML) analysis, but the **literature-mined meta-analysis reframe** is genuinely viable as a recruiter-legible portfolio project. **The dataset constraint (tiny n)** is the main limitation — the paper's own data only supports a small Bayesian regression demonstration, while the meta-analysis reframe requires substantial citation-mining effort. **Strongest portfolio angle: meta-regression across 15 published algal-biofilm studies + SHAP feature-importance explanations**, framed as "applied ML for bioprocess engineering." **Cross-survey synergy: Allen + Edwards** (both at U of T ChemE; both work on microbial-system / wastewater-treatment biology with molecular tools — Edwards on RdhA proteins for chlorinated-solvent dehalogenation, Allen on algal biofilms). **Allen + Mahadevan** (both at U of T ChemE; both use bioprocess-engineering frameworks). **Allen + Frank Gu** (both at U of T ChemE; both use materials-science / surface-chemistry approaches in environmental applications).

### Article 2: Methylated Hemoglobin from Waste Blood as a Bioflocculant for Pulp/Paper Biosludge Dewatering (NSERC CRD with industry consortium)

- **Full citation:** Ghazisaidi, H., Garcia, R. A., Tran, H., Yuan, R., & **Allen, D. G.** (2020). Enhancing biosludge dewaterability with hemoglobin from waste blood as a bioflocculant. *Polymers* (MDPI), 12(11), 2755, 12 pages. DOI: 10.3390/polym12112755. Published 22 November 2020. **CC BY 4.0 Open Access.** **Funded by NSERC Canada CRDPJ 500952-16** (Collaborative Research and Development grant) under the U of T research program *"Increasing Energy and Chemical Recovery Efficiency in the Kraft Process—II"* with a ~25-company pulp/paper industry consortium (Andritz, Arauco, Babcock & Wilcox, Canadian Kraft Paper Industries, Celulose Nipo-Brasileira, Clyde Industries, CMPC Celulosa, ERCO Worldwide, FITNIR, FPInnovations, Georgia-Pacific, International Paper, Irving Pulp & Paper, Metso KFS, Klabin, Meadow Lake Mechanical Pulp, Mercer, Noram, Rayonier AM, Resolute FP, Sappi, Södra, Stora Enso Research, Suzano, Valmet, WestRock).
- **Authorship signal:** Allen is **last + corresponding author**. Ghazisaidi (first author) is **his PhD student** in U of T ChemE, co-supervised with Honghi Tran (also U of T ChemE — kraft-process recovery / pulp-paper specialist). Garcia (USDA-ARS Eastern Regional Research Center, Wyndmoor, PA) is the **bioflocculant-chemistry collaborator** with multiple prior hemoglobin-flocculant papers (Garcia et al. 2017, 2020; Essandoh et al. 2017; Piazza et al. 2015). Yuan is a research assistant. **Same strongest-PI-signal class as Article 1.** **Tran-Allen co-supervision** confirms the multi-PI ChemE department culture.
- **Problem in plain English:** Pulp and paper mills generate huge amounts of biosludge (waste activated sludge) — Canadian P&P mills alone produce >1.5 million tons of dry solid sludge per year, and **management is up to 60% of the mill's wastewater treatment cost**. Sludge is 98%+ water held in a gel-like extracellular polymeric substance (EPS) network. Synthetic polymers (e.g., Zetag8165, a cationic polyacrylamide) are the industry standard flocculant, but they're petroleum-derived, expensive, dose-sensitive, and ecotoxic. **Hemoglobin from waste animal blood** (US: 2 million tons/year as meat-processing byproduct, >90% protein dry basis) is a candidate sustainable alternative. **Methylated hemoglobin (MeHb)** — produced by Fraenkel-Conrat-Olcott esterification of carboxylic acid groups with methanol — has its isoelectric point shifted from pH 6 (for native Hb) to pH 8, so at typical sludge pH (6.9 in this study) MeHb carries a net positive charge → can neutralize the negatively charged sludge particles → improved flocculation and dewatering. The paper systematically tests Hb vs. MeHb at 0, 2.5, 5, 7.5, 10 wt% dosages on biosludge from a Canadian sulfite/mechanical pulp mill, and characterizes the mechanism via 5 complementary measurements: cake dry solids (crown press), zeta potential, particle size distribution, stratified EPS protein + polysaccharide content (3 layers), DSC-measured bound water content.
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper.** Reframings:
  - (1) **Tabular dose-response regression** — predict {dry solids %, zeta potential, TB-EPS protein, TB-EPS polysaccharide, bound water %} from {protein type ∈ {Hb, MeHb}, dosage ∈ {0, 2.5, 5, 7.5, 10}}. Tiny n (~10-20 rows after replicates).
  - (2) **Literature-mined meta-analysis of bioflocculants** — combine this paper with Bonilla & Allen 2018, Piazza et al. 2015, Essandoh et al. 2017, Garcia et al. 2017/2020, Mowla, Tran, Allen 2013 (review), Bo et al. 2012, Li et al. 2014, etc. → ~50-200 row tabular dataset of {flocculant type × dosage × sludge/suspension type × dewaterability metric}. **Same reframing pattern as Article 1's meta-analysis** — could be packaged as a unified Allen-lab portfolio angle.
  - (3) **Bayesian optimization for flocculant formulation** — given measured dose-response curves (Figure 2 in the paper), train a Gaussian process surrogate and use BO to suggest next-best dosage / pH / protein-type combinations. Industry use case: pharma formulation, food science.
  - (4) **DSC bound-water curve fitting** — paper uses DSC enthalpy decomposition; ML reframe via PINN or sklearn curve fitting on raw thermograms. Adjacent to Abbatt Article 3 mixed-peroxide spectral deconvolution.
  - (5) **Multi-modal mechanism elucidation** — unsupervised PCA / NMF clustering on the joint {zeta, particle size, EPS-PS, EPS-PN, bound water} measurement vector across dosages → identify which mechanisms (charge neutralization vs. EPS solubilization vs. bound-water release) dominate at which dosage.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: tabular categorical (protein type) + numerical (dosage wt%) → tabular continuous outputs (5 measurement modalities)
  - Shape / size if stated: 5 dosages × 2 protein types × ~3 replicates per condition = **~30 measurements per output modality, but only ~10 distinct {protein, dosage} conditions.** Plus reference Zetag8165 control. **Tiny dataset by ML standards.**
  - Preprocessing: methylation of bovine hemoglobin via Fraenkel-Conrat method (3% w/v Hb in methanol + 0.8 mol/L HCl, shake 48 h, centrifuge 10,000×g for 15 min, dialyze through 6000-8000 MWCO membrane); EPS stratification via 3-step centrifugation (2000×g supernatant = soluble EPS, 5000×g supernatant = LB-EPS, ultrasound-treated 20,000×g supernatant = TB-EPS); modified Lowry for protein, Anthrone for polysaccharide; DSC at 5°C/min from 25°C to -60°C and back
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: cake dry solids content (wt%, range 10-15.3%), zeta potential (mV, range -34.3 to -19.0), particle size distribution mean (μm, ~55-60 μm range), EPS-PN at 3 layers (mg/g DS), EPS-PS at 3 layers (mg/g DS), bound water content (wt%, range 43-52%)
  - Units / label space: continuous regression targets, all standard SI/engineering units
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. Statistical analysis only — ANOVA via Minitab Software with α = 0.05.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Custom-generated experimental dataset
  - Size: ~30 measurements per output modality across 10 distinct conditions, all from a single biosludge batch (TS = 15.1 ± 0.2 g/L)
  - Source: Canadian sulfite/mechanical pulp mill secondary clarifier; bovine hemoglobin from Sigma-Aldrich (lyophilized); methylation reagents from Sigma-Aldrich; Zetag8165 reference
  - Public? **No** — paper does not include a data-availability statement; results presented in figures only; no SI data file. (CC BY license permits digitizing the figures using WebPlotDigitizer or similar, but the underlying tabulated data is not directly provided.)
  - Link: MDPI article DOI: https://doi.org/10.3390/polym12112755 (CC BY — open access; figures can be digitized for ML reframes)
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **Cake dry solids: 10.2 ± 0.3 wt% → 15.0 ± 1.0 wt% with 10% MeHb** (47% relative increase; comparable to commercial 1% Zetag8165 at ~15.7 wt%, but MeHb requires 10× higher dose so is not yet cost-competitive)
  - Zeta potential: −34.3 ± 3.2 mV → −19.0 ± 2.1 mV (clear charge-neutralization signature)
  - TB-EPS protein: 34 → 23.3 mg/g DS; TB-EPS polysaccharide: 15.6 → 10.8 mg/g DS (EPS solubilization confirmed)
  - Bound water: 52% → 43% (releasing 10% of the previously-bound water into the free fraction is the key dewaterability gain)
  - Particle size distribution: minimal change → suggests charge-neutralization, NOT bridging-flocculation, is the dominant mechanism
  - **Native (non-methylated) Hb: no dewaterability improvement at any dosage** — confirms methylation is essential for the application
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Literature-mined meta-analysis dataset across published bioflocculant studies** — assemble dewaterability + dosage + flocculant-type data from: Bonilla & Allen 2018 *Sep. Purif. Technol.*; Piazza, Lora & Garcia 2015 *J. Chem. Technol. Biotechnol.*; Essandoh, Garcia & Strahan 2017 *J. Chem. Technol. Biotechnol.*; Garcia, Bumanlag & Piazza 2017 *J. Sci. Food Agric.*; Garcia, Qi, Essandoh & Bumanlag 2020 *J. Dispers. Sci. Technol.*; Mowla, Tran & Allen 2013 *Biomass Bioenergy* (review — Allen-lab); Bo et al. 2012 *Bioresour. Technol.*; Li et al. 2014 *Bioresour. Technol.*; Bratby 2006 *Coagulation and Flocculation* (textbook). Could plausibly assemble 50-200 rows. Match: **direct, after construction**. Access: **medium** (manual extraction from paper figures + tables). **Same reframing pattern as Article 1's algal-biofilm meta-analysis — could be packaged as a unified Allen-lab portfolio piece.**
  - **EPA WAS (Waste Activated Sludge) operational datasets** — EPA's Toxic Release Inventory + selected open-data WWTP performance datasets. Match: **loose** (operational performance, not bench-scale flocculant testing). Access: **easy** — https://www.epa.gov/toxics-release-inventory-tri-program
  - **Kaggle "Wastewater Treatment" datasets** — assorted WWTP operational and effluent-quality datasets. Match: **loose**. Access: **easy** — https://www.kaggle.com/datasets?search=wastewater
  - **UCI ML Repository — Water Treatment Plant** — classical 38-variable monthly WWTP dataset. Match: **very loose** (not biosludge-specific). Access: **easy** — https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/106/water+treatment+plant
  - **Bonilla & Allen 2018 *Sep. Purif. Technol.*** — same Allen-lab predecessor paper on cationic-protein bioflocculants comparing synthetic polymers, surfactants, and proteins. Match: **direct** (sister paper). Access: **medium** (paywalled Elsevier)
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Hard.** Data not in SI; figures only. The CC BY license permits digitizing via WebPlotDigitizer (~10-15 min per figure, manual). Strongest reframe (literature-mined meta-analysis) requires manual extraction across ~10-20 papers. **Substantially harder than Article 1** — Article 1 had data in Springer SI files; Article 2 has nothing released except the figures.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:**
  - Already-known: pandas / numpy / sklearn linear regression / random forest / gradient boosting / scipy.stats / statsmodels — all already in toolkit
  - Light additions: **WebPlotDigitizer** (free, browser-based — https://automeris.io/WebPlotDigitizer/) for figure digitization in the meta-analysis reframe; **scikit-optimize** or **GPyTorch** for the Bayesian-optimization reframe; **scipy.optimize.curve_fit** for the DSC curve-fitting reframe
  - No new ecosystem lift beyond Article 1
  - Domain reading: Mowla, Tran, Allen 2013 *Biomass Bioenergy* (Allen-lab review on biosludge properties — the foundational reference for this thread); Bonilla & Allen 2018 (Allen-lab predecessor paper); Piazza & Garcia 2016 *Biobased Flocculants Derived from Animal Processing Protein* (Elsevier book — the field overview). **Domain accessible to chemistry undergrads with a few weeks of colloid-chemistry + bioprocess-engineering reading.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project would look like:**
  - **Strongest single framing — Unified Allen-lab meta-analysis (Article 1 + Article 2 combined):** *"Predicting bioprocess engineering outcomes from tabular factorial designs across 25+ published Allen-lab and adjacent studies — a unified meta-regression on algal-biofilm dry weight + biosludge dewaterability."* Combine literature-mined data from this paper, Article 1, Cheung et al. 2020, Irving & Allen 2011, Mowla/Tran/Allen 2013, Bonilla & Allen 2018, Garcia / Piazza / Essandoh hemoglobin papers, Genin et al. 2014, Gross et al. 2013/2016, etc. Train sklearn LightGBM / RandomForest with SHAP explanations. README headline: *"Across 25 published bioprocess-engineering studies, [X] explains [Y]% of dewaterability / biofilm-biomass variance — methylation + dosage + EPS structure rank above material choice."* **Strongest cross-paper synergy — packaged as a single multi-paper portfolio piece this is more recruiter-legible than two separate small projects.** ~3-week project; CPU-only.
  - **Stretch — Bayesian optimization for flocculant formulation:** *"Gaussian-process Bayesian optimization for biosludge dewatering — replaying Allen et al. 2020 with sequential-design pruning."* Train a GP surrogate on the paper's dose-response curves; simulate sequential BO trials that aim to find optimum dosage in fewer experiments than the original grid. Industry-relevant for pharma formulation, food science, materials chemistry. ~1-2 week project; small but clean.

### Article 3: TaqMan qPCR Primer Development for Species-Specific Quantification of *S. obliquus* and *C. vulgaris* via the psbA Chloroplast Gene (Methodological-Foundation Sister Paper to Article 1)

- **Full citation:** Cheung, S. L., **Allen, D. G.**, & Short, S. M. (2020). Specific quantification of *Scenedesmus obliquus* and *Chlorella vulgaris* in mixed-species algal biofilms. *Bioresource Technology*, 295, 122251, 9 pages. DOI: 10.1016/j.biortech.2019.122251. Published online 09 October 2019. © Elsevier (paywalled). **Funded by NSERC Canada Strategic Grants program 463037-14 (the same Strategic grant as Article 1).** First-author Cheung supported by the same award stack as Article 1 (Ontario Graduate Scholarships, NSERC CGS, Queen Elizabeth II Graduate Scholarship in Science and Technology, and ChemE @ U of T).
- **Authorship caveat:** Allen is **MIDDLE author (2nd of 3), NOT corresponding.** **Steven M. Short (U of T Mississauga, Department of Biology) is corresponding author** — Short provides the molecular-biology / qPCR expertise as the senior PI on the methodological-development side; Allen is the bioprocess-engineering co-supervisor as in Articles 1 and 2. Cheung (first author) is **the same PhD student who first-authored Article 1**, working under Allen + Short co-supervision. **This is the methodological-foundation paper for Article 1** (which used the primers developed here) — so although the authorship signal places Short as senior PI on Article 3, the lab-signal weight for Allen remains meaningful because (a) it's the same PhD-thesis project as Article 1, (b) it's the same NSERC Strategic grant, and (c) Allen is the ChemE-side co-supervisor on a sustained Cheung-Allen-Short collaboration. **Lab-signal interpretation:** treat Article 3 as "Allen-affiliated methodological development" — weaker than Articles 1 and 2 (both Allen-last-corresponding) but stronger than a peripheral collaboration.
- **Problem in plain English:** Algal biofilms grown for biofuel production are inherently mixed-species communities — even when you inoculate a target species like *Chlorella vulgaris* or *Scenedesmus obliquus*, the non-sterile reactor accumulates contaminating bacteria, cyanobacteria, heterotrophs, and competing algae. To know which species are actually growing in a given biofilm, you need a **species-specific cell-counting method**. Microscopy can't reliably distinguish morphologically similar algae embedded in a biofilm matrix; flow cytometry can't either; spectrophotometry only reads bulk chlorophyll. **Quantitative PCR can** — it amplifies a species-specific gene fragment and reports cell abundance proportional to fluorescence cycle threshold. But typical qPCR primer targets (16S rRNA for prokaryotes, 18S rRNA for eukaryotes) have variable copy numbers per cell across algae, breaking the cell-abundance proportionality. The paper proposes a better target: **psbA, a chloroplast gene encoding photosystem II protein D1**, which is single-copy per chloroplast and has high inter-species sequence variability. Paper develops + validates two TaqMan qPCR assays (one for *S. obliquus*, one for *C. vulgaris*), publishes the primer/probe sequences, generates plasmid standard curves and cell calibration curves, tests cross-reactivity, and demonstrates accurate species-level quantification in spike-in biofilm samples.
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper, but has the clearest reframing of the three Allen articles** because it operates on public GenBank sequence data:
  - (1) **Sequence-based primer specificity prediction** — Train a neural network (CNN, transformer, or DNABERT-style) on (primer + non-target sequence) pairs to predict cross-reactivity probability or amplification efficiency. Real ML problem with real industry use case (Bio-Rad, Thermo Fisher, IDT DNA, Qiagen). **Training data is directly downloadable from NCBI GenBank** — no email-the-author barrier.
  - (2) **psbA-based phylogenetic classifier** — Train a sequence-to-species classifier on the GenBank psbA accessions across Chlorophyta (~2000+ sequences). Lightweight transformer or CNN, fits on free Colab. Standard bioinformatics task with a clean supervised setup.
  - (3) **Standard curve calibration regression with batch effects** — Predict cell abundance from Cq values + sample-specific covariates using a regression model. Industry-relevant for qPCR diagnostic labs.
  - (4) **Cross-paper synergy: Article 1 + Article 3 unified portfolio** — Article 1 USES these primers; Article 3 DEVELOPS them. Together they form a self-contained "Allen-lab ML for algal biofilms" portfolio piece: train sequence-specificity classifier (Article 3 data) → apply primers in Article 1's biofilm system → predict species composition under varying experimental conditions (Article 1 data). **Strongest cross-Allen-article synergy** of the three.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: DNA sequence strings (psbA amplicons), one-hot encoded over 4-letter alphabet (A, C, G, T) — for the sequence-based ML reframes
  - Shape / size if stated: 5 oligonucleotides per assay (forward, reverse, forward/reverse for nested, probe), each 17-27 bp; full psbA amplicons 107-317 bp; thousands of psbA accessions in GenBank for the broader training corpus. Plasmid abundance range 3.2 × 10⁰ to 3.2 × 10⁷ copies per reaction; cell calibration range 10² to 10⁶ cells; Cq values 10-40
  - Preprocessing: PCR amplification + Sanger sequencing of psbA fragments at The Centre for Applied Genomics (TCAG), Hospital for Sick Children, Toronto; sequence manipulation via BioEdit v.7.2.5; BLAST alignment against GenBank for cross-reactivity check; ClustalW for inter-species alignment; Beacon Designer 7.0 for primer/probe candidate generation; ≥4 mismatches required between target primer and alternate algal species
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts (in the paper): primer/probe sequences (output of design phase); standard-curve slope, intercept, R², efficiency (output of validation phase); cell abundance (output of application phase)
  - Units / label space: continuous Cq values → log10(plasmid copies) or log10(cells) via standard curve / cell calibration curve regression
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. Primer design via Beacon Designer 7.0 (heuristic + thermodynamic scoring). Standard-curve regression: simple linear regression of Cq vs. log10(plasmid copies). Cell calibration: simple linear regression of log10(gene copies) vs. log10(cells).
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Custom-generated PCR + Sanger sequencing data + qPCR calibration data + biofilm spike-in test data
  - Size: 5 designed primers + 5 designed probes (Table 1) for the two algal species; 4-replicate quadruplicate standard curves spanning 8 orders of magnitude; 12 spike-in biofilm samples for recovery test; ~2000+ public psbA accessions in NCBI GenBank that could be tapped for training a broader ML model
  - Source: Algal cultures from Canadian Phycological Culture Centre (CPCC, Waterloo) — *S. obliquus* CPCC 5, *C. vulgaris* CPCC 90; algal biofilms from a vertical parallel-plate airlift photobioreactor (Genin et al. 2014); Sanger sequencing at TCAG Toronto; **public GenBank psbA accessions: DQ396875.1 (*S. obliquus* strain UTEX 393), AB001684.1 (*Chlorella vulgaris* C-27)**
  - Public? **Partial** — paper data is in the figures (standard curves, cross-reactivity, calibration, biofilm spike-in); primer/probe sequences are in Table 1 (directly usable); the broader **GenBank psbA database is fully public and directly downloadable**
  - Link: Article DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.biortech.2019.122251 (paywalled Elsevier); GenBank Entrez API: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/genbank/ (free programmatic access via Biopython)
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - *S. obliquus* standard curve: R²=0.99, efficiency 92.4%, slope -3.52 (3.2×10⁰ to 3.2×10⁷ copies/reaction)
  - *C. vulgaris* standard curve: R²=0.99, efficiency 96.6%, slope -3.41 (4.94×10⁰ to 4.94×10⁷ copies/reaction)
  - Cell calibration: slope 0.98 (*C. vulgaris*), 1.11 (*S. obliquus*); R²=0.95 for both — **slopes near 1.0 confirm one psbA copy per cell** (single chloroplast assumption holds)
  - Cross-reactivity: *S. obliquus* assay = NO cross-reactivity with *C. vulgaris* plasmids; *C. vulgaris* assay = minimal cross-reactivity (1 in 10⁵) with *S. obliquus* plasmids
  - Biofilm spike-in recovery: added 10⁷ cells/mL → recovered 2.1 × 10⁷ ± 3.5 × 10⁶ (*S. obliquus*) and 1.0 × 10⁷ ± 4.2 × 10⁵ (*C. vulgaris*) — **demonstrates robust species-level quantification in a complex biofilm matrix**
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **NCBI GenBank — psbA sequence corpus** — **direct public dataset**, ~2000+ psbA accessions across Chlorophyta + Cyanobacteria. Match: **direct** (the paper's primary reference data). Access: **easy** — free programmatic access via Biopython Entrez API. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/nucleotide/. **The strongest reframing path**.
  - **NCBI BLAST+ tool (programmatic)** — for batched cross-reactivity checks on candidate primer sequences. Match: **direct** (the paper's validation methodology). Access: **easy** — free download from NCBI; Biopython has wrappers. https://blast.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Blast.cgi
  - **PrimerBank / RTPrimerDB / qPrimerDB** — published qPCR primer databases for various genes / organisms. Match: **loose** (mostly mammalian / pathogen primers, not algal). Access: **easy** — http://primerbank.org/ ; https://medgen.ugent.be/rtprimerdb/
  - **DNABERT / Nucleotide Transformer** — pretrained DNA language models from HuggingFace (Ji et al. 2021; Dalla-Torre et al. 2023). Match: **adjacent** (provides foundation for primer-specificity classifier). Access: **easy** — HuggingFace models. https://huggingface.co/zhihan1996/DNA_bert_6 ; https://huggingface.co/InstaDeepAI/nucleotide_transformer_v2_500m_multi_species
  - **SILVA rRNA database** — gold-standard 16S/18S/ITS rRNA sequence database with ~9 million sequences. Match: **loose** (rRNA, not psbA, but adjacent for the broader phylogenetic-classifier reframe). Access: **easy** — https://www.arb-silva.de/
  - **Greengenes / RefSeq** — additional public sequence databases for the broader phylogenetic-classifier reframe. Access: **easy**.
  - **Cheung et al. 2022 (Article 1, *J. Appl. Phycol.*)** — direct downstream paper that USES these primers; provides paired species-abundance + experimental-condition data. Match: **direct** (sister paper). Access: **medium** (Springer SI files via institutional access).
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium-easy.** Paper's own data is in figures + Table 1 (directly usable for primer/probe sequences). **GenBank psbA + BLAST + DNABERT are all directly public** for the strongest reframe (sequence-based primer specificity prediction). **This is the most accessible of the three Allen articles** because the dominant reframe operates on public NCBI data, not on proprietary biosludge or biofilm experimental measurements.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:**
  - Already-known: pandas / numpy / sklearn / scipy.stats / matplotlib
  - **Key new additions: Biopython** (Entrez API, BLAST wrappers, sequence alignment via Bio.Align) — well-documented, widely-taught in bioinformatics intro courses; **HuggingFace transformers** (already in many ML curricula); **DNABERT** or **Nucleotide Transformer** for the sequence-based classifier reframe — pretrained models, just need fine-tuning with PyTorch
  - Optional advanced: **EMBOSS** (sequence-analysis suite) for batched alignment + cross-reactivity checks; **scikit-bio** (Bio-statistics in Python) for diversity metrics
  - **Moderate new ecosystem lift** (~1-2 weeks of bioinformatics ramp) — but well-supported by the existing Python ML toolchain, no R or proprietary software required
  - Domain reading: Bustin et al. 2009 *MIQE Guidelines* (qPCR best practices); Ji et al. 2021 *Bioinformatics* on DNABERT; Untergasser et al. 2012 *Nucl. Acids Res.* on Primer3 (cited in this paper); Whelan, Russell & Whelan 2003 *J. Immunol. Meth.* on absolute qPCR quantification (cited in this paper). **Domain accessible to chemistry undergrads with a few weeks of bioinformatics ramp.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project would look like:**
  - **Strongest single framing — Sequence-based primer specificity classifier:** *"Predicting qPCR primer cross-reactivity from sequence homology — a Biopython + DNABERT pipeline trained on GenBank psbA + Cheung et al. 2020 ground-truth data."* Train a binary classifier (cross-reactive: yes/no) on (primer-sequence, non-target-sequence) pairs. Ground-truth labels from Cheung's paper + BLAST-derived synthetic positives/negatives. Compare a hand-crafted-feature sklearn baseline (mismatch count, GC content, melting temperature) against a DNABERT fine-tuning baseline. README headline: *"DNABERT-fine-tuned cross-reactivity classifier achieves [X]% accuracy on held-out qPCR primer pairs."* **Recruiter-legible to bioinformatics / diagnostic / pharma audiences** (Bio-Rad, Thermo Fisher, IDT DNA, Qiagen, 10X Genomics). ~3-week project; trains in hours on free Colab; uses fully public GenBank data.
  - **Stretch — psbA-based phylogenetic classifier:** *"Predicting algal species identity from psbA sequence fragments — a CNN + transformer benchmark on GenBank Chlorophyta accessions."* Download ~2000+ psbA accessions across Chlorophyta + Cyanobacteria; train species-classifier; benchmark CNN vs. transformer vs. simple k-mer-based sklearn baseline. Adjacent to standard environmental-DNA / metabarcoding tasks.
  - **Cross-paper unified portfolio (Article 1 + Article 3):** *"From qPCR primer design to algal-biofilm species composition prediction — an end-to-end Allen-lab ML pipeline."* Step 1: Article 3 → train sequence-specificity classifier on psbA + GenBank. Step 2: Article 1 → use validated primers' qPCR-derived species abundances as the supervised target for predicting biofilm composition from {wastewater pretreatment, attachment material, inoculation strategy}. Single notebook chains both papers. **Strongest single Allen-anchored portfolio piece** — engages 2 of 3 Allen papers + uses fully public GenBank data + maps to a real bioprocess-engineering ML problem.
  - **qPCR Cq → cell abundance ML pipeline with batch correction:** *"Robust ML correction of qPCR calibration curves under inter-plate variation."* Train a regression model on Cq values + sample-specific covariates (DNA extraction yield, no-template-control noise, inter-plate variation as batch ID). Industry use case: diagnostic labs running thousands of qPCR plates per day need batch-effect correction.

## Maria Dittrich

**Affiliation:** University of Toronto **Scarborough** (UTSC), **Department of Physical & Environmental Sciences**, **Biogeochemistry Group** (1065 Military Trail, Toronto, ON M1C 1A4). **Different campus from St. George** (where most surveyed professors are based). UTSC is a separate suburban campus with its own faculty hiring + graduate admissions; programs are coordinated with St. George via U of T Graduate Studies.

**Lab focus (one sentence):** **Inferred** from Article 1: biogeochemistry + surface chemistry of microbial cells + nanoparticles + biomineralization, with applications spanning antibiofilm nanotechnology, cyanobacterial water-quality management, and CaCO₃ biomineralization on cyanobacterial surfaces (per cited Liang et al. 2013 + Paulo et al. 2018 + Dittrich & Sibler 2005 papers). **Verify with additional articles before treating this as authoritative.**

**Lab webpage / Scholar:** Not yet provided. Email per Article 1: mdittrich@utsc.utoronto.ca. Search U of T Scarborough Department of Physical & Environmental Sciences faculty directory + Google Scholar for "Maria Dittrich biogeochemistry."

### Article 1: Cationized Phytoglycogen Nanoparticles as Environmentally-Friendly Antibiofilm Agents Against Cyanobacteria

- **Full citation:** Tavafoghi, M., Garg, S., Korenevski, A., & **Dittrich, M.** (2021). Environmentally friendly antibiofilm strategy based on cationized phytoglycogen nanoparticles. *Colloids and Surfaces B: Biointerfaces*, 207, 111975, 11 pages. DOI: 10.1016/j.colsurfb.2021.111975. Published online 21 July 2021. © Elsevier (paywalled). **Funded by Mitacs-Elevate Graduate Research Internship Program** (postdoctoral fellowship for Tavafoghi); **Dittrich supported by NSERC Discovery Grant + Canada Foundation for Innovation + Ontario Research Fund Leaders Opportunity Fund (Grant 22404).**
- **Authorship signal:** Dittrich is **last + corresponding author**. **Tavafoghi (first author) is a postdoc in Dittrich's group** (now at UCLA Bioengineering per affiliation note). **Korenevski is from Mirexus Biotechnologies Inc.** (Guelph, ON) — the industry collaborator providing the PhytoSpherix™ phytoglycogen nanoparticle product, with declared financial interest (employee + patent application US5656591A) and Mitacs program sponsorship. Garg is a UTSC researcher. **Strongest-PI-signal class** with industry-collaboration overlay. **Mirexus is a real Canadian biotech** spun out of University of Guelph polymer chemistry research; suggests a master's student in Dittrich's lab could plausibly access industry placements via Mitacs internships.
- **Problem in plain English:** Cyanobacterial biofilms cause real-world problems in drinking water reservoirs, water treatment plants, and cooling systems — operational fouling, microbially-induced corrosion, taste/odor/color issues, and (worst case) cyanotoxin release that causes skin irritation, allergic responses, diarrhea, liver/kidney damage on contact or ingestion. The genus *Synechococcus* is particularly prevalent and projected to rise to 14% relative abundance under global warming. Antibiotics are increasingly ineffective due to biofilm tolerance; metallic antibacterial nanoparticles (silver, copper) are environmentally toxic and expensive. **Phytoglycogen** — a multibranched polysaccharide of glucose extracted from non-genetically-modified corn (Mirexus PhytoSpherix™ product) — is cost-effective, water-dispersible, edible-grade non-toxic, but in its native form (PhX) has only hydroxyl surface groups → no antibiofilm activity. **Cationization** via reaction with glycidyltrimethylammonium chloride (GTAC) introduces quaternary ammonium (QA) cations on the polysaccharide surface (degree of substitution DS=0.88) → **cationic PhXC** with strongly positive zeta potential (~+30 mV) → strong electrostatic binding to negatively-charged cyanobacterial surfaces (dominated by carboxylic + phosphoric groups) → blocks cell-cell + cell-substrate adhesion → prevents biofilm formation, eradicates preformed biofilms, and inhibits planktonic growth at concentrations 100-1000× lower than required against pathogenic bacteria like *P. aeruginosa*. Paper systematically characterizes the surface interactions (titration, zeta potential, FTIR, TEM, anthrone adsorption) and quantifies efficacy (MIC, BPC, BIC).
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper.** Reframings:
  - (1) **Tabular dose-response regression** — predict {planktonic OD650, biofilm OD650, cell aggregation %, zeta potential, EPS removal, viable cell %} from {nanoparticle type ∈ {PhX, PhXC}, concentration ∈ {0, 0.1, 0.2, 0.4, 0.8, 1.6, 3.2, 6.4, 12.8, 25.6, 51.2, 102.4, 204.8, 409.6} μg/mL, biofilm stage ∈ {planktonic, prevention, eradication}, time}. ~80-100 distinct conditions across the paper. Modest n; usable for sklearn / Bayesian regression with PyMC.
  - (2) **Surface-property → antibacterial-efficacy QSAR** — Quantitative Structure-Activity Relationship: given polysaccharide nanoparticle physicochemical properties (zeta potential, particle size, degree of substitution, surface functional group composition from titration) → predict MIC / BPC / BIC against various bacterial species. Combine with literature meta-analysis across published nanoparticle-antibacterial studies (Chronopoulou et al. 2016, Ong et al. 2019, Nomura et al. 2016, Shao et al. 2015, Goodman et al. 2004, Rendueles et al. 2013, Kalishwaralal et al. 2010, Hajipour et al. 2012, etc.). Could plausibly assemble 50-200 row tabular dataset.
  - (3) **Potentiometric titration curve fitting / chemometrics** — paper uses **Linear Programming Method (LPM)** for charge-excess vs pH curve decomposition into ligand concentrations + pKa values (Eq. 1). Could reframe as ML: train Gaussian process or neural network on titration curves → predict surface ligand composition. **Standard chemometrics task; sklearn-friendly via scipy.optimize.linprog.**
  - (4) **Cross-survey synergy with Allen** — Allen + Dittrich both work on cyanobacteria-related biofilms but with **complementary methodologies**: Allen (St. George ChemE) uses qPCR + bioprocess engineering on algal biofilms; Dittrich (UTSC Biogeochemistry) uses surface chemistry + nanoparticle antibiofilm characterization on cyanobacterial biofilms. **A unified portfolio piece** could combine Allen Article 1's biofilm-cultivation factorial design with Dittrich Article 1's PhXC-treatment dose-response → predict species-specific biofilm response to nanoparticle treatment.
  - (5) **Bayesian optimization for nanoparticle formulation** — given measured efficacy at varying degrees of substitution + dosages → use BO to suggest next-best PhXC variants (DS variations, alternative cationizing agents, hybrid Mirexus formulations). Industry-relevant for diagnostic / antimicrobial / coating R&D.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: tabular categorical + numerical experimental factors (nanoparticle type, concentration, biofilm stage, time) → tabular continuous outputs (multi-modal: OD, ZP, aggregation %, viable cells, ligand-pKa)
  - Shape / size if stated: 14 PhX/PhXC concentrations × 2 nanoparticle types × 3 stages (planktonic/prevention/eradication) × ~3 replicates ≈ ~250 measurement events; titration curves at pH 4-10.5 with 0.2 pH-unit intervals × 3 replicates × 5 sample types (PhX, PhXC, LS, LS/PhX, LS/PhXC) ≈ ~500 (pH, charge-excess) datapoints; ZP measurements 5 reps × 6+ pH values × 5 samples ≈ ~150 ZP values
  - Preprocessing: phytoglycogen extraction from frozen sweet corn via 12,000×g centrifugation + 500 kDa cross-flow filtration + ethanol precipitation + 50°C oven drying + 45-mesh milling (~70 nm DLS particles); cationization via GTAC + catalytic NaOH (DS=0.88 by NMR; ~55 nm particles); cyanobacteria *Synechococcus* LS0427 from Lake Superior (oligotrophic, ~10 nM dissolved P) batch-cultured in modified Z8 medium at 21°C under 32 μE/m²/s; potentiometric titration with N₂-degassed 0.01 M NaNO₃ + 0.1 M NaOH titrant; LPM modeling via Eq. 1 (charge excess = sum over n monoprotic ligands of K_a × L_T / (K_a + [H+])); ANOVA + Tukey HSD with α=0.05
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: ligand concentrations L_T (mmol/g) + pKa values for surface functional groups (carboxylate, phosphoric, amine, hydroxyl); zeta potential (mV); MIC/BPC/BIC (μg/mL); biofilm OD; cell aggregation % (Eq. 2)
  - Units / label space: continuous regression targets in standard physicochemical units
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. Linear Programming Method (LPM, Eq. 1) for titration-curve decomposition. Standard linear regression for calibration curves. ANOVA + Tukey HSD for statistical comparisons.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Custom-generated experimental dataset
  - Size: titration data (~500 datapoints), ZP (~150 values), MIC/BPC/BIC (~40 dose-response measurements per stage), TEM images (~5), FTIR spectra (~5)
  - Source: UTSC Biogeochemistry lab + Mirexus Biotechnologies (Guelph) for the PhytoSpherix™ phytoglycogen nanoparticle product; *Synechococcus* LS0427 from Environment and Climate Change Canada (Dr. S.A. Watson, personal communication; isolated from Lake Superior 47°20′N, 89°54′W); TEM at OCCAM Toronto; ZP equipment from Shoichet lab at U of T Institute of Biomaterials and Biomedical Engineering
  - Public? **Partial** — paper data is in figures + Supplementary Information (Figs. S1-S3, Table S1); raw data files not released. Mirexus PhytoSpherix product has limited public technical specs (mostly in Mirexus patents)
  - Link: Article DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.colsurfb.2021.111975 (Elsevier, paywalled); SI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.colsurfb.2021.111975 supplementary; Mirexus patents: US5656591A, WO2017177342A1
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **MIC (planktonic) = 3.2 μg/mL PhXC** — within range of strong antibiotics (penicillin, ciprofloxacin, tylosin) at 4-5 μg/mL against cyanobacteria, and **100-1000× lower** than PhXC's MIC against pathogens (300-2500 μg/mL for *P. aeruginosa*, *E. coli*)
  - **BPC (biofilm prevention) = 6.4 μg/mL PhXC** — 95% biofilm inhibition; cell viability drops to 40%
  - **BIC (biofilm eradication) = 102.4 μg/mL PhXC** — 94% eradication of preformed biofilm; cell viability remains 90% on residual biofilm (suggesting EPS-buffered cell protection)
  - **Native PhX (no cationization)**: ~30% inhibition at high concentrations only; no biofilm prevention or eradication. **Cationization is essential.**
  - Surface characterization: PhX/PhXC = ~98% hydroxyl groups (pKa~10) + ~1% carboxylate + ~1% phosphoric (PhXC additionally has non-titratable QA cations from GTAC); cyanobacterial surface = ~35% carboxylate + ~35% phosphoric + ~10% amine + ~20% hydroxyl
  - **Counter-intuitive non-linear dose-response in biofilm prevention**: PhXC at low dosages (0.1-0.8 μg/mL) **promotes** biofilm formation (via cell aggregation), but at ≥6.4 μg/mL inhibits it (via cytotoxicity)
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Literature-mined nanoparticle-antibacterial QSAR dataset** — assemble MIC/BPC/BIC + nanoparticle physicochemical properties from Chronopoulou et al. 2016 *J. Nanopart. Res.* (positive polymeric NPs vs *P. aeruginosa* biofilms), Ong et al. 2019 *PLoS One* (chitosan-propolis NPs vs *S. epidermidis*), Nomura et al. 2016 *J. Nanopart. Res.* (polystyrene NPs vs *Pseudomonas*), Shao et al. 2015 *Cell Prolif.* (polymeric NP zeta potential vs cytotoxicity), Goodman et al. 2004 *Bioconjug. Chem.* (gold NP cationic/anionic toxicity), Hajipour et al. 2012 *Trends Biotechnol.* (general antibacterial NP review), Kalishwaralal et al. 2010 (silver NPs vs *P. aeruginosa* + *S. epidermidis* biofilms). Could plausibly assemble 50-200 rows of {NP material × surface charge × size × bacterial species × MIC/BPC/BIC}. Match: **direct, after construction**. Access: **medium** (manual extraction from ~10-15 paper PDFs + tables)
  - **NanoCommons / NanoSafetyDB / nanoHUB** — public nanoparticle property + toxicity datasets. Match: **loose** (mostly engineered metallic NPs, not polysaccharide-derived). Access: **easy** — https://www.nanocommons.eu/ ; https://nanohub.org/
  - **PubChem / ChEBI / ChEMBL** — small-molecule + glycan structures; could query for polysaccharide-related entries with antibacterial annotations. Match: **loose**. Access: **easy** — https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/
  - **NCBI GenBank — *Synechococcus* + cyanobacteria genomes** — extensive public sequence data for the bacterial-side of any QSAR reframe. Match: **direct, but bacterial-side only** (the paper's main contribution is on the nanoparticle side). Access: **easy**. **Cross-survey synergy: same database used for Allen Article 3's psbA primer specificity reframe.**
  - **Mirexus PhytoSpherix patents (US5656591A, WO2017177342A1)** — published patent specifications include synthesis protocols + degree-of-substitution measurements. Match: **direct** (foundational IP for the paper's nanoparticle product). Access: **easy** — https://patents.google.com/patent/WO2017177342A1
  - **Dittrich & Sibler 2005 *J. Colloid Interface Sci.*** + **Liang et al. 2013 *Colloids Surf. B*** + **Paulo et al. 2018 *Geosciences*** — same Dittrich-lab predecessor papers on *Synechococcus* surface chemistry + biomineralization. Match: **close** (same lab, same methodology). Access: **medium** (paywalled but methods directly applicable)
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium-hard.** Paper figures + SI accessible via Elsevier institutional access; raw data files not released. **Strongest reframe (literature-mined QSAR)** requires substantial citation-mining (~10-15 papers); **alternative reframe (titration LPM curve fitting on synthetic data)** is trivially easy via scipy.optimize.linprog + Eq. 1 + simulated noise.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:**
  - Already-known: pandas / numpy / sklearn / scipy.stats / matplotlib
  - Light additions: **scipy.optimize.linprog** (already in scipy) for the LPM titration-curve decomposition reframe; **scikit-optimize** or **GPyTorch** for the Bayesian-optimization reframe; **WebPlotDigitizer** (browser-based, free) for figure digitization in the literature-mined QSAR reframe
  - Optional advanced: **RDKit** for polysaccharide structural descriptors in the QSAR reframe; **PyTorch / DNABERT** for bacterial-genome side of the QSAR (overlap with Allen Article 3)
  - **Minimal new ecosystem lift** — most reframes use scipy/sklearn fundamentals already in toolkit
  - Domain reading: Dittrich & Sibler 2005 *J. Colloid Interface Sci.* (Dittrich-lab foundational paper on cyanobacterial surface chemistry); Paulo et al. 2018 *Geosciences* (effect of phosphorus on *Synechococcus* surface properties); Liang et al. 2013 *Colloids Surf. B* (CaCO₃ biomineralization on *Synechococcus* — same lab); Rendueles et al. 2013 *Environ. Microbiol.* (review on antibiofilm polysaccharides). **Domain accessible to chemistry undergrads with a few weeks of colloid chemistry + microbiology reading.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project would look like:**
  - **Strongest single framing — Literature-mined nanoparticle-antibacterial QSAR:** *"Predicting MIC/BPC/BIC of polymeric + metallic nanoparticles against bacterial biofilms from surface-charge + size + composition descriptors — a tabular meta-regression across 15 published antibiofilm-nanoparticle studies."* Manually extract data from this paper + Chronopoulou 2016 + Ong 2019 + Nomura 2016 + Shao 2015 + Goodman 2004 + Hajipour 2012 review + Kalishwaralal 2010 + Rendueles 2013 + ~5 other antibiofilm-NP papers. Train sklearn LightGBM / RandomForest, evaluate via 5-fold CV, surface SHAP feature importances ("does zeta potential matter more than size? Do polysaccharide NPs differ from metallic NPs?"). README headline: *"Across 15 published antibiofilm-nanoparticle studies, [X] explains [Y]% of MIC variance — surface charge dominates."* **Recruiter-legible to nanotechnology / antimicrobial / water-treatment / pharma audiences.** ~2-3 week project; CPU-only.
  - **Stretch — Cross-survey Allen + Dittrich unified portfolio:** *"From nanoparticle treatment to species-specific biofilm response — combining Dittrich's PhXC dose-response with Allen's qPCR-quantified biofilm species composition."* Predict species-specific biofilm response (Allen Article 1 outputs) under PhXC treatment regimes (Dittrich Article 1 dose-response). Maps to a real bioremediation use case: tailoring antibiofilm dosage to specific cyanobacterial / algal species. **Strongest single cross-professor portfolio piece in the survey.**
  - **Potentiometric titration LPM curve-fitting reframe:** *"Bayesian decomposition of acid-base titration curves into surface ligand pKa + concentration — a PyMC port of Eq. 1 on synthetic + real cyanobacterial titration data."* Generate synthetic titration curves from known ligand mixtures + Gaussian noise + scipy.optimize.linprog baseline; train PyMC model + compare. Maps to standard chemometrics + bioinorganic chemistry use cases.
  - **Bayesian optimization for PhXC formulation:** *"Sequential-design BO for cationized polysaccharide nanoparticle dose + DS optimization — replaying Tavafoghi et al. 2021 with fewer experiments."* GP surrogate on dose-response + DS variations; BO suggests next-best PhXC variants. Industry-relevant (Mirexus, antimicrobial-coating R&D, pharma formulation).

**Article 1 overall score:** Provisional (7 of 8 variables): (5 + 5 + 9 + 8 + 6 + 6 + 6) / 7 = 45 / 7 = **6.4 / 10**

**Article 1 verdict:** A **clean primary-research paper from Dittrich's own lab** (last + corresponding author, postdoc first author, NSERC-funded UTSC Biogeochemistry, with Mirexus industry collaboration) that establishes Dittrich as a strong PI signal. The paper itself is colloid + surface chemistry + microbiology with rigorous multi-modal characterization, but only modest inherent ML structure — **the literature-mined nanoparticle-antibacterial QSAR reframe is the strongest single portfolio anchor**, plus a unique **cross-survey synergy with Allen** (combining Dittrich's PhXC dose-response with Allen's qPCR biofilm-species data → species-specific antibiofilm-treatment prediction). **The dataset constraint** is similar to Allen Articles 1+2: small experimental n + literature-mining reframe required for serious portfolio piece. **Strongest portfolio angle: literature-mined QSAR across ~15 antibiofilm-NP studies + LightGBM + SHAP**, framed as "applied ML for water-quality + antimicrobial nanotechnology." **Cross-survey synergies**: **Dittrich + Allen** (cyanobacterial vs algal biofilms — strongest); **Dittrich + Frank Gu** (both nanoparticle materials chemistry in environmental contexts); **Dittrich + Edwards** (UTSC vs ChemE; both microbiological-systems with surface/sequence approaches).

### Article 2: Geochemical Controls on Internal Phosphorus Loading in Lake of the Woods (Aquatic Geochemistry / Limnology, Environment and Climate Change Canada Partnership)

- **Full citation:** Alam, M. S., Barthod, B., Li, J., Liu, H., Zastepa, A., Liu, X., & **Dittrich, M.** (2020). Geochemical controls on internal phosphorus loading in Lake of the Woods. *Chemical Geology*, 558, 119873, 14 pages. DOI: 10.1016/j.chemgeo.2020.119873. Available online 10 September 2020. © Elsevier (paywalled). **Funded by NSERC Discovery + Canada Foundation for Innovation + Ontario Research Fund (Leaders Opportunity Fund grant 22404, the same grant as Article 1) + Environment and Climate Change Canada to Dittrich.** First-author Alam supported by the Roger E. Deane Postdoctoral Fellowship.
- **Authorship signal:** **Both Alam (first) AND Dittrich (last) are co-corresponding authors.** Alam is a postdoctoral fellow in Dittrich's UTSC group. Co-authors: Barthod (Dittrich-lab MSc student per cited Barthod 2019 thesis at U of T + Université de Genève), Li (now at HKUST Department of Ocean Science), Zastepa (Environment and Climate Change Canada, Canada Centre for Inland Waters in Burlington — government-research partnership), Liu × 2 (Nanjing University international collaborators). **Strongest-PI-signal class with multi-institutional collaborative network**: Dittrich's group + ECCC + HKUST + Nanjing University. **Markedly different methodological flavor from Article 1** (which was colloid + surface chemistry + nanotechnology; Article 2 is field-based aquatic geochemistry + limnology + diagenetic modeling).
- **Problem in plain English:** Phosphorus is the productivity-limiting nutrient in freshwater systems — too much P causes harmful algal blooms (HABs), bottom-water hypoxia, biodiversity loss, and water-quality deterioration. **Lake of the Woods** (LOW) is a 3850 km² lake shared by Canada (Ontario) and the US (Minnesota), providing drinking water for 750,000+ people. External P inputs (Rainy River agricultural + industrial runoff = 75% of LOW's external P) have been declining since the 1950s, **but cyanobacterial blooms persist and water quality continues to deteriorate.** This persistence suggests **internal P loading** — P that has accumulated in lake sediments over decades being released back into the water column under favorable geochemical conditions — is a major driver. The paper quantifies this internal loading at 4 stations across LOW (Echo Bay West EB, Bigstone Bay 120, Poplar Bay 180, Rainy River Outlet 710), determines the geochemical controls (redox-sensitive Fe-bound P; organic-matter mineralization; Ca/Al-bound P; vivianite mineral burial), and provides multi-lake comparison data (Table 3) for 7 Canada/US polymictic lakes. **Mechanistic findings:** redox-sensitive BD-P (Fe-bound P) is the dominant P binding form in surface sediments (50-70% of TP); under anaerobic conditions, reductive dissolution of FeOOH releases legacy P; under aerobic conditions, P is re-sorbed by FeOOH at the SWI; long-term P burial is via Ca-P + Al-P + vivianite (Fe₃(PO₄)₂·8H₂O) precipitation in deep sediments.
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper, but the strongest ML angle of the two Dittrich articles** because it operates on a multi-station + multi-lake setup with public lake-water-quality data substrates:
  - (1) **Multi-lake P-loading prediction** — Table 3 directly provides comparison data across 7 Canada/US polymictic lakes (Big Traverse Bay, Bay of Quinte, Lake Diefenbaker, Lake Erie Western Basin, Lake Simcoe, Lake Winnipeg, plus the 4 LOW stations from this paper). Train tabular regression: lake morphometry (depth, area, mixing regime, trophic status) + sediment chemistry (TP, Fe, Al, Ca, organic carbon, BD-Fe:BD-P ratio) → annual internal P loading. Could expand via citation-mining to ~15-30 lake studies. **~50-200 row dataset achievable.**
  - (2) **HAB (Harmful Algal Bloom) early-warning prediction** — combine internal P loading (this paper) + water-column nutrient profiles + meteorological covariates (temperature, irradiance, ice cover) → predict cyanobacterial bloom risk. **Hot recruiter area** (NOAA, EPA, Heidelberg Tributary Loading Program for Lake Erie HABs).
  - (3) **Spectral mineral classification** — paper uses XRD + Raman + SEM-EDS to identify vivianite (Fe₃(PO₄)₂·8H₂O) in deep sediments. Train a mineral-classifier on **RRUFF mineral spectral database** (fully public, ~10,000 mineral entries with Raman + IR spectra). Standard chemometrics task; cleaner ML setup than Article 1's titration LPM.
  - (4) **Diagenetic ODE / PINN modeling** — paper uses Fick's first law (Eq. 3, 5) + stoichiometric mass balance (Eq. 7) for fluxes. Could train neural network surrogates or PINN (Physics-Informed Neural Network) for the coupled C-Fe-P-S diagenetic system. Adjacent to Abbatt Article 1's autoxidation-kinetics framing.
  - (5) **Cross-Article unified portfolio (Dittrich Article 1 + Article 2) — Cyanobacterial-bloom pipeline:** Article 2 quantifies the **nutrient-supply side** (internal P loading from sediments → cyanobacterial growth in water column); Article 1 quantifies the **mitigation side** (PhXC nanoparticle dose-response against cyanobacterial biofilms). **Unified ML pipeline:** predict cyanobacterial bloom risk from internal P loading (Article 2) → recommend antibiofilm dosage (Article 1). **Highest-coherence cross-paper synergy in any single professor's body of work in this survey.**
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: tabular categorical + numerical environmental covariates → tabular continuous outputs (P fluxes, P binding fractions, mineralization rates)
  - Shape / size if stated: 4 stations × ~30 sediment-depth points × ~10 measurement modalities (porewater SRP, Fe²⁺, Mn²⁺, NH4⁺, SO4²⁻, Ca, Si, plus solid-phase TP, BD-P fractions, organic C, TN, porosity) ≈ ~1200 datapoints; water-column profiles at ~5 depths × 4 stations × ~10 parameters ≈ ~200 datapoints; multi-lake comparison Table 3 = 7 lakes × ~5 covariates
  - Preprocessing: UWITEC gravity corer for sediment cores (5.5 cm × ~70 cm); dialysis peeper porewater extraction (1-cm intervals, 2-week deployment); Rhizon pore samplers (0.2 μm membrane); Unisense microelectrodes for O₂/pH/Eh/H₂S calibrated with quinhydrone redox standards; ICP-AES for Fe/Al/Ca/Mn/Si; ²¹⁰Pb-derived sedimentation rates from Zastepa et al. 2017a; **Hupfer 6-fraction P speciation protocol** (NH4Cl-P → BD-P → NaOH-SRP → NaOH-NRP → HCl-P → ref-P); PHREEQC v3 saturation index calculation; XRD (Rigaku D/max Rapid II, Mo Kα), Raman (LabRAM HR800, 532 nm laser), SEM-EDS (Zeiss Supra 55) for vivianite identification
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: short-term diffusive P fluxes (μmol/m²/d, Eq. 3), long-term net P release (mg/m²/d, Eq. 4 Rydin method), organic matter mineralization rates (mmol C/m²/d, Eq. 5+6+7), 6-fraction P speciation (mg/g DW), dissolved-phase concentration profiles, P retention efficiency (%), Corg:P (mol:mol) flux ratio
  - Units / label space: continuous regression targets in standard biogeochemistry units; some categorical (aerobic vs anaerobic stations)
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. Diagenetic flux modeling via Fick's first law + linear porosity correction + tortuosity θ² = 1 - ln(φ²); long-term P release via Rydin 2000 stabilization-depth method; saturation indices via PHREEQC v3.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Custom-generated field + lab dataset; **multi-lake comparison Table 3** is a literature-mined sub-dataset
  - Size: 4 stations × ~30 depths × ~10 modalities = ~1200 sediment + porewater datapoints; 7-lake comparison in Table 3
  - Source: Lake of the Woods sampling (September 2017) — UTSC Biogeochemistry lab + Environment and Climate Change Canada (Burlington); analytical chemistry at U of T Toronto + Nanjing University; cited LOW historical P loading data from Hargan et al. 2011 + Edlund et al. 2017 + James 2017a/b
  - Public? **Partial** — paper data in figures + Tables 1-3; raw data not released. **Cited public datasets:** NOAA Great Lakes (relevant for Lake Erie comparison); Environment and Climate Change Canada Open Data (LOW long-term monitoring); IJC Lake of the Woods Basin reports (publicly available); USGS NLET methods
  - Link: Article DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chemgeo.2020.119873 (Elsevier, paywalled); SI: doi link supplementary; ECCC Open Data: https://open.canada.ca/en/open-data
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **Short-term diffusive P fluxes: 0.3-7.8 mg/m²/d** across the 4 stations; **negative flux (-3.72 mg/m²/d) at station 120** (P diffusing INTO sediment at sampling time; transient)
  - **Long-term net P release: 0.21-9.96 mg/m²/d** across the 4 stations
  - **Annual internal P loading: 76-3635 mg/m²/yr** — the highest (3635 mg/m²/yr at Poplar Bay 180) is **>3× higher than the previously reported maximum for any Canadian/US polymictic lake** (Big Traverse Bay 1171 mg/m²/yr per James 2017b; Bay of Quinte 1026 per Markovic 2019a)
  - **P retention: 13% (anaerobic 180) to 74% (aerobic 710)** — anaerobic sediments are net sources of P; aerobic sediments are net sinks
  - **Cross-station mechanism:** anaerobic stations 120+180 have **Corg:JSRP ratio of 23-32** (much lower than Redfield 106 → ~70% of P release is from legacy Fe-P, NOT from current organic-matter degradation); aerobic stations 710+EB have **Corg:JSRP of 80-230** (organic-matter degradation dominant; reabsorption by oxic surface layer prevents P release)
  - **Vivianite confirmed at station 710 (13-15 cm depth)** via XRD + Raman + SEM-EDS — first identification at that location and contributes to long-term P burial
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **NOAA Great Lakes Environmental Research Laboratory (GLERL)** — extensive public water-quality, sediment, and HAB-forecasting data for Lakes Erie, Huron, Michigan, Ontario, Superior. Match: **direct** (sister system to LOW). Access: **easy** — https://www.glerl.noaa.gov/ ; https://coastwatch.glerl.noaa.gov/
  - **EPA Water Quality Portal (WQP) / STORET** — aggregates water-quality data from EPA + USGS + state agencies across US. Match: **close** (covers Big Traverse Bay LOW directly + Lake Erie). Access: **easy** — https://www.waterqualitydata.us/
  - **Environment and Climate Change Canada — Open Data Portal** — Canadian water-quality monitoring datasets including LOW long-term records. Match: **direct** (paper authors include ECCC researchers). Access: **easy** — https://open.canada.ca/en/open-data
  - **International Joint Commission (IJC) Lake of the Woods Basin Reports** — Clark & Sellers 2014 + DeSellas et al. 2009 (cited in paper) provide tabulated P loading data. Match: **direct** (foundational references). Access: **easy** — https://ijc.org/en/lwwlrb
  - **RRUFF Mineral Database** — fully public Raman + IR + XRD spectra for ~10,000 minerals including vivianite + apatite + Fe-oxides. Match: **direct** for the spectral-mineral-classification reframe. Access: **easy** — https://rruff.info/
  - **Heidelberg Tributary Loading Program (HTLP)** — long-term Lake Erie tributary nutrient loading data, foundational for HAB forecasting. Match: **close**. Access: **easy** — https://ncwqr.org/monitoring/data/
  - **NCAR / NOAA Great Lakes ice + meteorology data** — for the HAB-prediction reframe. Match: **adjacent**. Access: **easy** — https://www.glerl.noaa.gov/data/
  - **Multi-lake meta-analysis dataset** (Table 3 + ~10-20 cited papers) — Markovic et al. 2019a/b (Bay of Quinte), Doan et al. 2018, Matisoff et al. 2016 (Lake Erie), Nürnberg et al. 2013 (Lake Simcoe), North et al. 2015 (Lake Diefenbaker), Edlund et al. 2017 (LOW historical), Orihel et al. 2017 (Canadian freshwater P review). Match: **direct, after construction**. Access: **medium** (paywalled but tables extractable)
  - **Markovic et al. 2019a *Chem. Geol.* (Bay of Quinte)** — same Dittrich-lab predecessor paper with identical 6-fraction P speciation methodology. Match: **direct** (sister paper). Access: **medium** (paywalled Elsevier)
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium.** Paper data in figures + Tables 1-3 (digitizable); raw data not released. **NOAA + EPA + ECCC + IJC datasets are direct downloads** for the multi-lake meta-analysis and HAB-prediction reframes — substantially better public-data substrate than Article 1. **RRUFF database** is fully public for the spectral-mineral reframe.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:**
  - Already-known: pandas / numpy / sklearn / scipy.optimize / matplotlib
  - Light additions: **WebPlotDigitizer** for figure digitization (already in toolkit from Allen + Dittrich Article 1 reframes); **scipy.interpolate** for porewater profile interpolation
  - Moderate additions: **geopandas + folium / kepler.gl** for spatial / multi-station mapping (helpful but not required); **phreeqpy** (Python wrapper for PHREEQC) for the saturation-index reframe; **scipy.optimize for ODE diagenetic-system fitting**
  - Optional advanced: **PyTorch / DeepXDE** for PINN diagenetic modeling reframe; **HuggingFace transformers** if pursuing a sequence-based HAB classifier with ECCC bacterial-genome data; **rampy / spectrochempy** for Raman-spectra preprocessing in the mineral-classifier reframe
  - **Moderate new ecosystem lift** if pursuing GIS or PINN reframes; **minimal lift** if sticking with multi-lake tabular meta-analysis
  - Domain reading: Boudreau 1997 *Diagenetic Models* (foundational textbook on Fick's law for sediment diagenesis); Hupfer & Lewandowski 2008 *Int. Rev. Hydrobiol.* (P binding form review); Markovic et al. 2019a (Bay of Quinte sister paper); Orihel et al. 2017 (Canadian internal P loading review); Dittrich et al. 2013 (Lake Simcoe Dittrich-lab foundational paper). **Domain accessible to chemistry undergrads with a few weeks of limnology + biogeochemistry reading.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project would look like:**
  - **Strongest single framing — Multi-lake internal P-loading prediction:** *"Predicting annual internal phosphorus loading from lake morphometry + sediment geochemistry across 15-30 polymictic lakes — a tabular meta-regression with SHAP feature importances."* Combine Table 3 from this paper + Markovic 2019a (Bay of Quinte) + Doan 2018 + Matisoff 2016 (Lake Erie) + Nürnberg 2013 (Lake Simcoe) + North 2015 (Lake Diefenbaker) + Edlund 2017 (LOW historical) + Orihel 2017 review + ~10 other internal-P-loading studies.
