### Article 3: Bio-Cement Materials Engineering — Comparative Study of Ureolytic vs. Photoautotrophic Bacterial Admixtures in Ternary Mortars (Antex Western Industry Partnership via Mitacs Accelerate)

- **Full citation:** Aceituno-Caicedo, D., Shvarzman, A., Zhutovsky, S., & **Dittrich, M.** (2024). Impact of bacterial admixtures on the compressive and tensile strengths, permeability, and pore structure of ternary mortars: Comparative study of ureolytic and phototrophic bacteria. *Biotechnology Journal*, 19, e2300157, 11 pages. DOI: 10.1002/biot.202300157. Published online January 2024 (received 8 May 2023; revised 22 August 2023; accepted 21 September 2023). **CC BY 4.0 Open Access** — Wiley. **Funded by NSERC Discovery + Mitacs Accelerate Grant IT19632 (to Aceituno-Caicedo + Dittrich + Antex Western Ltd) + Canada Foundation for Innovation + Ontario Research Fund Leaders Opportunity Fund grant 22404 (the same grant as Articles 1 and 2).**
- **Authorship signal:** Dittrich is **last + corresponding author**. Aceituno-Caicedo (first author) is in Dittrich's group at U of T (likely PhD student or postdoc). Co-authors: **Shvarzman from Antex Western Ltd / ACM Technologies (Winnipeg, Manitoba)** — industry partner with direct Mitacs Accelerate co-funding; **Zhutovsky from Technion – Israel Institute of Technology** (international civil engineering / concrete-materials specialist). **Strongest-PI-signal class with industry + international academic collaboration.** **NEW INFO: Dittrich's affiliation reads "Earth Sciences Department, University of Toronto" (St. George) in addition to UTSC Phys & Env Sci**, and email is **m.dittrich@utoronto.ca** (NOT mdittrich@utsc.utoronto.ca as on Articles 1+2). **This suggests Dittrich has a joint appointment between UTSC and St. George Earth Sciences** — meaningful for master's applicants because graduate students in her lab may have access to BOTH UTSC and St. George resources. **Cited Zhu et al. 2018, 2021, 2015** reveal a sustained Dittrich-lab thread on cyanobacterial mortar carbonation going back ~10 years — bio-cement is not a new direction but a long-standing third research niche.
- **Problem in plain English:** Cement and concrete production = **~8% of global anthropogenic CO₂ emissions**, projected to grow 50% by 2050 (additional 85-105 gigatons CO₂). Bio-cement uses bacterial cells to improve mortar properties (strength, permeability, self-healing crack repair) via **microbially-induced calcium carbonate precipitation (MICP)**. **Existing approaches use ureolytic *Bacillus* species** (~84% of bio-mortar studies per the cited Dinarvand & Rashno 2021 review), but **ureolytic bacteria produce ammonia** — which causes ecosystem eutrophication, soil acidification, and (if NOx is released) increased greenhouse effect. **Photoautotrophic bacteria (cyanobacteria) sequester CO₂ via photosynthesis without producing ammonia** → potentially better environmental alternative. Paper directly compares 4 bacterial modifications: **vegetative *B. subtilis* (BS1)**, **sporulated *B. subtilis* (BS2)**, **live *Synechocystis* PCC6803 (PCC1)**, **UV-killed *Synechocystis* (PCC2)**, plus a **commercial Green-Basilisk healing agent (Com)** + cellulose-fiber-only control (Cf) + plain control (Cntl). Cells are immobilized on cellulose fibers as a carrier (Ultra Fiber 500, Solomon Colors). **Multi-modal characterization** via ASTM-standard mechanical tests + mercury intrusion porosimetry + DSC/XRD/TGA + SEM. **Headline finding:** photoautotrophic mortars achieve **13.7%/12.4% higher 28-day compressive strength + 42% lower water absorption** than sporulated heterotrophic mortars, while precipitating more calcite — **photoautotrophic = environmentally preferable AND mechanically superior**.
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper, but cleaner inherent ML structure than Articles 1 or 2.** Reframings:
  - (1) **Multi-modal property-prediction tabular regression** — predict {28-day compressive strength, splitting tensile strength, water absorption, initial sorptivity, secondary sorptivity, total/capillary/gel pore volume, critical pore diameter, CaCO₃ mass %, calcite:dolomite ratio, DSC peak temperature, heat absorbed} from {bacterial type, viability state, concentration, cellulose-fiber content, mortar mix design, age (3/7/28 days)}. **The paper itself uses PCA (Tables S17-S21, Figure 5) for multi-variate analysis** — natural ML extension via PLS regression, kernel PCA, or autoencoders.
  - (2) **MIP curve analysis with CNN** — Figure 4 shows mercury intrusion porosimetry curves (log differential intrusion vs pore diameter) for each mortar. Train a CNN or 1D transformer on these curves to predict mortar properties directly. **Standard chemometrics + curve-deconvolution task; cleaner ML setup than tabular alone.**
  - (3) **Literature-mined bio-cement meta-analysis** — assemble published bio-mortar performance data from Dinarvand & Rashno 2021 review + Jonkers et al. 2010 + Mondal & Ghosh 2018 + Singh & Gupta 2020 + Wang et al. 2014 + Rauf et al. 2020 + Jiang et al. 2017 + Rong et al. 2020 + ~30 other bio-mortar studies. ~84% of bio-mortar studies use *Bacillus*; combining with this paper's *Synechocystis* data adds a rare phototroph datapoint. Could plausibly assemble 100-300 row tabular dataset. Match: direct, after construction.
  - (4) **Bayesian optimization for bio-cement formulation** — given measured property dose-responses, BO to suggest next-best bacterial concentration + viability state + carrier material. **Highly industry-relevant** — multi-billion-dollar concrete/construction-materials industry actively pursuing low-carbon alternatives (LafargeHolcim, HeidelbergCement, CEMEX, Cementos Argos, Argos USA).
  - (5) **Cross-paper unified Dittrich-lab portfolio (Articles 1+2+3)** — unified theme: **microbial surface chemistry → mineral precipitation/inhibition across application domains**. Article 1 = surface charge (PhXC) → biofilm inhibition; Article 2 = sediment Fe²⁺ + dissolved P + Ca → vivianite/Ca-P precipitation; Article 3 = bacterial cell surface + EPS → CaCO₃ precipitation in mortar pores. **Unified ML pipeline: predict mineral precipitation rate from microbial surface properties** across aquatic (Article 2) + nanotechnology (Article 1) + cementitious (Article 3) systems. **Strongest 3-paper cross-portfolio synergy in the survey.**
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: tabular categorical (bacterial type, viability state, modification ID) + numerical (cell concentration, fiber content, age) → tabular continuous outputs (mechanical + permeability + microstructural + compositional)
  - Shape / size if stated: 7 modifications × 3 ages (3/7/28 day) × ~12 measurement modalities × 3 replicates ≈ ~750 measurement events; MIP curves at ~50 pore-diameter bins × 7 modifications = ~350 (pore-diameter, log-differential-intrusion) datapoints; DSC/XRD/TGA spectra at multiple temperatures × 7 modifications ≈ similar
  - Preprocessing: bacterial culture in Yeast Extract-Peptone-Urea (B. subtilis) or BG11 medium (Synechocystis); endospore preparation via Schaeffer's sporulation medium; UV-killing via 1-h UV-radiation; cell concentration adjustment to 10⁷ cells/mL; cellulose fiber bacteria-immobilization via 24-h incubation; mortar mixing per ASTM C305-20; final cell concentrations 5×10⁶ cells per cube + 8×10⁶ per cylinder; ASTM C109/C109M-13 (compressive), ASTM C496-96 (splitting tensile), ASTM C1585-20 (water absorption); MIP via mercury intrusion; DSC + XRD + TGA + SEM via standard protocols; statistics via One-Way ANOVA + post-hoc Tukey + F-Tests (sorptivity); PCA via OriginPro 2022
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: 28-day compressive strength (MPa, range ~50-90), tensile strength (MPa), total water absorbed (g/cm²), initial + secondary sorptivity (mm/s^1/2), total/capillary/gel pore volume (% or cc/cc), critical pore diameter (μm, range 0.007-9.6), CaCO₃ mass % (range 4.7-7.3% by TGA), calcite:dolomite ratio (range 0.22-0.31), DSC heat absorbed (J/g, range 153-211), DSC peak temperature (°C, range 801-812)
  - Units / label space: continuous regression targets in standard civil-engineering / materials-science units
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. Statistical analysis only — One-Way ANOVA + post-hoc Tukey + F-Tests; **PCA for multi-variate feature integration via OriginPro 2022** (Figure 5).
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Custom-generated experimental dataset
  - Size: ~750 mechanical/permeability/compositional measurements + ~350 MIP curve datapoints + spectra
  - Source: U of T Dittrich-lab + Antex Western (Winnipeg, MB) + Technion (Haifa, Israel); *B. subtilis* UAMH 6930 from UAMH Centre for Global Microfungal Biodiversity; *Synechocystis* PCC6803 from Pasteur Culture Collection; cellulose fibers from Solomon Colors Inc. (Ultra Fiber 500); commercial healing agent from Green-Basilisk BV (US Patent 8460458B2)
  - Public? **Partial** — paper data in figures + Table 1 + SI Tables S2-S21; raw data on request. **CC BY 4.0 license permits free figure digitization.** Mitacs Accelerate IP overlay may limit some industry-side data
  - Link: Article DOI: https://doi.org/10.1002/biot.202300157 (Wiley, **CC BY Open Access**); SI: doi link supplementary; Green-Basilisk patent: https://patents.google.com/patent/US8460458B2/en
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **Photoautotrophic mortars (PCC1, PCC2) have 13.7%/12.4% higher 28-day compressive strength than sporulated B. subtilis (BS2)** at the same cell concentration
  - **PCC1, PCC2 absorb 42.11% less water than BS2** — substantially lower permeability
  - **PCC1, PCC2 precipitate the most calcite** (7.32% and 7.28% CaCO₃ by TGA mass) — highest of all bacterial modifications
  - **BS1 (vegetative B. subtilis) precipitates the most overall carbonate** (25.52% TGA mass) but most is dolomite, not calcite (low calcite:dolomite ratio of 0.28)
  - **Critical pore diameter shifts:** BS1 + PCC1 (live cells) achieve <0.1 μm critical pore diameter (vs. 9.6 μm for cellulose-only Cf mortar) — 100× finer pore structure
  - **UV-radiation triggers EPS production** in Syn. PCC6803 (UV-defense mechanism) → PCC2 EPS contributes to additional pore-plugging despite cell death
  - **Conclusion: photoautotrophic = environmentally preferable (CO₂ sequestration, no ammonia) AND mechanically superior** to sporulated heterotrophic — strong policy + industry implications

### Article 1: Passive Air Samplers for Vertical Gaseous Mercury Concentration Gradients in Forests and Above Contaminated Soil (Multi-Site International Field Study)

- **Full citation:** Quant, M. I., Feigis, M., Mistry, S., Lei, Y. D., Mitchell, C. P. J., Staebler, R., Di Guardo, A., Terzaghi, E., & **Wania, F.** (2021). Using passive air samplers to quantify vertical gaseous elemental mercury concentration gradients within a forest and above soil. *Journal of Geophysical Research: Atmospheres*, 126, e2021JD034981, 15 pages. DOI: 10.1029/2021JD034981. Published online July 2021. © 2021 American Geophysical Union (Wiley) — appears under standard AGU terms (likely paywalled but with green-OA institutional repository access). **Funded by NSERC Discovery Grant to FW + Centre for Global Change Science U of T summer undergraduate internship to MF.**
- **Authorship signal:** Wania is **last + corresponding author**. **CRediT statement explicitly lists Wania as: Conceptualization, Funding acquisition, Investigation, Methodology, Supervision, Writing original draft + review/editing** — strongest possible PI signal class. Quant (first author) is in Wania's lab at UTSC (likely PhD student). Co-authors include: **Carl P. J. Mitchell (UTSC, also at the same department)** — Wania's frequent collaborator with multiple cited joint papers; **Ralf Staebler (Environment and Climate Change Canada)** — government partner who operates the Borden Forest tower; **Antonio Di Guardo + Elisa Terzaghi (University of Insubria, Como, Italy)** — international collaborators for the Brescia study. **Multi-institutional collaborative network** spanning UTSC + ECCC + Italian academic partner.
- **Problem in plain English:** Mercury (Hg) is a global pollutant that bioaccumulates and harms human health + ecosystems. Most atmospheric Hg is **gaseous elemental mercury (GEM)**, which has a long atmospheric lifetime (months to over a year) and undergoes long-range transport. Vegetation acts as a **net Hg sink** via foliar uptake during the growing season; contaminated soils are **net Hg sources** via evaporation. Quantifying the **air-surface flux** of GEM is critical for understanding global Hg cycling and assessing contaminated sites — but conventional methods (dynamic flux chambers, micrometeorological techniques) are **expensive, energy-reliant, labor-intensive, and lack spatial resolution.** **Passive air samplers (PAS)** — which rely on diffusive uptake without pumps or electricity — are cheap and easy to deploy but historically have struggled to discriminate the small concentration differences along vertical gradients. The paper demonstrates that a recently-developed PAS (McLagan et al. 2016, sulfur-impregnated activated carbon in Radiello diffusive body) can record vertical GEM concentration gradients at scales from cm to tens of meters over deployments lasting months, by adjusting sampling rates for height-specific wind speed + temperature. Three field studies validate the approach: (1) **Borden Forest Tower** (Ontario, mixed deciduous, 22 m canopy) with 13 sampling heights × 9 deployments × 1.5 years; (2) **UTSC campus** (Scarborough) with 6 sampling heights × 13 deployments × 1.5 years × natural vs. HgCl₂-contaminated soil × forest vs. clearing; (3) **Brescia, Italy** industrially-contaminated agricultural field with 22 sampling sites × 5 heights × 3-month deployment.
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper, but the cleanest ML reframings of any single article in the survey** because of strong public-data substrates + multiple supervised-task structures:
  - (1) **Multi-site time-series regression** — predict GEM concentration at varying heights from meteorological covariates (wind speed, temperature, leaf area index/LAI, solar irradiance, snow cover, season). LSTM / transformer / temporal-fusion-transformer on multi-station time-series. **Direct match to GMOS / MercNet / NEON / ECCC NAtChem public data** (~30 stations globally with 5-15 years of monthly Hg measurements each).
  - (2) **Spatial regression / kriging** — Brescia field has 22 sites with paired GEM-air + Hg-soil measurements. Predict GEM concentration in air from soil Hg + meteorological covariates via Gaussian process regression / PyKrige. **Standard geostatistics task** with a clean supervised setup.
  - (3) **Sampling-rate correction model** — paper uses empirical adjustments per McLagan, Mitchell et al. 2017 for wind speed + temperature dependence of PAS sampling rates. ML reframe: train a neural network or Gaussian process to learn the correction directly from calibration data. Industry-relevant for environmental-monitoring companies.
  - (4) **Contaminated-site assessment ML pipeline** — predict GEM emission flux from soil Hg + meteorological covariates, with uncertainty quantification. Direct industry application: environmental remediation consulting (AECOM, Stantec, ERM, Tetra Tech).
  - (5) **Cross-survey unified portfolio: Wania + Dittrich** — both at UTSC! Both work on environmental contaminant chemistry. Wania = atmospheric Hg fate/transport (Article 1); Dittrich = aquatic Hg/P biogeochemistry (Dittrich Article 2 has Hg-relevant aquatic-sediment data) + cyanobacterial-biofilm carbonation. **Strongest within-campus cross-survey synergy** — could combine Wania's atmospheric-deposition data with Dittrich's aquatic-biogeochemistry data for a **fate-and-transport pipeline**: atmospheric Hg deposition → vegetation/soil → leaching to lake sediments → biomineralization or recycling.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: tabular continuous (height, deployment length, temperature, wind speed, LAI, solar irradiance, soil Hg concentration) + categorical (site, season, contaminated vs. natural soil, forest vs. clearing) → tabular continuous outputs (GEM concentration ng/m³, Hg mass ng, evaporation flux ng/m²/h)
  - Shape / size if stated: 602 PAS deployments total = 171 (Borden) + 360 (Scarborough) + 71 (Brescia); 13 sampling heights × 9 Borden deployments + 6 heights × 13 Scarborough deployments × 4 vertical profiles + 22 Brescia sites × 1-5 heights; 1.5-year time-series at Borden + Scarborough; 3-month deployment at Brescia
  - Preprocessing: thermal-desorption + amalgamation + atomic-absorption spectroscopy per US EPA method 7473; field-blank correction; sampling-rate adjustment for wind-speed + temperature profile via McLagan, Mitchell et al. 2017 calibration; height-specific wind profile via Yi 2008 method below canopy; standard logarithmic wind profile via von Kármán + friction velocity above canopy or in non-forested sites; 3-point moving average for smoothing of vertical profiles
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: GEM concentration (ng/m³, range 1.0-14.1 across the three sites), Hg mass on PAS (ng), GEM evaporation flux (ng/m²/h, range 0-600), seasonal/spatial gradient direction (regression / classification)
  - Units / label space: continuous regression in standard atmospheric-chemistry units
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. Modified Bowen Ratio (MBR) flux estimation via Eq. 1 (F = -K · ΔC/Δz, where K is turbulent exchange coefficient). Standard linear regression of Cq vs height. ANOVA + statistical comparison.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: Custom-generated PAS deployments + cited public covariates
  - Size: 602 PAS deployments + meteorological time-series from each site (Borden has 12 height-resolved temperature + 2-height wind speed at hourly resolution; Scarborough nearest weather station; Brescia nearest weather station + Carozzi 2012 / Stutz 2002 boundary-layer parameters)
  - Source: UTSC Wania-lab + Borden Forest Tower (operated by ECCC); Scarborough UTSC campus; Brescia agricultural field (Italian collaborator Di Guardo + Terzaghi); cited public Cole et al. 2014 Canadian Hg trends; cited Sprovieri et al. 2016 GMOS network; cited Morosini et al. 2021 Brescia soil Hg map
  - Public? **Partial** — paper data in Tables S1-S4 + figures (digitizable); raw data on request likely. **Cited public datasets are exceptionally strong** (GMOS, MercNet, NEON, ECCC NAtChem, GAPS — see below)
  - Link: Article DOI: https://doi.org/10.1029/2021JD034981 (AGU/Wiley); SI Tables S1-S4 publicly accessible
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **Borden seasonal cycle**: GEM 1.1-1.2 ng/m³ in summer (foliage uptake) → 1.4-1.5 ng/m³ in winter; vegetation = net Hg sink during growing season
  - **Scarborough contaminated soil**: GEM evaporation flux **50-600 ng/m²/h** (estimated 0.87 mg / 0.49 mg total Hg loss from clearing / forest sites over the 1.5-year deployment, vs. 125 mg HgCl₂ initially added)
  - **Brescia industrial field**: GEM **4.7-14.1 ng/m³ at 5 cm above contaminated soil** (vs. 2.4 ng/m³ at background sites 3.4 km away; ~10× elevation over typical Italian background of 1.5 ng/m³); flux **150-1350 ng/m²/h**; spatial pattern in air aligns with soil Hg map but with East-shift due to prevailing Southwesterly winds
  - **PAS performance:** average precision 3.6% / accuracy 9% (per McLagan, Hussain et al. 2018); 7.2-7.6% replicate precision in this study (slightly worse due to low wind speeds below canopy / close to ground)
  - **Demonstrated capabilities:** PAS can identify net flux direction (deposition vs. evaporation); when combined with MBR method, can estimate evaporative GEM flux from contaminated soil; spatial-resolution and cost advantages over conventional micrometeorological techniques
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **GMOS (Global Mercury Observation System) network** — Sprovieri et al. 2016 cited; ~30 ground-based atmospheric-Hg monitoring sites globally (from Arctic to Antarctica) with multi-year time-series of GEM concentrations. Match: **direct** (same measurement type as the paper). Access: **easy** — https://www.gmos.eu/sdi/index.php
  - **NOAA Mercury Speciation Network (MercNet)** — Schmeltz et al. 2011 cited; US national atmospheric-Hg network with public data via NADP (National Atmospheric Deposition Program). Match: **direct**. Access: **easy** — https://nadp.slh.wisc.edu/networks/mercury-deposition-network/
  - **Environment and Climate Change Canada NAtChem Mercury** — Canadian Air and Precipitation Monitoring Network (CAPMoN) Hg deposition data; cited Cole et al. 2014 *Atmosphere* paper used exactly this data for Canadian GEM trends. Match: **direct** (same country + agency as Borden Forest tower). Access: **easy** — https://open.canada.ca/data/en/dataset?q=mercury+atmospheric
  - **NEON (National Ecological Observatory Network) Mercury data** — Obrist et al. 2017 *Nature* cited; US ecological-monitoring sites with multi-year Hg + flux measurements. Match: **direct**. Access: **easy** — https://data.neonscience.org/
  - **GAPS (Global Atmospheric Passive Sampling) network** — Herkert et al. 2018 *Environ. Sci. Process. Impacts* cited; **Wania is part of this network** — global PAS-based POP monitoring at ~50+ sites. Match: **direct** (same passive sampling methodology). Access: **easy** — https://www.canada.ca/en/environment-climate-change/services/air-pollution/monitoring-networks-data/persistent-organic-pollutants/global-atmospheric-passive-sampling.html
  - **AMAP/UN Environment Global Mercury Assessment 2018** — paper cited as reference; public report with country-level Hg emission inventories. Match: **adjacent** (emissions, not concentrations). Access: **easy** — https://www.unenvironment.org/resources/publication/global-mercury-assessment-2018
  - **EPA Air Quality System (AQS)** — US national air quality data including atmospheric Hg at select sites. Match: **close**. Access: **easy** — https://www.epa.gov/aqs
  - **OpenAQ** — global open-air-quality data aggregator; some Hg data. Match: **adjacent**. Access: **easy** — https://openaq.org/

  - **Borden Forest Tower (ECCC)** — Wania et al. cited Canada (E.A.) 2017; ECCC operates this tower with public data feeds for temperature, wind speed, humidity, eddy-covariance fluxes. Match: **direct** (same site as Article 1). Access: **easy** — https://www.canada.ca/en/environment-climate-change/services/air-pollution/research-science/applications/borden-forest-station.html

The Borden Forest Tower (ECCC) is the same site as the paper, with public meteorological data feeds.

### Article 2: Nested Exposure Model (NEM) for Global Multimedia Fate-and-Transport of Persistent Organic Pollutants (Norwegian-Led International Model-Development Collaboration)

- **Full citation:** Breivik, K., Eckhardt, S., McLachlan, M. S., & **Wania, F.** (2021). Introducing a nested multimedia fate and transport model for organic contaminants (NEM). *Environmental Science: Processes & Impacts*, advance article, 12 pages. DOI: 10.1039/d1em00084e. Published 12 July 2021. **CC BY-NC 3.0 Open Access** — Royal Society of Chemistry. **Funded by Norwegian Research Council #244298 + Long-range Research Initiative of European Chemical Industry Council (CEFIC) project ECO-53.**
- **Authorship caveat:** **Wania is last author but NOT corresponding** — Breivik (Norwegian Institute for Air Research, NILU + University of Oslo) is corresponding author. Wania's CRediT statement: **conceptualization + funding acquisition + supervision + methodology + writing review/editing.** Co-authors include Sabine Eckhardt (NILU) + Michael S. McLachlan (Stockholm University, Department of Environmental Science). **This is a four-PI international model-development collaboration** rather than a Wania-lab primary research paper. **Slightly weaker PI signal class than Article 1.** However: **the cited foundational paper (Wania, Breivik, Persson, McLachlan 2006 — CoZMo-POP2 paper) is Wania-FIRST-authored**, and NEM is described as adopting "major parts of the code from CoZMo-POP2" — meaning Wania is **substantially intellectually responsible for the underlying model framework** that NEM extends. **15+ year sustained Wania-Breivik-McLachlan collaboration on POPs fate-and-transport modeling.** Wania is at UTSC Phys & Env Sci on this paper (only Phys & Env Sci listed; not Chemistry like Article 1).
- **Problem in plain English:** **Persistent Organic Pollutants (POPs)** — like polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs) — are banned globally under the **Stockholm Convention** because they undergo **long-range atmospheric transport (LRAT)**, accumulate in remote regions (Arctic, mountain lakes), and harm human health + ecosystems. Regulatory effectiveness evaluation under the Stockholm Convention requires **fate-and-transport models** that can predict where POPs end up given known emissions. Existing models suffer from a tradeoff: **simple multimedia models** (CoZMo-POP2, BETR-Global) are computationally efficient but have coarse spatial resolution; **high-resolution transport models (HRTMs)** like FLEXPART or GEOS-Chem are accurate but computationally too demanding for decadal POP simulations. The paper introduces **NEM (Nested Exposure Model)** — a multimedia fate-and-transport model that achieves **global coverage at coarse resolution (15° or 30°) AND high resolution (1°) within user-defined target regions** through nested grids. Validates NEM for PCB-153 against 9 EMEP / AMAP European monitoring stations (Andøya, Aspvreten, Birkenes, Košetice, Pallas, Rörvik, Råö, Stórhöfði, Zeppelin); achieves model-measurement agreement within factor 1.5 at 1°×1° resolution. **Quantifies effect of numerical diffusion**: coarse 15° resolution overestimates atmospheric concentrations by factor 3-8 at remote sites due to artificial horizontal mixing. **Demonstrates application to atmospheric deposition to Baltic Sea**: 15° prediction is 1.5-2.2× higher than 1° prediction for total deposition, with order-of-magnitude discrepancies for individual sub-basins.
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper.** Reframings:
  - (1) **ML emulator / surrogate model for NEM** — train a neural network or Gaussian process to approximate NEM predictions at 1° resolution given coarse 15°-resolution inputs + meteorology. **Standard physics-emulator ML** (similar to AI4ClimateScience emulator-development efforts at NCAR / NOAA / ECMWF). Reduces computational cost from "thousands of CPU-hours" to "minutes on free Colab." **Hot AI4Earth area.**
  - (2) **PCB emission inventory ML downscaling** — paper uses Breivik et al. 2002 + 2016 emission inventory at population-density resolution (which the paper itself notes is increasingly inaccurate at finer scales). ML reframe: train regression model on cited Gluge 2017 Switzerland PCB inventory + Travnikov 2020 EMEP inventory + similar high-resolution national inventories to predict country-level PCB emissions at 1° resolution.
  - (3) **Spatial-resolution numerical-diffusion correction** — train ML model to predict the discrepancy between 15° and 1° NEM predictions from local source proximity + meteorology + land-cover covariates. **Industry/scientific use case**: correcting coarse global atmospheric transport models without running fine-resolution simulations. Direct industry adjacency: NCAR / NOAA / ECMWF model-development teams.
  - (4) **Multi-station LRAT classifier** — direct extension of Figure 5 in the paper. Predict the fraction of atmospheric POP burden at a monitoring station that comes from local emissions vs. LRAT, given site coordinates + emission inventory + meteorological covariates. Could classify any new candidate monitoring location as "remote" vs. "source-influenced" before instrument deployment.
  - (5) **Cross-paper synergy (Wania Article 1 + Article 2)** — Article 1 = ground-truth atmospheric Hg measurements via PAS at fine spatial resolution (cm to tens of meters); Article 2 = global-scale POP modeling via nested grid (1° to 30°). Together they span the **full air-monitoring-to-modeling pipeline** for atmospheric persistent contaminants. **Strong cross-paper coherence** within Wania's lab.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: spatially-gridded multi-dimensional environmental data (latitude × longitude × month × compartment) → spatially-gridded predicted concentrations
  - Shape / size if stated: 1° resolution = 64,800 grid cells globally (180 × 360); 5° resolution = 2,592 grid cells; 15° resolution = 288 grid cells. NEM is constrained to ~3000 grid cells per single simulation, so 5° is the finest globally feasible without nesting. Compartments: 2-layer atmosphere + 2 forest canopies (needleleaf/broadleaf) + 3 soil types + permanent snow/ice + freshwater + freshwater sediment + seawater + marine sediment + seasonal sea ice. Time-step: 6 hours (30°/15°), 3 hours (10°), 1 hour (5°), 15 minutes (1°). Simulation period: 1930-2020 (90 years). 9 EMEP / AMAP monitoring stations for validation
  - Preprocessing: WaterGAP 2.2c freshwater discharge (monthly means 2000-2009, 0.5° resolution) + linear interpolation to daily; SODA3 ocean reanalysis (horizontal + vertical velocities + bathymetry + sea-ice cover); ECMWF temperature + wind speed + precipitation + dry/wet period durations (monthly means at 0.5° resolution); IGBP land-cover classification at 0.5°; FAO/ISRIC soil organic carbon at 0.5°; Breivik 2002+2016 PCB emission inventory; Li et al. 2003 PCB partition coefficients (KOW, KOA, KAW) + activation energies; Brubaker & Hites 1998 OH-radical reaction rate constant; sine-function seasonal emission variability (50% above annual mean in warmest month per grid cell)
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: PCB-153 atmospheric concentration (pg/m³) at each grid cell × month × atmospheric layer; PCB-153 concentration in 9 other compartments (soils, freshwater, sediment, seawater, etc.); atmospheric deposition flux (g/m²/y) to specific receiving environments (Baltic Sea sub-basins); ratio of "atmospheric inflow vs. local emissions" per grid cell (dimensionless, 0-1)
  - Units / label space: continuous regression in standard environmental-fate-modeling units (pg/m³ for air concentrations; ng/m²/h for fluxes)
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. **NEM is a fugacity-based dynamic multimedia mass-balance model**: tracks chemical mass between compartments via partition coefficients (Wania-Mackay framework) + first-order kinetics for chemical transformation + advective transport via wind/water/ocean flows. Numerical solution via Eulerian box-model time-integration. Implementation extends CoZMo-POP2 (Pascal/Excel — Wania-co-developed) + BETR-Global (Python/MATLAB).
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: NEM model output for PCB-153 (1930-2020) + cited public input datasets + EMEP/AMAP validation data
  - Size: ~64,800 grid cells × 90 years × ~12 monthly time-steps × 11 compartments = ~7.7 × 10⁸ datapoints for the full 1° simulation; 9 EMEP/AMAP stations × 5-25 years of monthly observations each = ~1,000-2,000 validation datapoints
  - Source: NEM developed in Norway (Breivik/Eckhardt at NILU) + Sweden (McLachlan at Stockholm University) + Canada (Wania at UTSC); EMEP measurement data via ebas.nilu.no; AMAP data via amap.no; emission inventories from Breivik et al. 2002+2016; meteorological data from ECMWF; WaterGAP 2.2c; SODA3
  - Public? **Partial** — paper data in figures + Tables S1-S2 (digitizable under CC BY-NC); raw NEM output not released; **but all input datasets are PUBLIC** (EMEP, AMAP, WaterGAP, SODA3, ECMWF, IGBP, FAO/ISRIC, Breivik PCB inventory)
  - Link: Article DOI: https://doi.org/10.1039/d1em00084e (RSC, **CC BY-NC**); EMEP: https://ebas.nilu.no/; AMAP: https://www.amap.no/; ECMWF ERA5: https://cds.climate.copernicus.eu/; WaterGAP: https://www.uni-frankfurt.de/45217767/WaterGAP; SODA3: http://www.atmos.umd.edu/~ocean/
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **At 1°×1° resolution**: NEM predictions within factor 1.5 of EMEP measurements at 7 of 9 northern European stations
  - **92%+ of atmospheric PCB-153 burden** at EMEP stations attributed to **advective atmospheric transport from outside the grid cell** (i.e., LRAT) at 1° resolution — confirms that EMEP "background" sites genuinely capture LRAT signal rather than local sources
  - **Coarse 15° resolution overestimates concentrations by factor 3-8** at remote sites due to numerical diffusion (artificial horizontal mixing dilutes source-region concentrations into remote cells)
  - **Geometric-mean ratio (model/observation) across 9 sites**: 3.8 at 15° / 2.4 at 5° / **1.5 at 1°** — model-measurement agreement systematically improves with finer resolution
  - **Baltic Sea atmospheric deposition prediction**: 15° prediction is **1.5-2.2× higher than 1° prediction** for total deposition; sub-basin-level discrepancies can exceed one order of magnitude (Bothnian Sea winter; Kattegat/Skagerrak summer)
  - **Numerical diffusion "edge effect"** at the boundary of nested domains can be reduced from factor 1.96-1.26 (30° outside) to 1.09-0.93 (10° outside), demonstrating a clear nesting-strategy tradeoff
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **EMEP database (ebas.nilu.no)** — Tørseth et al. 2012 cited; long-term European POP monitoring including PCB-153 at the 9 stations validated in this paper. **Direct match** for the validation side. Access: **easy** — https://ebas.nilu.no/
  - **AMAP (Arctic Monitoring and Assessment Programme)** — Hung et al. 2016 cited; long-term Arctic POP monitoring. Match: **direct** for Zeppelin / Stórhöfði stations. Access: **easy** — https://amap.no/
  - **GAPS (Global Atmospheric Passive Sampling) network** — Pozo et al. 2006 cited; **same network referenced in Wania Article 1**; global PAS-based POP monitoring at ~50+ sites. Wania-lab is part of this network. Access: **easy** via ECCC + cited papers
  - **IADN (Integrated Atmospheric Deposition Network) Great Lakes** — Hillery et al. 1998 cited; joint Canada-US monitoring of POPs at 5+ Great Lakes stations. Match: **direct** for North American POPs work. Access: **easy** — https://www.canada.ca/en/environment-climate-change/services/air-pollution/monitoring-networks-data.html
  - **Stockholm Convention Global Monitoring Plan (GMP)** — UNEP 2017 cited; international policy-driven POP monitoring synthesizing data from EMEP + AMAP + IADN + GAPS. Match: **direct** for the regulatory framework. Access: **easy** — https://www.pops.int/
  - **WaterGAP 2.2c global freshwater model** — Müller Schmied et al. 2014 cited; Goethe University Frankfurt. Match: **direct** for the freshwater-transport input data. Access: **easy** — https://www.uni-frankfurt.de/45217767/WaterGAP
  - **SODA3 ocean reanalysis** — Carton et al. 2018 cited; University of Maryland. Match: **direct** for ocean-circulation input data. Access: **easy** — http://www.atmos.umd.edu/~ocean/
  - **ECMWF ERA5 reanalysis** — paper cites ECMWF for temperature/wind/precipitation; ERA5 is the modern public version. Match: **direct**. Access: **easy** — https://cds.climate.copernicus.eu/
  - **IGBP land cover** (Belward 1996) — public global 1km land-cover classification. Access: **easy** — https://earlybird.gsfc.nasa.gov/cgi-bin/lalo.cgi
  - **FAO / ISRIC soil maps** (Batjes 2005) — public global soil database. Access: **easy** — https://www.isric.org/
  - **Breivik et al. 2002 + 2016 PCB emission inventories** — public emission data covering 1930-2020 by country. Match: **direct** (paper's primary emission input). Access: **easy** — supplementary materials of the cited papers
  - **CoZMo-POP2 (Wania, Breivik, Persson, McLachlan 2006 — Wania-FIRST-authored sister paper)** — earlier non-spatially-resolved version of NEM; published in *Environ. Modell. Software*. Match: **direct** (foundational paper). Access: **medium** (paywalled Elsevier but methodology fully described)
  - **BETR-Global** (MacLeod et al. 2011, Göktas & MacLeod 2016) — sister model that NEM extends; **published on GitHub** at https://github.com/BetrTools/BetrTools and https://betr-research.readthedocs.io/. Match: **direct**. Access: **easy** (open source)
  - **FLEXPART** (atmospheric transport model) — Halse et al. 2013 cited; open-source HRTM that NEM is positioned as an alternative to. Match: **adjacent**. Access: **easy** — https://www.flexpart.eu/
  - **GEOS-Chem** (atmospheric chemistry model) — Friedman & Selin 2012 cited; open-source HRTM. Match: **adjacent**. Access: **easy** — https://geos-chem.seas.harvard.edu/
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Medium-easy.** Paper data in figures + Tables S1-S2 (digitizable under CC BY-NC); NEM raw output not released but **all input datasets are public** (EMEP, AMAP, WaterGAP, SODA3, ECMWF, IGBP, FAO/ISRIC, Breivik PCB inventory). **BETR-Global is open-source on GitHub** as a starting point for any ML-emulator reframe. **Same exceptional public-data substrate as Article 1** plus modeling-side public datasets.

  - Already-known: pandas / numpy / sklearn / scipy / matplotlib
  - **Moderate new ecosystem: xarray + netCDF4** (atmospheric-science multi-dimensional data standard; well-documented but new); **cartopy** for geographic-projection plotting
  - Optional advanced: **PyTorch / pytorch-forecasting** for ML emulator reframe; **JAX / jax-cfd** for differentiable atmospheric modeling; **WRF-Chem / GEOS-Chem / FLEXPART** as heavy primary-modeling frameworks (would be substantial new ecosystem; only if pursuing primary-modeling reframe rather than ML emulator)
  - **Higher ecosystem lift than Article 1** primarily because atmospheric-science multi-dimensional data manipulation requires xarray/netCDF, plus the NEM model itself is in custom Pascal/Excel/Python code. **However, ML emulator reframe is feasible on free Colab using only xarray + sklearn + PyTorch**

README headline: *"NEM-emulator achieves [X]% accuracy at [Y]× speedup over physics-based multimedia modeling — opens up sensitivity analyses + thousands of POP scenarios that were previously computationally infeasible."* ~4-week project; trains on free Colab GPU.

Step 2 (Article 2): use ML emulator of NEM to predict POP atmospheric concentration at any global location from emission inventory + meteorology. Single notebook chains both papers. **Strongest cross-paper coherence within Wania's lab.** ~5-week project.

Industry-relevant for NCAR / NOAA / ECMWF model-development teams. ~2-week project.

### Article 3: Polyurethane Foam Partitioning Data Compilation + Uncertainty Quantification for Passive Air Sampling (Foundational Methodology Paper)

- **Full citation:** Li, Y., & **Wania, F.** (2021). Partitioning between polyurethane foam and the gas phase: data compilation, uncertainty estimation and implications for air sampling. *Environmental Science: Processes & Impacts*, advance article, 12 pages. DOI: 10.1039/d1em00036e. Published 12 April 2021. Royal Society of Chemistry (likely CC BY-NC, same as Wania Article 2 in the same journal). **Funded by NSERC Discovery Grant to FW + Connaught scholarship to YL.**
- **Authorship signal:** Wania is **last + corresponding author** with PhD/Master's student Yuening Li as first author. **Strongest PI signal class** — back to Wania-corresponding primary research from his own lab (vs. Article 2's Wania-last-but-not-corresponding international collaboration). **Wania-Li 2-author paper signals tight PhD-supervision dynamic** (no other co-authors required for the methodology development).
- **Problem in plain English:** **Passive Air Sampling (PAS)** with polyurethane foam (PUF) sorbents is the most widely used method for measuring atmospheric semi-volatile organic compound (SVOC) concentrations — a recent review identified 300+ PAS-PUF papers. PAS works by letting target chemicals diffuse from air into a sorbent over weeks-to-months, then quantifying the chemical mass on the sorbent and back-calculating air concentration via an effective sampling volume Vair. **However, Vair calculation requires knowing the equilibrium partition coefficient KPUF/G between PUF and gas phase** — and KPUF/G values are uncertain, which propagates to uncertain Vair. **Most PAS-PUF studies don't quantify this uncertainty.** This paper does the foundational work: (a) **compiles 547 measured KPUF/G values for 281 distinct chemicals** from 9 peer-reviewed studies; (b) **compares empirical values to polyparameter Linear Free Energy Relationship (ppLFER) predictions** to identify experimental-method bias; (c) **quantifies uncertainty propagation** from KPUF/G to breakthrough volumes (VB) + sampling rates (SR) + air concentrations (Cair); (d) **shows minimum 0.2 log-units uncertainty in log KPUF/G translates to ~50% minimum uncertainty in PAS-derived air concentrations** — even under best conditions; (e) **provides a 7-step procedure (Table 1) for estimating Cair uncertainty** that any PAS practitioner can follow; (f) **recommends Monte Carlo simulation over standard error propagation** when uncertainty in KPUF/G > 30% (which is most cases). **This is foundational methodology infrastructure for the entire PAS-PUF research community.**
- **ML task type:** **Not a primary ML paper, but the cleanest inherent ML setup of any Wania article** because chemical property prediction from molecular descriptors is a classical chemoinformatics task with direct ML benchmarks:
  - (1) **ML-based KPUF/G predictor (replacing ppLFER)** — train neural network or gradient-boosted regression on the compiled 547-measurement KPUF/G dataset using (chemical structure → log KPUF/G) mapping. Direct comparison to ppLFER baseline. **Standard chemoinformatics task** with direct match to recently popular property-prediction models (Chemprop, ATB-XGBoost, MolBERT). **Hot AI4Chemistry area.**
  - (2) **Bayesian / uncertainty-quantified property prediction** — train Bayesian neural network (PyMC) or Monte Carlo dropout regression on the compiled dataset to predict log KPUF/G with calibrated uncertainty estimates. **Directly extends the paper's uncertainty-quantification framework to ML.**
  - (3) **Bias-detection ML model** — train classifier to predict which experimental method is likely biased for a given chemical class (Harner method overestimates volatile chemicals; Tromp method underestimates less-volatile chemicals). Could plausibly extend to other PAS sorbent literature.
  - (4) **Monte Carlo uncertainty estimator for PAS-derived Cair** — implement the paper's 7-step Table 1 procedure as a Python package + web tool. **Real software-engineering portfolio piece** with industry/policy adjacency: Stockholm Convention compliance verification, REACH chemical registration, EPA chemical safety assessment.
  - (5) **Cross-paper synergy with Articles 1 + 2** — Article 1's GEM PAS used sulfur-impregnated activated carbon (different sorbent); Article 3's uncertainty framework directly applies via KSulfurAC/G replacing KPUF/G. **Strong cross-paper methodological coherence** within Wania's lab. Article 2's NEM model uses PCB partition coefficients from Li, Wania, Lei & Daly 2003 (cited Wania-co-authored PCB property compilation) — Article 3 is a sister compilation but for PUF partitioning. **Sustained 20+ year Wania-lab thread** on chemical property compilation + ppLFER + uncertainty for environmental modeling.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: tabular chemical-property + molecular-descriptor data; (chemical SMILES + Abraham descriptors A/B/S/V/L + temperature → log KPUF/G prediction)
  - Shape / size if stated: **547 KPUF/G values for 281 distinct chemicals** from 9 studies; chemicals span alkanes, alkenes, halogenated alkanes, alkanols, phenols, ketones, esters, aldehydes, ethers (incl. phthalates), alkylated/halogenated aromatics, organophosphates, anilines, nitriles, carboxylic acids, polyaromatics; temperatures span -1.9°C to 95°C (mostly 5-35°C for less-volatile compounds); log KPUF/G range 1-11
  - Preprocessing: unit conversion (volumetric → mass-based KPUF/G via PUF density correction); van't Hoff temperature correction to 15°C reference (Eq. 8); ppLFER prediction via Kamprad & Goss 2007 + Baskaran personal communication equations (Eq. 6, 7); solute descriptors from UFZ-LFER database; internal energies of phase transfer ΔUOA + ΔUPUF/G via Eq. 9, 10; standard error propagation (Eq. 11) + Monte Carlo simulation (Oracle Crystal Ball, 10,000 trials)
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: log KPUF/G (mL/g, range 1-11); uncertainty estimate σ(log KPUF/G) (range ~0.2-1.5); breakthrough volume VB (mL); sampling rate SR (m³/day); effective sampling volume Vair (m³); volumetric air concentration Cair (pg/m³)
  - Units / label space: continuous regression targets; standard PAS analytical chemistry units
- **Model architecture (brief):** No ML model in the paper. **ppLFER (polyparameter Linear Free Energy Relationship)** as the regression baseline: log KPUF/G(15°C) = 1.63·S + 3.39·A + 0.71·L + 0.27·V - 0.02 (Eq. 7). Standard error propagation + Monte Carlo as uncertainty methods. Statistical analysis: linear regression with 95% confidence intervals; van't Hoff plots for temperature dependence.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: **Compiled 547-measurement KPUF/G dataset** (THE main contribution of the paper)
  - Size: 547 KPUF/G values × 281 distinct chemicals × 9 source studies (Bidleman 2016; Harner/Francisco 2017 + Parnis 2016 + Saini 2019; Shoeib & Harner 2002; Tromp 2019; Park 2010; Zhao 2004; Kamprad & Goss 2007)
  - Source: Literature meta-analysis from peer-reviewed papers; **published as Electronic Supplementary Information (ESI)** in CSV/Excel format
  - Public? **YES — direct public release** in ESI under RSC license. **Best public-data substrate of any single article in the survey.**
  - Link: Article DOI: https://doi.org/10.1039/d1em00036e (RSC); ESI: supplementary materials accessible via DOI; **UFZ-LFER database**: http://www.ufz.de/lserd (Wania uses this directly for solute descriptors)
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **547 KPUF/G measurements compiled** for 281 chemicals from 9 peer-reviewed studies
  - **Minimum uncertainty in log KPUF/G = 0.2 log units** (e.g., for hexachlorobenzene at 15°C; n=13 measurements; standard deviation 0.18) → **~46% minimum relative error in VB and SR**
  - **Bias identification:** Harner method (Parnis/Francisco/Saini) overestimates KPUF/G for chemicals with predicted log KPUF/G between 5-7 (RMSE 0.91 vs. ppLFER); Tromp method underestimates KPUF/G for chemicals with predicted log KPUF/G > 9
  - **log KPUF/G correlates strongly with log KOA** at 15°C (R² = 0.978, slope = 0.946 ± 0.009) → justifies single-parameter LFER for non-polar compounds
  - **Monte Carlo vs. error propagation comparison:** for log KPUF/G between 5.8-8.6 (typical SVOC range), the two methods diverge by 20-40% → **Monte Carlo recommended when uncertainty > 30%**
  - **Bottom line: PAS-derived Cair has minimum 50% uncertainty** even under best conditions (good KPUF/G data + good analytical procedures + ideal sampling assumptions)
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **The compiled 547-measurement KPUF/G dataset itself** — published as ESI in this paper. Match: **direct** (this IS the dataset). Access: **easy** — RSC ESI download. **Best public-data substrate of any single article in the survey.**
  - **UFZ-LFER database** (Ulrich, Brown, Watanabe, Bronner, Abraham, Goss) — public solute-descriptor database for ppLFER applications. Match: **direct** (Wania uses this in Article 3). Access: **easy** — http://www.ufz.de/lserd
  - **CompTox Chemicals Dashboard (US EPA)** — public chemical property database with 1M+ chemicals, OPERA predicted properties, Abraham descriptors. Match: **direct** for the molecular-descriptor side. Access: **easy** — https://comptox.epa.gov/dashboard/
  - **OPERA (OPEn structure-activity Relationship App)** — Mansouri et al. public chemical property prediction models including KOA + KOW. Match: **close** (sister property-prediction tool). Access: **easy** — https://github.com/NIEHS/OPERA
  - **PubChem / ChEMBL** — public chemical structures + descriptors. Access: **easy**
  - **EPI Suite (US EPA Estimation Programs Interface)** — public chemical property estimation suite. Access: **easy** — https://www.epa.gov/tsca-screening-tools/epi-suitetm-estimation-program-interface
  - **iSafeRat** — chemical property database. Access: **medium**
  - **Wania & Shunthirasingham 2020 *ESPI* "Passive Air Sampling for Semi-Volatile Organic Chemicals"** — Wania-FIRST-authored sister review paper, cited 14× in this paper. Match: **direct** (Wania-lab foundational review). Access: **medium** (paywalled but RSC-OA option available)
  - **Li, Wania, Lei & Daly 2003 *J. Phys. Chem. Ref. Data*** "A comprehensive and critical compilation, evaluation, and selection of physical-chemical property data for selected polychlorinated biphenyls" — Wania-co-authored PCB compilation cited in Article 2 (used as input to NEM for PCB-153 KOW/KOA/KAW). Match: **direct** (sister compilation paper). Access: **medium** (paywalled AIP)
  - **GAPS (Global Atmospheric Passive Sampling) network** — Pozo et al. 2006; Herkert et al. 2018 cited; same network referenced in Articles 1 and 2. Direct PAS-PUF deployment data. Access: **easy**
  - **Bidleman & Melymuk 2019 "Forty-Five Years of Foam"** — retrospective on 45 years of PUF-based air sampling. Match: **direct** (field-history review). Access: **easy** (Bull. Environ. Contam. Toxicol. open access)
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Easy.** Compiled 547-measurement dataset is **directly published as ESI** under RSC license. UFZ-LFER + CompTox + OPERA + EPI Suite all freely accessible. **Best data accessibility of any single article in the survey** — the entire dataset is one CSV download away.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:**
  - Already-known: pandas / numpy / sklearn / scipy / matplotlib + RDKit (chemistry-undergrad-standard)
  - Light additions: **scipy.stats.qmc + scipy.stats** for Monte Carlo simulation in pure Python (free alternative to Oracle Crystal Ball that the paper uses); **statsmodels** for linear regression with confidence intervals (replicates Fig. 4-5 of the paper)
  - Optional advanced: **PyMC** for Bayesian uncertainty quantification; **Chemprop / DeepChem / MolBERT** for ML-based property prediction (PyTorch-based, well-documented); **Mordred** for additional molecular descriptors beyond Abraham
  - **No new ecosystem lift** — entire toolkit is chemistry-undergrad-accessible standard Python data science
  - Domain reading: Endo & Goss 2014 *Environ. Sci. Technol.* "Applications of Polyparameter Linear Free Energy Relationships in Environmental Chemistry"; Shoeib & Harner 2002 *ESC&T* (foundational PUF-PAS paper, cited 7× in Article 3); Wania & Shunthirasingham 2020 *ESPI* "Passive Air Sampling for Semi-Volatile Organic Chemicals" (Wania-FIRST sister review, cited 14× in Article 3); Bidleman & Melymuk 2019 (45-year PUF retrospective). **Domain accessible to chemistry undergrads with a few weeks of physical-organic chemistry + chemoinformatics reading.**
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project would look like:**

## Joshua L. Pulsipher

**Affiliation:** **University of Waterloo, Department of Chemical Engineering** (200 University Ave W, Waterloo, ON N2L 3G1). **First non-U-of-T professor in the survey** — the user mentioned "University of Toronto" but the path + the article's author affiliation clearly indicate **UWaterloo**. ~1.5 hour drive west of downtown Toronto (or via GO Transit + commuter); meaningful campus-distance flag for a Toronto-based applicant.

**Lab focus (one sentence):** **Confirmed across three articles**: **physics-informed machine learning + nonlinear optimization for chemical engineering process control** — spanning data-driven sensor analysis (Article 1, PCA + K-means + S-PLS for fire safety), supply chain optimization (Article 2, multiperiod MILP + GDP collaboration with CMU Grossmann/Laird group), and physics-informed neural networks for nonlinear model predictive control (Article 3, PINN/PICNN + NMPC for plug-flow reactors). **Pulsipher is also the developer of InfiniteOpt.jl** (cited in Article 3's introduction) — a Julia package for infinite-dimensional optimization that underpins the lab's research program.

**Lab webpage / Scholar:** Not yet provided. Email per Article 1: pulsipher@uwaterloo.ca. Search UWaterloo Chemical Engineering faculty directory + Google Scholar for "Joshua L. Pulsipher" or "Joshua Pulsipher chemical engineering Waterloo."

### Article 1: Data-Driven Framework for High-Dimensional Fire Sensor Analysis (PCA + K-means + S-PLS on Compartment Fire Data)

- **Full citation:** Mazzadi, S., Lakhani, A., Gupta, V., Weckman, B., & **Pulsipher, J. L.** (2026). A framework for high-dimensional fire sensor data analysis. *Fire Safety Journal*, 163, 104738, 10 pages. DOI: 10.1016/j.firesaf.2026.104738. Received 25 September 2025; accepted 15 March 2026; published online 24 March 2026. **CC BY 4.0 Open Access** — Elsevier. Part of the *FISJ_IAFSS 2026* special issue. **Funded by NSERC Discovery Grants + Tri-Council New Frontiers Research Fund + Ontario Graduate Scholarships + UWaterloo Faculty of Engineering + American Chemistry Council + City of Calgary + BC Housing.**
- **Authorship signal:** Pulsipher is **last + corresponding author**. Mazzadi (first author) is a PhD/Master's student in Pulsipher's ChemE group. Co-authors Lakhani + Gupta + Weckman are from UWaterloo Department of Mechanical and Mechatronics Engineering (the experimental side — Beth Weckman runs the burn-house facility per cited Forrest et al. 2023 + Lakhani et al. 2024 predecessors). **Strongest PI signal class.** **Multi-departmental UWaterloo collaboration** (ChemE + MechEng) signals interdisciplinary engineering culture typical of UWaterloo.
- **Problem in plain English:** Modern energy-efficient residential buildings have **two compounding fire-safety challenges**: (1) airtight construction reduces natural ventilation → fires become under-ventilated faster → produce more toxic gases; (2) modern furnishings use polyurethane foam + synthetic fabrics instead of legacy cellulosics → produce different/worse gaseous toxins. **Characterizing fire dynamics + toxicity in such fires** is technically challenging because numerous quantities must be measured simultaneously across many spatial locations: gas concentrations of 10+ species (O₂, CO, CO₂, NO, NO₂, HCN, HCl, CH₄, NH₃, VOCs) at 19 stations × 3 heights, plus temperatures + fuel mass loss rate (MLR) + air exchange. **The resulting datasets are high-dimensional time-series**, and existing zone-model approaches simplify by averaging over space/time, **losing valuable information** about flame spread, burning rates, species yields, wall heat transfer. The paper introduces a **3-step data-driven framework** to extract physically meaningful trends from such high-dimensional fire datasets without lossy averaging: **(1) PCA** for initial sensor screening + identification of faulty sensors + visualization of fire trajectory in 2D latent space; **(2) K-means clustering** on PCA latent space to segment fire timeline into physically meaningful stages (ignition/growth, fully-developed/peak, decay); **(3) Sparse Partial Least Squares (S-PLS) regression** within each stage to identify which gas species are most predictive of MLR, revealing stage-specific combustion chemistry. Applied to a 75 kg polyurethane couch fire in UWaterloo's two-storey burn house with no external ventilation (replicates modern airtight Canadian residence).
- **ML task type:** **PRIMARY ML / data-science paper** — the entire framework IS the methods contribution. Specific tasks:
  - (1) **Unsupervised dimensionality reduction** (PCA) on multi-modal fire sensor data → 2D latent space retaining 89% of variance; faulty-sensor outlier detection
  - (2) **Unsupervised clustering** (K-means, n_c=3 via elbow plot) on PCA latent space → 3 fire stages with physically meaningful breakpoints (transition #1 at 360.86 s = peak burning rate; transition #2 at 487.61 s = decay onset)
  - (3) **Supervised sparse regression** (S-PLS) per cluster → R² = 0.96/0.90/0.91 vs. global model R² = 0.59 (stage-specific S-PLS substantially outperforms global S-PLS)
  - **Cleanest inherent ML structure of any single-article professor entry in the survey** — the article is itself a methods paper with explicit ML algorithms applied to a real engineering problem
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: multi-modal multi-station time-series (10 gas species + thermocouples + load cells); time-resolved at sub-second sampling; spatial-resolved at 19 GIS stations × 3 heights × 7 locations
  - Shape / size if stated: ~1375 seconds × ~10 features (gas species + MLR) = ~13,750 datapoint matrix per sensor station; 19 GIS stations × 3 heights = 57 stations total (paper uses subset for 1 MV experiment); 12 experiments in the broader campaign with different fuel loads + ventilation conditions
  - Preprocessing: standardization (zero-mean unit-variance scaling); CO₂ sensor data excluded due to operational-range saturation (10,000 ppm limit); pre-ignition + post-fire-recovery time intervals truncated; HCl sensor excluded due to unknown cross-sensitivity / thermal shock
- **Output representation:**
  - What the model predicts: PCA latent variables (2D); cluster labels (1, 2, or 3 corresponding to ignition/growth, peak/early-decay, decay); MLR (kg/s, range 0-0.122); per-cluster S-PLS loadings Q^(i) ∈ R^{n_x-1×ℓ} encoding stage-specific gas-species-to-MLR relationships
  - Units / label space: continuous regression for MLR; categorical for cluster assignments; sparse linear coefficients for S-PLS loadings
- **Model architecture (brief):** **PCA**: eigendecomposition of empirical covariance matrix (Σ ∈ R^{n_x × n_x}); top-2 eigenvectors define latent space. **K-means**: minimize within-cluster variance with n_c=3 via Lloyd's algorithm; elbow plot for n_c selection. **S-PLS**: linear regression in sparse-loading-projected latent space; ℓ=2 components per cluster. **Implementations**: scikit-learn (Python) for PCA + K-means; spls package (R) for S-PLS.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: UWaterloo Burn House Test 1 MV (one of 12 experiments in the broader campaign)
  - Size: 1 fire experiment × 1375 s × 19 GIS × 3 heights × 10+ gas species + 4 load cells + 20+ thermocouples
  - Source: UWaterloo Burn House two-storey steel structure (Lakhani et al. 2024 cited; Forrest et al. 2023 cited as foundational paper); 75 kg polyurethane couch (CAL TB 117-2013, no flame retardants); BS 5852 wood crib ignition; sealed structure (no ventilation) until self-extinction
  - Public? **Partial** — paper data shown in figures (digitizable); raw experimental data on request likely (Beth Weckman lab); methodology fully documented and reproducible via scikit-learn + spls
  - Link: Article DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.firesaf.2026.104738 (Elsevier, **CC BY 4.0 Open Access**)
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **PCA latent space retains 89% of variance** in 2D representation
  - **3-stage segmentation** (n_c=3 via elbow plot): Cluster 1 (ignition/growth, 0-361 s); Cluster 2 (peak/early-decay, 361-488 s); Cluster 3 (decay, 488-1375 s); transition between Cluster 1 and 2 **precisely coincides with peak heat release rate**
  - **Cluster-specific S-PLS R²: 0.96 / 0.90 / 0.91** for clusters 1/2/3 vs. **global S-PLS R² = 0.59** — clear win for stage-specific modeling (~50% improvement in predictive accuracy)
  - **Stage-specific gas-species-to-MLR correlations**: Cluster 1 dominated by VOC (loading 0.985) + O₂ (-0.626); Cluster 2 dominated by O₂ (0.781) + NO₂ (-0.624) + VOC (0.798) + HCN (0.603); Cluster 3 dominated by VOC (0.913) + NO₂ (0.856); **physically interpretable** in terms of polyurethane combustion chemistry (NO from N-fuel oxidation; HCN from partial oxidation; NH₃ as global fire indicator)
  - **PCA-based outlier detection identified CO₂ sensor saturation** (sensor exceeded 10,000 ppm operational limit) as a clear outlier in PC2 — demonstrates framework's utility for sensor-fault detection
  - **Closed-loop trajectory in PCA latent space** confirms sensor recovery to baseline after fire — provides validation of sensor reliability + framework consistency
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**

  - **NIST Fire Calorimetry Database** — public cone calorimeter + room burn data. Match: **close**. Access: **easy** — https://www.nist.gov/programs-projects/fire-calorimetry-database-fcd
  - **IAFSS (International Association of Fire Safety Science) datasets** — various publicly-available IAFSS-affiliated fire research datasets. Match: **close**. Access: **medium** (some via member archives)
  - **Forrest et al. 2023 *Fire Saf. J.*** "Towards characterizing full-scale furniture fires in a two-storey house: Gaseous species concentrations during a ventilation-limited fire" — same UWaterloo Burn House facility, foundational paper for Article 1's dataset. Match: **direct** (same experimental facility). Access: **easy** (Elsevier; partial open access)
  - **Lakhani, DiPaola, Senez, Weckman 2024 *J. Phys. Conf. Ser.*** — sister paper from same UWaterloo group on impact of oxygen concentrations on fire development. Match: **direct**. Access: **easy** (CC BY)
  - **Solórzano et al. 2022 *Sensors Actuators B*** "Early fire detection based on gas sensor arrays" — sister paper using PCA + ML for fire detection. Match: **adjacent** (different sensor configuration but similar methodology). Access: **medium** (Elsevier)
  - **scikit-learn + spls (R) package implementations** — directly cited in the paper. Match: **direct** (same software stack). Access: **easy** — open-source Python/R packages

**Article 1 overall score:** Provisional (7 of 8 variables): (5 + 5 + 9 + 9 + 8 + 7 + 7) / 7 = 50 / 7 = **7.1 / 10**

**Article 1 verdict:** A **strong primary-research data-science paper from Pulsipher's own lab** (last + corresponding author with explicit Conceptualization + Funding-acquisition CRediT contributions, PhD student first author, NSERC + multi-source funding). **The cleanest inherent ML structure of any single-article professor entry in the survey** — the article IS a methods paper with explicit PCA + K-means + S-PLS algorithms applied to a real engineering problem. **Strongest portfolio angle: direct framework replication on NIST Fire Calorimetry Database compartment fire data** — uses public data + replicates a published ML methodology + extends to cross-dataset benchmarking. **Strongest cross-survey synergy: Pulsipher + Galatro** (both data-driven multivariate analysis for engineering-system diagnostics; complementary application domains — fire vs. Li-ion battery). **For a chemistry-undergrad master's-prospect with data-science / applied-ML / chemical-engineering interests**, **Pulsipher is genuinely strong on this single article** + adds geographic diversity (UWaterloo vs. all-U-of-T survey). **However, the UWaterloo affiliation is a real constraint for a Toronto-based applicant** — ~1.5 hour drive west; commuting feasibility / relocation question to consider.

### Article 2: Multiperiod MILP for Supply Chain Disruption Response (CMU-Led Collaboration with The Dow Chemical Company)

- **Full citation:** Ovalle, D., **Pulsipher, J. L.**, Ye, Y., Harshbarger, K., Bury, S., Laird, C. D., & Grossmann, I. E. (2025). Optimal reactive operation of general topology supply chain and manufacturing networks under disruptions. *AIChE Journal*, 71(7), e18833, 23 pages. DOI: 10.1002/aic.18833. Received 26 November 2024; revised 19 February 2025; accepted 16 March 2025. **CC BY-NC-ND open access** — Wiley Periodicals on behalf of AIChE. **Funded by Center for Advanced Process Decision-making (CAPD) + Department of Chemical Engineering, Carnegie Mellon University.**
- **Authorship signal:** Pulsipher is **2nd author of 7, NOT corresponding**. **Grossmann at CMU is corresponding**; **Ovalle (CMU PhD student under Grossmann/Laird) is first author**. Pulsipher's CRediT contributions: *"writing – review and editing; conceptualization"* — a **collaborative/secondary** PI signal, meaningfully weaker than Article 1's last+corresponding role. **However**, Pulsipher has an **established multi-paper collaborative thread with the CMU Grossmann/Laird group**: the paper cites Ovalle, Vyas, Laird, Grossmann 2024 (ML surrogates for plant scheduling feasibility) + Ovalle, Liñán, Lee et al. 2025 (logic-based discrete-steepest descent) + **Ovalle, Mazzadi, Laird, Grossmann, Pulsipher 2025 *"Event constrained programming"*** (preprint arXiv:2501.06353) — Mazzadi is the same PhD student who first-authored Pulsipher's Article 1. **Cross-thread signal**: Pulsipher's UWaterloo lab is networked into the CMU PSE community + maintains a process-systems-engineering / optimization research thread alongside the fire-safety data-science thread from Article 1.
- **Problem in plain English:** Chemical-industry supply chains (raw-material suppliers → plants → warehouses → customers, with arbitrary topology — material can loop back, plants can have both internal and external demand, multiple transportation modes per arc) are vulnerable to **disruptions**: supplier shortages, plant equipment failures, route closures, logistics-resource limitations, market shocks like COVID-19. When a disruption occurs, decision-makers need to **react optimally and quickly** — adjusting production, shipment, procurement, and order-management decisions (deliver on time / deliver late / cancel) to minimize the financial impact. **Existing approaches** either (a) focus on long-term resilient network design rather than short-term operational response, or (b) provide fixed inventory policies that don't adapt to specific disruption scenarios. The paper introduces a **multiperiod mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) model** that integrates production scheduling, shipping, procurement, and order management for **arbitrary-topology** networks under disruption, using **Generalized Disjunctive Programming (GDP)** as the modeling framework with **big-M MILP reformulation** for solution. Demonstrated on a **silicone rubber supply chain from The Dow Chemical Company** (10 products, 2 raw-material suppliers, 2 plants, 2 warehouses, 9 customers, 120-day horizon at 12-hour discretization).
- **ML task type:** **NOT primary ML** — this is **mathematical optimization** (multiperiod MILP + GDP). However, the paper's future-work section explicitly cites ML integration via **Ovalle/Pulsipher group's own 2024 paper** on ML surrogates for scheduling feasibility + **Badejo & Ierapetritou 2022** on data-driven feasibility analysis. **ML reframings** required for portfolio:
  - **ML surrogate for the MILP solution** — train an XGBoost or neural-net regressor (or imitation-learning policy) to predict optimal response variables (production schedule, shipment quantities, cancellations) from disruption parameters (which arc/plant disrupted, severity, duration); this directly mirrors Ovalle/Pulsipher 2024
  - **Reinforcement learning for supply chain disruption response** — MILP solutions become "expert demonstrations" for behavioral cloning or inverse RL; train a policy that responds in real-time to disruptions
  - **Time-series demand forecasting / disruption classification** — predict disruption type/severity from multivariate supply-chain telemetry (analogous to Pulsipher's Article 1 PCA framework applied to supply-chain sensor signals instead of fire sensors)
  - **Graph neural networks on supply-chain network** — use GNNs to predict disruption propagation through arbitrary-topology networks; relevant to the paper's emphasis on "non-feed-forward" supply chain topologies
  - **Note**: the paper's case study + sensitivity analysis already runs **10,200+ disrupted scenarios** to generate a Profit-vs-(plant capacity, disruption duration) surface — this is a **ready-made tabular dataset** for ML surrogate training
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (paper's MILP): tabular **structured data** — sets (suppliers, plants, warehouses, customers, materials, recipes, time periods), arcs (origin × destination × material × transport mode), parameters (production coefficients φ, capacities, costs, shipment durations τ, initial inventories, disruption profiles)
  - Modality (ML reframings): tabular features describing disruption scenarios (disrupted node ID, disruption type, severity fraction, duration fraction, time of onset) → tabular outputs OR sequence outputs (production schedule per material per time period); for GNN reframe: graph-structured supply-chain network with node + edge features
  - Shape / size: 17,345 continuous variables + 497 binary variables + 15,480 constraints for the base 120-day case; up to **266K continuous + 98K binary + 377K constraints** for the largest model in time-scalability table; 50 replicas × 48 model variants × multiple time-horizon lengths = thousands of solved instances available via the open repo
  - Preprocessing: standard MILP preprocessing in Pyomo (variable bounds tightening, big-M reformulation of disjunctions, recipe-based bill-of-materials encoding); for ML surrogate training: standard scikit-learn-style scaling + train/val/test split
- **Output representation:**
  - Paper's MILP: optimal schedule = continuous flows F_in/F_out per arc per time period + production levels P per recipe per plant per time + inventory levels I per material per node per time + binary order-cancellation y per material per customer per time + objective = profit minus costs
  - ML reframings: regression to predict profit / number of cancellations / total shipments; classification for cancellation decisions y; sequence-output for time-resolved schedules
  - Units / label space: kg for material flows; units/day for production; $ for profit; binary for cancellations
- **Model architecture (brief):** **Multiperiod MILP** formulated via **Generalized Disjunctive Programming (GDP)** with disjunctions for (i) order cancellation, (ii) fixed-transportation-cost arc activation, (iii) service-level-agreement minimum purchase, (iv) negative-inventory deviation as a ReLU-style penalty. **Big-M reformulation** to standard MILP solved with **Gurobi v9.5.1** via **Pyomo** (Python). For the ML reframings: standard sklearn / PyTorch surrogate models trained on MILP-solution datasets.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: **The Dow Chemical Company's silicone rubber business** (anonymized) — 10 products (DP, Fluid F, Blend A/B/C/C1, SR 1-4) + 2 raw materials + 2 plants + 2 warehouses + 9 customers + 120-day horizon
  - Size: ~17K-377K decision variables per problem instance; 50 replicas × 48 model variants × multiple horizons = thousands of solved instances
  - Source: anonymized industrial data from Dow Chemical Company; **sample data + source code released** at https://github.com/dovallev/supply_chain_disruptions
  - Public? **Code is fully public** (GitHub MIT license likely, **CC BY-NC-ND for the paper itself**); **sample illustrative data is public**; actual Dow proprietary numbers are anonymized but the structure + methodology are reproducible
  - Link: **GitHub: https://github.com/dovallev/supply_chain_disruptions** + paper DOI: https://doi.org/10.1002/aic.18833
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **Solution time: 0.23 s for the base disrupted case** (17,345 continuous + 497 binary + 15,480 constraints); scales to **9 minutes average** for the largest 1500-period instance (98K binary variables)
  - **Time-scalability summary table**: min 25 / max 98,231 binary vars; min 1199 / max 266,666 continuous vars; min 898 / max 377,889 constraints; min 2421 / max 881,549 non-zeros — model proves **scalable across 4 orders of magnitude** in problem size
  - **Sensitivity analysis** on cancellation cost λ^δ + late-delivery cost λ^U identifies cost regions where the model prefers (a) all-late-delivery, (b) all-cancellation, (c) mixed strategies — gives decision-makers a **"what-if" tool** for cost-parameter calibration
  - **Disruption-characterization plateau**: when plant capacity is near-undisrupted OR disruption duration is near-zero, the model recovers near-undisrupted profit — confirms the framework correctly handles boundary cases
  - **Demonstrated scenarios**: plant equipment failure (2 of 3 reactors at Plant 1, 2-month duration) + complete route closure (Plant 1 → Warehouse 1 trucking blocked for 1 month) — both yield **integrated, network-wide reactive responses** (e.g., switching to airfreight for downstream catch-up; smaller-more-frequent shipments in disrupted scenarios)
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Paper's own GitHub repo** (https://github.com/dovallev/supply_chain_disruptions) — sample data + Pyomo source code released. Match: **direct, perfect** for replicating the paper's MILP. Access: **easy** — clone + `pip install pyomo gurobipy` (Gurobi requires academic license — free for students)
  - **DataCo SMART SUPPLY CHAIN dataset** (Kaggle, ~180K supply chain transactions with order history + delays + cancellations). Match: **loose** (real e-commerce supply chain, not chemical industry, but rich enough for ML surrogate training). Access: **easy** — https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/shashwatwork/dataco-smart-supply-chain-for-big-data-analysis
  - **SupplyGraph** (Wasi et al., AAAI 2024) — **graph-structured supply chain dataset** designed for graph ML. Match: **close** for the GNN reframing. Access: **easy** — https://github.com/CIOL-SUST/SupplyGraph
  - **Brazilian E-Commerce / Olist dataset** (Kaggle, 100K orders with delivery times + statuses). Match: **loose** but useful for delivery-time prediction. Access: **easy** — https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/olistbr/brazilian-ecommerce
  - **MIPLIB 2017** — standard MILP benchmark library; useful for "learning to optimize" / "learning to branch" research extensions. Match: **adjacent** (MILP problems but not supply-chain specific). Access: **easy** — https://miplib.zib.de/
  - **OR-Library** (Beasley) — classical operations research benchmarks including supply chain + scheduling instances. Match: **adjacent**. Access: **easy** — https://people.brunel.ac.uk/~mastjjb/jeb/info.html
  - **Synthetic data generation via paper's repo** — modify the sample Pyomo model to generate thousands of (disruption parameter → optimal response) pairs as a custom training dataset. Match: **direct, after construction**. Access: **medium** (requires Pyomo + Gurobi setup but everything is open-source)
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Easy.** GitHub repo is open with sample data + full Pyomo source code; CC BY-NC-ND open access for the paper itself. **Better than typical industrial-collaboration papers** — Dow's actual numbers are anonymized but the structure + methodology are fully reproducible.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:**
  - **Pyomo** — Python algebraic modeling language for MILP/MINLP/GDP — substantial new lift, **not in current toolkit**
  - **Gurobi** (or open-source CBC / HiGHS / SCIP) — commercial MILP solver; **free academic license** — minor setup lift
  - **GDP (Generalized Disjunctive Programming)** — a Pyomo extension for disjunctive constraints — minor additional lift on top of Pyomo
  - For ML surrogate reframing: already-known sklearn / PyTorch / XGBoost — covers the surrogate training side
  - For RL reframing: **Stable-Baselines3** (PyTorch) or **CleanRL** — substantial new lift if pursuing this angle
  - For GNN reframing: **PyTorch Geometric** or **DGL** — already flagged in earlier professors' projects (Sánchez-Lengeling Article 1, Mahadevan Article 1)
  - **Substantial overall new tooling lift** — Pyomo + Gurobi + GDP are NEW; this is the most significant tooling change of any single article in the Pulsipher entry
  - Domain reading: Grossmann & Trespalacios 2013 *AIChE J.* (GDP foundations); Trespalacios & Grossmann 2015 *Comput. Chem. Eng.* (improved big-M reformulation); Bynum, Hackebeil, Hart et al. 2021 *Pyomo book*; Klibi, Martel, Guitouni 2010 *Eur. J. Oper. Res.* (resilient supply chain network design — the OR community foundational review)
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project would look like:**
  - **Strongest single framing — ML surrogate for MILP solutions:** *"Training an XGBoost surrogate for optimal supply chain disruption response — a Pyomo + sklearn pipeline based on Ovalle et al. 2025."* Use the paper's open-source GitHub repo to generate thousands of (disruption parameter → optimal profit / cancellations / shipment count) tuples; train an XGBoost regressor; benchmark surrogate accuracy vs. MILP exact solution. **Recruiter-legible** for ML-for-OR / industrial-AI roles. Most parallel to **Ovalle/Vyas/Laird/Grossmann 2024** (the same group's ML surrogate paper cited as future work).
  - **Stretch — GNN for disruption propagation prediction:** *"Graph neural networks for supply chain disruption propagation — a SupplyGraph + Pyomo benchmark."* Use SupplyGraph + custom synthetic data from the Ovalle 2025 repo to train a GNN that predicts which downstream nodes get affected + by how much, given a disrupted source node. **Hot ML-for-OR area** (NeurIPS / ICML have growing GNN-for-supply-chain tracks). Substantial tooling lift but high portfolio impact.
  - **Light/replication — Pyomo MILP replication on DataCo dataset:** *"Reformulating the DataCo Smart Supply Chain Kaggle dataset as a multiperiod MILP — a Pyomo + Gurobi implementation of Ovalle et al. 2025."* Take real Kaggle e-commerce supply chain data, construct an arbitrary-topology MILP per the paper's framework, solve disruption scenarios. **Demonstrates OR/optimization skills** for industrial-engineering / process-systems-engineering roles. Good complement to a more ML-heavy portfolio.
  - **Cross-survey synergy: Pulsipher A1 + Pulsipher A2 unified portfolio:** *"PCA-based sensor analysis (fire-safety) + MILP-based optimal response (supply-chain disruption) — a unified Pulsipher data-driven engineering portfolio."* Combine Article 1's PCA + clustering framework (anomaly/disruption detection) with Article 2's MILP (optimal response) into a **single end-to-end "detect-then-respond" pipeline**. **Differentiated portfolio framing** that demonstrates Pulsipher-lab-aligned chemical-engineering data science thread end-to-end.
  - **Cross-survey synergy: Pulsipher A2 + Galatro A2 (battery) + Wania A2 (NEM):** *"MILP-based optimal response across three engineering systems — supply chain (Pulsipher), battery degradation (Galatro), POP transport (Wania)."* Aspirational unified portfolio — implements MILP on three different engineering systems to demonstrate generality. Highest difficulty + highest portfolio impact.
