**Article 2 verdict:** A **collaborative-author paper from a strong CMU-led PSE thread** (Pulsipher 2nd author, Grossmann corresponding) that confirms Pulsipher maintains a **process-systems-engineering / optimization research thread** alongside the fire-safety data-science thread of Article 1. **Reproducibility is exceptional** (open GitHub repo + CC BY-NC-ND) — among the strongest in the survey — but **tooling alignment is the weakest of any Pulsipher article** because Pyomo + Gurobi + GDP are entirely new ground for a chemistry-undergrad with PyTorch/sklearn skills. **Best portfolio framing: ML surrogate for MILP solutions** — preserves familiar sklearn/PyTorch tooling while leveraging the paper's open-source MILP infrastructure for synthetic data generation, directly mirroring **Ovalle/Pulsipher 2024 ML-surrogates paper**. **The ML reframing requirement makes this a meaningfully weaker portfolio fit than Article 1**, where the ML structure was inherent — Article 2 is an OR paper that *can* be reframed as ML, not an ML paper itself. **Authorship signal is meaningfully weaker than Article 1** (2nd author + collaborative vs. last + corresponding) — the paper signals Pulsipher's network into the CMU PSE community more than primary research from his own UWaterloo group.

### Article 3: Embedding Physics-Informed Neural Networks in Nonlinear Model Predictive Control (The Unifying Pulsipher-Lab Paper — PINNs + PICNNs + NMPC)

- **Full citation:** Elorza Casas, C. A., Ricardez-Sandoval, L. A., & **Pulsipher, J. L.** (2025). A comparison of strategies to embed physics-informed neural networks in nonlinear model predictive control formulations solved via direct transcription. *Computers and Chemical Engineering*, 198, 109105, 13 pages. DOI: 10.1016/j.compchemeng.2025.109105. Received 31 December 2024; revised 13 March 2025; accepted 17 March 2025; available online 18 March 2025. **CC BY 4.0 Open Access** — Elsevier. **Funded by University of Waterloo Engineering Excellence Master's Fellowship + NSERC.**
- **Authorship signal:** **Pulsipher is LAST + CORRESPONDING author** (email pulsipher@uwaterloo.ca confirmed in footnote). **Ricardez-Sandoval is co-supervisor** (UWaterloo ChemE colleague — both list overlapping CRediT contributions: Supervision + Project administration + Conceptualization + Formal analysis); first author **Elorza Casas is a Master's student** funded by the UWaterloo Engineering Excellence Master's Fellowship. **Strong PI signal class — matches Article 1's last+corresponding role**. **Co-supervised paper** with Ricardez-Sandoval (himself a senior UWaterloo ChemE faculty in process control) signals Pulsipher's integration into the UWaterloo ChemE PSE/control community alongside an established collaborator. **Cross-article connection**: this article's introduction cites Pulsipher's own 2022 *Computers and Chemical Engineering* paper "A unifying modeling abstraction for infinite-dimensional optimization" (Pulsipher, Zhang, Hongisto, Zavala 2022) — confirming **InfiniteOpt.jl** as Pulsipher's foundational research thread.
- **Problem in plain English:** **Nonlinear Model Predictive Control (NMPC)** is a powerful chemical-process-control method that solves an optimization problem at every sampling interval to choose the best control actions over a future prediction horizon. **For systems described by partial differential equations (PDEs)** — like plug-flow reactors, distillation columns, packed beds, fluid-flow systems — the optimization problem becomes computationally expensive because the PDEs must be discretized into thousands of algebraic constraints. **Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs)** are a hot ML technique that can serve as fast surrogates for these PDEs by embedding the PDE residual into the NN training loss function — they learn to predict the PDE solution without needing to solve it from scratch each time. **The open question this paper answers**: *How* exactly should you embed a trained PINN into the NMPC optimization problem? Two strategies exist: **(1) Explicit Constraint Embedding (ECE)** — translate the NN's algebra (matrix multiplications + activation functions) into algebraic constraints inside the optimization model itself, letting the algebraic modeling language (Pyomo) compute gradients via its own automatic differentiation; **(2) External Function Embedding (EFE)** — keep the NN external (in PyTorch) and let the PyTorch autodiff compute gradients, passing them back to the optimizer as black-box function evaluations. **The paper benchmarks ECE vs. EFE on three increasingly complex plug-flow reactor (PFR) chemical engineering benchmarks** — 1D isothermal PFR, non-isothermal PFR, and methane steam reforming PFR — using both PINNs (feedforward NNs) and PICNNs (physics-informed convolutional NNs) as surrogates.
- **ML task type:** **PRIMARY ML / OPTIMIZATION RESEARCH PAPER** — no ML reframing needed. Specific tasks:
  - (1) **Train physics-informed neural networks** (PINNs based on FNNs + PICNNs based on 1D CNNs) on PDE residuals from PFR mechanistic models — a direct application of the **Raissi, Perdikaris, Karniadakis 2019 PINN framework** (foundational PINN paper) extended to chemical engineering PFR systems
  - (2) **Embed trained NN surrogates into NMPC optimization** via three strategies: ECE-FS (full-space), ECE-RS (reduced-space), EFE (external function via PyTorch autodiff)
  - (3) **Closed-loop control simulation** of the PFR setpoint-tracking problem to benchmark wall-clock time, problem size, and trajectory consistency
  - **The article spans the entire Pulsipher-lab research direction**: data-driven ML (Article 1) + nonlinear optimization (Article 2) + neural networks for engineering systems = **physics-informed ML + nonlinear optimization for chemical engineering process control**
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality (PINN/PICNN training): tabular state vectors x̂_k ∈ ℝ^{n_x × n_fe} (PFR concentrations + temperatures at finite-element discretization points) + control vectors û_k ∈ ℝ^{n_u} (PFR inlet concentration + flow rate + jacket temperature) → next-step state x̂_{k+1}; **physics-informed loss** penalizes the residual of the discretized PDE evaluated on (x̂_k, û_k, x̂_{k+1})
  - Modality (NMPC closed-loop): state trajectories from the plant model (mechanistic PDE simulated via Scipy `solve_ivp` with Runge-Kutta 5(4)) at each sampling step, fed to the NMPC controller
  - Shape / size: 100,000 training samples per benchmark; PINN architecture: feedforward NN with 3,562 parameters (Benchmark 1); PICNN architectures: 5,834 / 7,508 / 337,562 parameters for Benchmarks 1/2/3 respectively; NMPC formulation has 430 (EFE/PINN) to 327,120 (ECE-FS/PICNN-Benchmark-3) decision variables
  - Preprocessing: uniform random sampling of state and control variables over their feasible sets X and U; backward finite-difference spatial discretization; PDE residuals computed at training-time via the discretized differential operator
- **Output representation:**
  - PINN/PICNN: predicted next-step state vector x̂_{k+1} given current state x̂_k and control û_k
  - NMPC: optimal control trajectory {û_0, û_1, …, û_M} over the control horizon M, predicted state trajectory over the prediction horizon P
  - Units: chemical-engineering units (mol/L for concentration, K for temperature, mol/s for flow rate)
- **Model architecture (brief):** **PINN** = feedforward NN with smooth tanh/sigmoid activations + state-vector flattening (loses spatial structure) + L hidden layers + dense output layer reshape; **PICNN** = 1D convolutional NN with 32 channels + filter size 4 + smooth activations + Conv1d layers + flatten + dense output layer reshape (preserves spatial structure). **Training optimizer**: ADAM with initial learning rate 0.01 + exponential decay 0.7 every 100 epochs (PyTorch 2.0.1, 50,000 epochs for Benchmarks 1+2; 17,500 for Benchmark 3 due to compute cost). **NMPC solver**: IPOPT 3.14.9 via Pyomo 6.5.0 + OMLT 1.1 (for ECE) + PyNumero 1.3 (for EFE).
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: synthetic chemical-engineering plug-flow reactor data — Benchmark 1 (1D isothermal PFR), Benchmark 2 (non-isothermal 1D PFR), Benchmark 3 (methane steam reforming PFR)
  - Size: 100,000 uniform random samples per benchmark; closed-loop simulation horizons of 100 / 150 / 50 sampling steps for Benchmarks 1/2/3
  - Source: synthetic data generated from mechanistic PDE models — fully reproducible from the released code
  - Public? **Yes — code + data both released** at https://git.uwaterloo.ca/ricardez_group/benchmarking-nn-surrogate-embedding-methods-in-nmpc.git ; **CC BY 4.0** for the paper itself
  - Link: paper DOI: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.compchemeng.2025.109105 ; supplementary material at the same DOI
- **Reported headline metric:**
  - **EFE significantly outperforms ECE methods** on average wall-clock time across all benchmarks: e.g., Benchmark 1 ECE-FS/PINN = 1.180 s avg vs. **EFE/PINN = 0.402 s avg** (~3× speedup); Benchmark 2 ECE-FS/PICNN = 20.047 s avg vs. **EFE/PICNN = 9.586 s avg** (~2× speedup)
  - **However, the mechanistic PDE model often outperforms BOTH NN surrogate strategies** on average wall-clock time — Benchmark 1 mechanistic = 0.120 s avg (10× faster than EFE/PINN); Benchmark 2 mechanistic = 0.467 s avg (20× faster than EFE/PICNN); Benchmark 3 mechanistic = 16.364 s avg (~130× faster than EFE/PICNN). **Counter-intuitive headline finding**: replacing PDEs with NN surrogates is **not always advantageous** for NMPC solved via direct transcription
  - **First-step initialization penalty** for ECE methods is severe: Benchmark 3 ECE-FS first-step = 25,805 s (~7 hours!) vs. EFE first-step = 1,761 s — auxiliary variables introduced by ECE are hard to initialize
  - **State + control trajectory consistency** verified via L2-distance metric — both ECE and EFE produce indistinguishable trajectories when the same NN model is used, confirming embedding strategy doesn't affect solution quality (only solution time)
  - **Shooting methods underperform direct transcription** — shooting/PINN = 4.09 s avg on Benchmark 1 vs. EFE/PINN = 0.402 s; direct transcription is the right paradigm for NN-embedded NMPC
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **Paper's own Gitlab repo** at https://git.uwaterloo.ca/ricardez_group/benchmarking-nn-surrogate-embedding-methods-in-nmpc.git — **direct, perfect** match for replication; access: **easy** (clone + `pip install pyomo torch omlt pynumero` + IPOPT solver). **Best starting point.**
  - **OMLT** (Optimization & Machine Learning Toolkit) — Ceccon et al. 2022 — Python toolkit cited extensively in the paper for ECE methods; supports FNN + CNN embedding into Pyomo. Match: **direct** (the paper's primary tool). Access: **easy** — https://github.com/cog-imperial/OMLT
  - **PDEBench** (Takamoto et al., NeurIPS 2022) — public benchmark suite for PDE-based ML including reaction-diffusion, Navier-Stokes, Burgers' equation. Match: **close** (PDE surrogate benchmarking, different application domain). Access: **easy** — https://github.com/pdebench/PDEBench
  - **DeepXDE** (Lu et al. 2021) — open-source PINN library with extensive examples + benchmarks across forward + inverse PDE problems. Match: **close** (PINN training framework). Access: **easy** — https://github.com/lululxvi/deepxde
  - **PyTorch tutorials on PINNs** — multiple Colab notebooks reproduce Raissi et al. 2019 foundational PINN paper. Match: **close** (PINN-specific). Access: **easy**
  - **Pyomo + IPOPT** — open-source Python algebraic modeling language (cited in Articles 2 + 3 as Pulsipher's primary optimization tool); Pyomo Cookbook + Bynum et al. 2021 *Pyomo book* are reference resources. Match: **direct** (paper's optimization stack). Access: **easy** — `pip install pyomo` + IPOPT free academic license
  - **Antonelo et al. 2022 PINN-NMPC code** (cited as foundational PINN-NMPC paper) — released code on GitHub. Match: **close** (precedessor work — paper extends Antonelo's ODE methodology to PDEs). Access: **easy**
  - **Synthetic PFR-data generation via Scipy `solve_ivp`** — Benchmark 1 (1D isothermal PFR) is a textbook ChemE problem solvable in ~50 lines of Python without the paper's code. Match: **direct, after construction**. Access: **easy** (any ChemE textbook + Scipy)

### Article 1: SLA 3D Printed Lattice Electrodes for RFBs (Mass Transfer Investigation)

- **Full citation:** **van der Heijden, M.**, Kroese, M., Borneman, Z., & Forner-Cuenca, A. (2023). Investigating mass transfer relationships in stereolithography 3D printed electrodes for redox flow batteries. *Advanced Materials Technologies*, 8(18), 2300611. DOI: 10.1002/admt.202300611. **CC BY** (open access).
- **Authorship caveat:** Maxime is **sole first author** (Kroese is methodology + validation, likely an MSc/internship student under Maxime; Borneman is methodology + supervision from a separate TU Eindhoven group on Membrane Materials and Processes; Forner-Cuenca is corresponding/last-author PI). Authored during her PhD at TU Eindhoven, **NOT from her current independent group at UWaterloo**. **Strongest possible PhD-stage authorship class** (sole first authorship under a single corresponding-author PI), but **NOT** a Maxime-last-and-corresponding paper. **To assess Maxime's own UWaterloo-faculty research direction, future articles from her independent group are needed.**
- **Problem in plain English:** Conventional flow-battery electrodes are repurposed fuel-cell carbon-fiber mats (papers, cloths, felts) that were never designed for liquid-phase electrochemistry — they have limited morphological diversity and no deterministic control over pore size or arrangement. SLA 3D printing followed by carbonization can manufacture deterministic carbon lattice electrodes with tunable pillar shape, pore size, and orientation. The paper asks: which design knobs (printing direction, pillar geometry, flow field type) actually move the needle on mass transfer + pressure drop in flow cells, and how do these printed structures compare to commercial paper + cloth electrodes?
- **ML task type:** **The paper has NO ML.** It is fully experimental + analytical (3D-printing + thermal-treatment + multi-modal characterization + flow-cell electrochemistry). **Reframing required to ship an ML portfolio project.** The natural ML reframe is **tabular regression of (lattice geometry parameters → mass transfer coefficient + permeability)** on a *synthetic* dataset generated via OpenPNM (Gostick lab, open source) simulations of ordered lattice electrodes parameterized to match the paper's design space. The ML target is the Sherwood-Peclet correlation `Sh = C₃·Pe^C₄` parameters (C₃, C₄) + Darcy-Forchheimer permeability κ as a function of design variables.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: **tabular design vector** for the ML reframe — printing direction ∈ {horizontal, vertical, diagonal} (one-hot or 0°/45°/90° encoding), pillar shape ∈ {square, triangle, cylinder, helix} (one-hot), flow field ∈ {flow-through, interdigitated} (one-hot), electrolyte velocity v ∈ [0.5, 20] cm/s (continuous), pillar dimensions (continuous), measured pore size after carbonization (continuous, ~440 μm). Optional 3D modality: voxelized lattice geometry (32³ or 64³ grid) for a CNN angle.
  - Shape/size if stated: ~7 design configurations × ~5 velocities = ~35 (design, performance) tuples in the paper's Table 2 + figures — **TINY by ML standards**. The reframing requires synthesizing additional (geometry → mass-transfer) pairs via OpenPNM simulation.
  - Preprocessing in the paper: SLA print → wash isopropanol → oxidize 250°C 5h (1°C/min ramp) → carbonize 850°C 1h N₂ (5°C/min ramp; 1°C/min for cylindrical to prevent deformation) → SEM + EDS + XPS + micro-CT characterization → flow-cell test in TEMPO/TEMPO⁺PF₆⁻ in acetonitrile electrolyte. For the simulation route: generate cubic lattice networks via OpenPNM's `Cubic` class with parametric pillar geometry; run electrochemical PNM solver (Sadeghi 2019 / van Gorp 2023 framework) for each lattice; extract (Sh, Pe, κ, β) from simulation outputs.
- **Output representation:**
  - **Paper's outputs (measurements):** porosity (%), internal surface area (m²/m³), conductivity (S/m), permeability κ (m²), Forchheimer coefficient β (m⁻¹), polarization current density (mA/cm²) at fixed overpotential, volumetric mass transfer coefficient `a·k_m` (1/s), Sherwood-Peclet correlation parameters (C₃, C₄)
  - Units or label space: continuous regression targets — κ in m², β in m⁻¹, `a·k_m` in s⁻¹, Sh and Pe dimensionless
- **Model architecture (brief):** **No model in the paper.** Reframing options for the ML portfolio: (i) gradient-boosted regression (XGBoost/LightGBM) on tabular geometry features → (κ, a·k_m); (ii) MLP regression (PyTorch) on the same features; (iii) 3D CNN (PyTorch) on voxelized lattice geometries → mass-transfer scalars (heavier; needs larger synthetic dataset).
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: **In-house manufactured + tested 3D-printed lattice electrodes** (4 pillar shapes × 3 printing directions × 2 flow fields → ~7 systems × ~5 velocities = ~35 measurements) + commercial Freudenberg H23 paper + ELAT Cloth (woven, bimodal PSD) electrodes as benchmarks
  - Size: ~35 design-condition rows; n=2-3 replicates per system; supplementary tables S1-S5
  - Source: Custom — printed at TU Eindhoven on a Formlabs Form 3 SLA printer with Formlabs High Temp V2 acrylate-based UV-curing resin; carbonized in custom tubular oven; tested in custom symmetric flow cell with TEMPO/TEMPO⁺PF₆⁻ in acetonitrile (chosen for low surface tension + fast 1-electron kinetics)
  - Public? **No** — paper says "data are available from the corresponding author upon reasonable request." NOT publicly released. NO supplementary CSV. NO GitHub repo (paper has no code to release — fully experimental).
  - Link: not provided in paper
- **Reported headline metric:** Pressure drop of 3D-printed electrodes is **~14× lower than ELAT Cloth and ~65× lower than Freudenberg H23** at 10 cm/s electrolyte velocity (i.e., dramatically lower pumping demand). Internal surface area: ~6,500 m²/m³ (vs. 7.2×10⁴ m²/m³ for commercial paper — **~10× lower**, the trade-off cost of printer-resolution-limited large pores ~440 μm). **Diagonal printing direction** → highest internal surface area + lowest pressure drop (Sh-Pe exponent C₄ = 0.59). **Horizontal direction** → strongest velocity dependence of mass transfer (Sh-Pe exponent C₄ = 0.70). **Helical pillar shape** → 1.4× higher mass-transfer prefactor C₁ than square baseline (attributed to electrolyte mixing induced by the twist). **Interdigitated flow field** → ~2× higher mass-transfer prefactor than flow-through (at the cost of higher pressure drop in the 3D-printed case). Conductivity 85 S/m after 850°C carbonization (paper notes 1100°C+ needed for graphitic-class conductivity — limited by their oven's max temperature).
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **OpenPNM** (https://github.com/PMEAL/OpenPNM) — Gostick lab (UWaterloo, **note: Gostick is at UWaterloo Chemical Engineering — same campus as Maxime now**), MIT-licensed, well-maintained Python package; **simulate ordered lattice electrodes parametrically and generate synthetic (geometry → mass-transfer) labels** — match: **close-by-simulation** — access: **easy** (`pip install openpnm`)
  - **PoreSpy** (https://github.com/PMEAL/porespy) — image-based pore-network extraction; useful if you ingest open micro-CT scans — match: **loose** — access: **easy** (`pip install porespy`)
  - **Published micro-CT volumes of carbon-fiber GDL/RFB electrodes** (Brushett, Gostick, Eller groups) — wrong geometry (fibrous, not ordered lattice) but useful for substitution practice on segmentation / pore-network-extraction angle — match: **analogy** — access: **medium** (paper supplementary materials or Zenodo deposits, on a per-paper basis)
  - **Materials Project / OQMD** — wrong domain (atomistic crystals, not mesoscale electrode geometry); useful only for tooling practice (pymatgen) — match: **analogy** — access: **easy**
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Hard — request-only.** No public release. Can be approximated by **medium difficulty** if you switch to the OpenPNM-simulation route, which is fully open and well-documented.
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **OpenPNM** (Python pore network modeling framework — one major new component; well-documented; Gostick lab is the maintainer; ~1 week of self-study for basics + another week for the electrochemical solver pipeline); **pore network modeling concepts** (throat conductance, Hagen-Poiseuille flow on networks, Butler-Volmer kinetics — new for a chemistry undergrad with intermediate ML skills; fundamental to any RFB-electrode portfolio project); **standard ML stack** (PyTorch / sklearn / XGBoost — already familiar from prior professors' projects). Optional for stretch: 3D CNN on voxelized lattices (PyTorch); CFD simulations (OpenFOAM — heavy, skip unless committed); genetic algorithm wrapper (DEAP or PyMoo — light tooling lift, references the van Gorp/van der Heijden 2023 GA+PNM paper directly).
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** **"Neural surrogate for ordered-lattice electrode mass transfer in OpenPNM."** Generate a synthetic dataset of ~5,000 ordered lattice electrodes (parametric variation in pillar shape × pillar size × pore size × printing direction × velocity, matching the paper's design space) by running OpenPNM's electrochemical PNM solver on each. Train a tabular regressor (XGBoost or MLP) to predict (Sherwood number, permeability, mass-transfer coefficient) from (geometry parameters, velocity). Validate against the 7 experimental systems reported in van der Heijden et al. 2023 Table 2 by digitizing the published values. README headline claim: *"ML surrogate matches OpenPNM physics within ~10% RMSE on held-out lattice geometries, while running ~1000× faster — enabling rapid Pareto-frontier exploration of electrode designs."* **Stretch goal**: combine with a genetic algorithm (cf. van Gorp/**van der Heijden** 2023, *Chem. Eng. J.*) to search the ordered-lattice design space for Pareto-optimal (high-mass-transfer, low-pressure-drop) electrodes — **directly inspired by Maxime's two TU Eindhoven PhD papers as a unified portfolio** (the GA+PNM 2023 paper for the design framework + this experimental paper for the validation target).

**Article 1 verdict:** A **moderately credible** GitHub project is achievable, but **all the ML lift is on you** — the paper itself has no model, no released data, no released code (it's fully experimental). The only viable path is to **simulate ordered lattice electrodes in OpenPNM and learn a surrogate model**, which is methodologically clean but requires substantial setup time (learning OpenPNM, writing the simulation pipeline, validating against the paper's Table 2). **The strongest framing is the OpenPNM-surrogate-plus-genetic-algorithm angle inspired by Maxime's pair of PhD-era papers** — using the GA+PNM 2023 *Chem. Eng. J.* paper for the design framework + this 2023 *Adv. Mater. Technol.* paper for the validation target — which forms a natural two-paper portfolio. A **chemistry-undergrad master's-prospect with applied-ML / energy-storage / electrochemistry interests** would find this rewarding but should expect to spend the majority of project time on dataset construction (OpenPNM simulation pipeline) rather than ML modeling.

### Article 2: Operando Neutron Radiography of Concentration Distributions in RFBs (*Nature Communications*)

- **Full citation:** Jacquemond, R. R., **van der Heijden, M.**, Boz, E. B. (these three contributed equally), Carreón Ruiz, E. R., Greco, K. V., Kowalski, J. A., Muñoz Perales, V., Brushett, F. R., Nijmeijer, K., Boillat, P., & Forner-Cuenca, A. (2024). Quantifying concentration distributions in redox flow batteries with neutron radiography. *Nature Communications*, 15, 7434. DOI: 10.1038/s41467-024-50120-7. **CC BY 4.0** (open access). **Source Data Files released** + **ChemRxiv preprint with data** at https://chemrxiv.org/engage/chemrxiv/article-details/64e77958dd1a73847f6d8c15 + **two video supplementaries** online.
- **Authorship caveat:** Maxime is **co-first author** alongside Rémy Jacquemond (TU Eindhoven + DIFFER) and Emre Burak Boz (TU Eindhoven) — the three contributed equally per the explicit author-contribution statement. Forner-Cuenca is corresponding/last-author PI. **Major international collaboration**: TU Eindhoven Forner-Cuenca lab (Maxime, Boz) + DIFFER Dutch Institute for Fundamental Energy Research (Jacquemond, Nijmeijer) + Paul Scherrer Institute Switzerland (Carreón Ruiz, Boillat — neutron-imaging beamline scientists) + MIT Brushett group (Greco, Kowalski) + UC3M Madrid (Muñoz Perales) + JCESR (US DOE Energy Innovation Hub). **Funded by ERC FAIR-RFB 101042844 + Dutch NWO Veni 17324 + NWO Science XS OCENW.XS4.295 + JCESR/DOE + NSF Graduate Research Fellowship (Greco) + Swiss NSF P2EZP2_172183 (Forner-Cuenca postdoc support).** **Authored during her PhD at TU Eindhoven** — same era as Article 1, NOT yet UWaterloo-faculty work. **Co-first authorship with Jacquemond + Boz** is one tier below sole-first-authorship (Article 1's class) but the *Nature Communications* venue is several tiers above *Adv. Mater. Technol.* — net authorship-signal upgrade vs. Article 1.
- **Problem in plain English:** Standard electrochemical diagnostics (polarization curves, EIS, charge-discharge cycling) treat a redox flow battery as a "black box" — they tell you cell-level current and voltage but cannot tell you *where inside the cell* species are concentrated, where mass-transfer limitations form, or how charge carriers actually move between half-cells. To rationally design better electrodes, separators, and flow fields, the field needs an **operando, non-invasive, spatially-resolved** technique to image dissolved-species concentrations inside an *operating* flow cell. The paper introduces **operando neutron radiography** as that technique, leveraging the fact that hydrogen-rich organic redox molecules (TEMPO/TEMPO+) and boron-containing supporting ions (BF₄⁻) are highly attenuating to thermal/cold neutrons while the deuterated acetonitrile solvent (CD₃CN) and PF₆⁻ supporting ion are nearly transparent — yielding subtractive-imaging contrast between active species and supporting electrolyte.
- **ML task type:** **The paper has NO ML.** It is primary research on a new diagnostic technique (image-based concentration quantification via Lambert-Beer law + 2×2 energy-resolved matrix inversion). **Reframing required to ship an ML portfolio project — but the reframings are abundant and well-aligned because the released data is image data, the most ML-friendly modality.** The natural ML reframes are: **(1) U-Net semantic segmentation** of operando radiograph stacks (electrolyte vs. electrode vs. membrane vs. flow channels); **(2) CNN regression** mapping raw radiograph → concentration field, replacing the Lambert-Beer pixel-wise calculation with a learned decoder; **(3) Physics-informed neural network (PINN)** trained on the released ChemRxiv operando concentration data, constrained by the coupled Nernst-Planck-Poisson-Stokes reactive-transport PDEs — directly bridges Article 2 to Pulsipher's Article 3 PINN methodology.
- **Input representation:**
  - Modality: **2D neutron radiograph stacks** of operando flow cells. NEUTRA (thermal-neutron white beam): single grayscale 2D images per timestep, 6 μm pixel pitch, ~20 μm effective resolution, ~30 s exposure time per acquisition. ICON (cold-neutron time-of-flight): stacks of energy-binned grayscale images per timestep, 55 μm pixel pitch, ~150 μm effective resolution, 120 s per energy frame, 109 ToF frames per 11.36 ms cycle binned to High-Energy (17.0–77.3 meV, mean 28.3 meV) + Low-Energy (5.7–16.0 meV, mean 8.5 meV) frames.
  - Shape/size: NEUTRA images are 2D arrays at 6 μm pitch over a ~15 mm × 1.5 mm reactor area (~2,500 × 250 pixels per cell-area image); ICON images are 512 × 512 pixel² at 55 μm pitch (28 × 28 mm² field of view).
  - Preprocessing in the paper: 11-step image-processing pipeline — dark current correction, white-spot filtering (gamma-ray rejection), Gaussian filtering, open-beam correction, registration (correlation-based geometric transformation), intensity correction (gain + offset), scattered-background correction (via "black body" boron rods placed in front of the sample), referencing (pixel-wise division by reference cell), positioning (rotation), cell mask (region-of-interest selection), concentration calculation via Lambert-Beer law `T = exp(-σᵢ · nᵢ · δ)` with cuvette-calibrated cross-sections σᵢ. For ICON: a 2×2 matrix inversion `[ln T_LE; ln T_HE] = δ_cell · [σ_BF4_LE σ_TEMPO_LE; σ_BF4_HE σ_TEMPO_HE] · [C_BF4; C_TEMPO] · N_A` to deconvolute species concentrations pixel-wise.
- **Output representation:**
  - **Paper's outputs:** 2D pixel-wise concentration maps in mol/L for: (a) cumulative `[TEMPO]+[TEMPO+]` in the KPF6 system at NEUTRA (max ~0.5 M); (b) cumulative `[TEMPO]+[TEMPO+]+[BF4-]` in the BF4 system at NEUTRA (max ~1.0 M, includes BF4 contribution); (c) **deconvoluted `[TEMPO]+[TEMPO+]` AND `[BF4-]` separately** at ICON via energy-resolved 2×2 matrix inversion (each up to 0.5 M).
  - Units / label space: continuous regression target — concentration in mol/L (0 to 0.5 or 0 to 1.0 M depending on system); spatial dimension across reactor (μm-scale).
- **Model architecture (brief):** **No ML model in the paper.** The "model" is the Lambert-Beer law (pixel-wise scalar inversion at NEUTRA) + a 2×2 linear system (energy-resolved inversion at ICON). For the ML reframings: (i) **U-Net** (PyTorch, well-documented) for image segmentation of cell components; (ii) **2D CNN regressor** (PyTorch — standard ResNet/U-Net encoder-decoder backbone) for radiograph → concentration field; (iii) **PINN** (PyTorch + autograd-based PDE residual loss; tools like DeepXDE or NVIDIA Modulus) for Nernst-Planck-Poisson-Stokes reactive-transport modeling.
- **Dataset used in the paper:**
  - Name: **Operando neutron radiography stacks** of TEMPO/TEMPO+ + supporting-salt non-aqueous flow cells across two beamlines × multiple polarization conditions × multiple flow rates
  - Size: hundreds-to-thousands of images per beamline × multiple experimental conditions (6 polarization states × 2 flow rates × 2 supporting salts at NEUTRA + similar matrix at ICON); precise size depends on Source Data Files contents
  - Source: Custom — experiments at Paul Scherrer Institute SINQ facility (NEUTRA + ICON beamlines, proposal numbers 20202189 + 20202333); flow cells assembled at TU Eindhoven Forner-Cuenca lab; electrolytes synthesized in-house from TEMPO + nitrosonium tetrafluoroborate (NOBF4) / hexafluorophosphate (NOPF6); flow cells used Sigracet 39AA paper electrodes (NEUTRA) or AvCarb G100 felt electrode (ICON, 3200 μm thick to compensate for lower spatial resolution); Fumasep FAB-PK-130 anion exchange membrane (130 μm).
  - Public? **PARTIAL — Source Data Files released via Nature Communications + ChemRxiv preprint** at https://chemrxiv.org/engage/chemrxiv/article-details/64e77958dd1a73847f6d8c15 + two video supplementaries online. **MAJOR upgrade vs. Article 1's "data on request" status.** Code is on-request only ("The code used in this study can be provided by the corresponding author upon request") — drag, but the data itself IS publicly accessible.
  - Link: ChemRxiv preprint with data — https://chemrxiv.org/engage/chemrxiv/article-details/64e77958dd1a73847f6d8c15 ; Nature Comm article — DOI 10.1038/s41467-024-50120-7; Source Data Files downloadable from the published article supplementary
- **Reported headline metric:** Lambert-Beer law holds linearly across 0–0.5 M for TEMPO, TEMPO+PF₆⁻, and TEMPO+BF₄⁻ in CD₃CN (cuvette calibration, R² near 1.0). Operando: at +0.6 V WE polarization, a TEMPO+ concentration front accumulates near the membrane (Donnan exclusion); at lower flow rates the front is more pronounced (5.6 mL/min worse than 15.1 mL/min). With BF4⁻ as supporting anion, BF4⁻ migration across the anion-exchange membrane is **directly imaged** — confirms BF4⁻ is the dominant charge carrier. ICON ToF deconvolution successfully separates TEMPO and BF4⁻ concentration profiles via 2×2 matrix inversion with cuvette-calibrated LE+HE cross-sections (TEMPO: σ_LE=0.34 cm², σ_HE=0.28 cm²; BF4⁻: σ_LE=0.37 cm², σ_HE=0.25 cm²). Capacity utilization: 60% discharge at -0.6 V / 21.1 mL/min, 45% recharge at +0.6 V → 15% underutilization due to asymmetric starting BF4⁻ distribution.
- **Public alternatives to replicate or approximate this:**
  - **The paper's OWN released data** (Source Data Files via Nature + ChemRxiv preprint) — match: **direct, perfect** — access: **easy** (download from Nature article supplementary + ChemRxiv link)
  - **PSI ICON / NEUTRA beamline open datasets** (Paul Scherrer Institute neutron-imaging archive — some operando fuel-cell + battery datasets are publicly available) — match: **close-by-domain** — access: **medium** (PSI registration; not all data is open)
  - **ImageNet / COCO / Kaggle medical-imaging segmentation datasets** — wrong domain but useful for U-Net pretraining transfer-learning step — match: **analogy** — access: **easy**
  - **Lithium-ion battery operando neutron-radiography datasets** (Michalak 2015 *Sci. Rep.* gas evolution, Liu 2014 *Angew. Chem.* lithium transport, Lanz 2001 *J. Power Sources*) — wrong electrolyte but methodologically adjacent — match: **analogy** — access: **medium** (per-paper supplementary materials)
- **Data access difficulty (for the paper's own dataset):** **Easy-to-medium — Source Data Files + ChemRxiv preprint are publicly downloadable.** Code is request-only (drag for full reproducibility but not blocking for dataset use). **Substantial improvement vs. Article 1's "data on request" status.**
- **Tooling I would need to learn:** **PyTorch** (already familiar from prior projects); **U-Net architecture** (standard CV — well-documented; many open-source implementations and tutorials); **image preprocessing pipeline** (scikit-image / OpenCV — minor new pieces for neutron-imaging-specific corrections like dark-current + scattered-background subtraction); **HDF5 / TIFF stack handling** (h5py + tifffile — standard scientific Python). **Optional stretch (PINN route): autograd-based PDE residual losses** (DeepXDE or NVIDIA Modulus — one major new framework concept; closely related to Pulsipher Article 3's PINN methodology so cross-survey-synergistic to learn). **Optional stretch (segmentation transfer learning)**: pretrained ImageNet/medical-segmentation U-Net weights as initialization.
- **Portfolio angle — what a good GitHub project based on this would look like:** **Three viable framings, all stronger than Article 1's:**
  1. **"U-Net segmentation of operando neutron radiographs."** Train a U-Net on the released ChemRxiv stacks to segment electrolyte vs. electrode vs. membrane vs. flow channels in the operando radiographs. README headline claim: *"Pixel-accurate semantic segmentation of operando neutron-radiography stacks at 95%+ IoU, replacing the paper's 11-step manual ImageJ pipeline."* **Cleanest beginner-friendly framing.**
  2. **"CNN concentration regression replacing Lambert-Beer."** Train a 2D CNN (encoder-decoder backbone) on (raw radiograph → ground-truth concentration map) pairs from the released data, learning the cuvette-calibrated Lambert-Beer transformation end-to-end. README headline claim: *"Learned concentration decoder matches paper's Lambert-Beer baseline within ~5% RMSE without explicit cross-section calibration."* **Mid-difficulty; demonstrates ML-can-replace-physics framing.**
  3. **"Physics-informed neural network for reactive transport in an operating RFB."** Train a PINN on the released ChemRxiv concentration data, with the loss combining (a) data fitting to operando concentration maps, (b) Nernst-Planck-Poisson-Stokes PDE residuals, (c) boundary/initial conditions from the cell geometry. **Bridges directly to Pulsipher Article 3 PINN methodology** — natural co-supervision setup if Maxime + Pulsipher both at UWaterloo. README headline claim: *"PINN trained on operando neutron-imaging data recovers cell-level current-voltage from PDE physics within ~10% of measured polarization."* **Most ambitious framing; strongest portfolio impact.**

**Article 2 verdict:** A **substantially more credible** GitHub project than Article 1, because the data is publicly released (Source Data Files + ChemRxiv preprint), the paper appears in a top-tier journal (*Nature Communications*), and there are **three clean ML reframings** of progressively increasing ambition (U-Net segmentation → CNN regression → PINN reactive transport). **The strongest portfolio framing for a UWaterloo-bound master's prospect is the PINN angle**, which directly bridges Maxime's released operando concentration data (Article 2) to Pulsipher's PINN methodology (Article 3) — forming a unified "physics-informed ML for redox flow battery diagnostics" portfolio that simultaneously taps both UWaterloo Chemical Engineering professors. **Compute cost is moderate** (free Colab GPU sufficient for U-Net + CNN; PINN is heavier but doable on Colab with patience). **Tooling cost is low for U-Net + CNN paths** (PyTorch + standard CV libraries); **moderate for PINN** (one new framework concept). **Article 2 elevates the entire Maxime entry from "promising but uncertain" to "genuinely strong portfolio anchor with cross-survey synergies."**
